Typeset in Fraunces and Newsreader

This manual is a companion volume to 6S Success: Home Edition. It is a field reference to 114 micro zones across 20 rooms, and it does not re-teach the method. Cross-references in the form "See Chapter 21, the hazard hunt" point to the main book.

The six steps are Sort, Straighten, Shine, Safety, Standardize, and Sustain, always in that order. The method is adapted from the industrial 5S practice developed in Japanese manufacturing, with Safety added as a distinct fourth step. The second step is called Straighten throughout this series.

This manual names types of product, not brands. Any trademarks, service marks, or product names that appear are the property of their respective owners and are used for identification only. Their use does not imply any endorsement, affiliation, or sponsorship in either direction.

Retail figures in the appendices are planning estimates for a type of product, gathered as a range. They are not quotations, they carry no brand, and they will move.

Important

Read this before you begin

Safety notice

This is a guide to organizing and cleaning a home. It is not a safety manual and it is not a substitute for a qualified professional. Every task described here is undertaken at your own risk, and the author and publisher accept no liability for loss, injury, or damage arising from its use, to the fullest extent permitted by law.

Always read and follow the manufacturer's instructions for every product, tool, and appliance you use. Never mix cleaning products. Unplug tools, or remove their batteries, before cleaning them. Keep chemicals, tools, batteries, and medicines away from children and pets. Test any method on a small hidden area first.

This material does not teach structural, electrical, plumbing, gas, roofing, or carpentry work. Where a job needs one of those trades, stop and call a qualified professional.

In an emergency, contact your local emergency number or poison control service. If you smell gas, leave the area, do not operate switches or flames, and call your gas supplier from outside. Nothing here is medical or veterinary advice.

Know your limits, and stop at them. Where a zone card takes you toward structural, electrical, plumbing, gas, roofing, or carpentry work, the instruction is always to stop and call a qualified trade. Follow it. If a job requires a licensed trade where you live, use one.

Height. Several zones ask you to clear a high shelf, a garage rafter, or the top of a wardrobe. Use a stable step stool or step ladder on a level surface. Do not stand on furniture, on a box, or on the top step, and where you can, have another adult in the room.

Anything you route out of the house. The bathroom, laundry, garage, and workshop zones all move things out. Dispose of medicines and hazardous household waste according to your local authority, your pharmacy take-back scheme, or your national regulator. Local rules differ, and they take precedence over anything suggested here.

Products. This manual names types of product, not brands, and the two appendices list those types. It does not test, rate, certify, or recommend any particular make. Where a companion website or resource list carries affiliate links, that relationship is disclosed on the page where the links appear.

Third-party content. Any website address given here was accurate at the time of publication. Neither the author nor the publisher is responsible for content on other people's websites.

The book taught you the six steps. This is the book you carry to the shelf. 114 micro zones across 20 rooms, each one small enough to finish in a single sitting, each one ending in a change you can see from the doorway.

Why zonesSmall enough to finish

Most home projects fail on scope, not on effort. You set out to do the kitchen, you are four hours in with every cupboard on the floor, and dinner still has to happen. The room beat you because a room is not a unit of work.

A micro zone is. One drawer. One shelf. The strip of counter beside the kettle. Small enough that you finish, and finishing is the whole point, because a finished zone is proof, and proof is what carries you to the next one.

Micro zone

The smallest patch of your home that can be finished in one sitting and judged from the doorway.

The methodSix steps, always in this order

The order is not decoration. Each step cuts the work of the next: sorting first means you straighten fewer things, straightening first means you clean around less, and safety lands where it can still change the layout rather than merely comment on it.

First S

Sort

Keep what serves the zone, route out the rest.

Second S

Straighten

A home for everything, chosen by how it is used.

Third S

Shine

Clean to inspect, not just to impress.

Fourth S

Safety

The step that protects everything else.

Fifth S

Standardize

Make the right state obvious at a glance.

Sixth S

Sustain

Design the habit, do not rely on willpower.

How to use this manual

  • Pick one zone. Not a room, not a floor. One zone.
  • Read its card before you touch anything, including "Done looks like".
  • Work the six steps in order, without skipping ahead to the satisfying ones.
  • Stop when the card says you are done. Resist the pull into the next zone on the same day.
  • Set the trigger before you walk away. A zone without a trigger comes undone.

Where to beginStart with the entryway

Run the entryway first. It is small, it is fast, and it is the room your household crosses twice a day, so the payoff shows up before anyone has time to lose faith in the project. Belief is a resource, and early rooms are how you fund it.

After that, go where the friction is loudest. The zone you swear at at seven in the morning is worth more than the zone that merely looks untidy on a Sunday.

One idea to keep

A finished zone is worth more than three started rooms.

Room 01 of 20

Entryway

5 zones

Everything the outside world sends into your house passes through the entryway twice a day, which is why it fails faster than any other room. It fails from unmade decisions rather than dirt: mail with no verdict, keys with no home, a fourth pair of shoes nobody will admit to owning. Start here before any other room, because it is small and because you will walk through the result four times tomorrow.

Where to startDo the Door, Mat, and Immediate Floor first. It is the shortest zone in the room, it gives you somewhere to stand while you work the other four, and the change shows on the hallway floor within a week.
Cleaning noteGrit is this room's real dirt, and it is abrasive. Vacuum both mats every time the vacuum comes out, take the inside one outdoors and beat it monthly, and tip the boot tray rather than wiping it.
The trapThis is the room people try to fix by buying storage. Another rail of hooks, a bench with cubbies, a bigger basket. Every hook you add raises the number of coats the room will hold, and it fills to whatever you give it. Set the caps first, then find out whether you needed the furniture at all.

Landing Zone

30-45 min, most of it in Sort, because the paper is the slow part

The spot where pockets empty on the way in and refill on the way out.

Done looks like

One tray holding keys and sunglasses, one wallet and one phone per adult, a single upright folder with fewer than ten sheets of paper standing in it, and bare surface on both sides of the tray.

  • SortEmpty the tray and the paper stack onto the floor. Keys that open nothing, loyalty cards, dead batteries and last month's flyers leave now. Every piece of mail gets a verdict of act, file, or recycle before anything goes back on the surface.
  • StraightenPut the tray where your hand actually opens on the way in, not where the furniture happens to be. Keys and sunglasses in the tray, one upright folder for act-on paper, and the charger plugged in at the far end away from anything that drips.
  • ShineLift the tray out and wipe under it, then wipe the tray itself. Keys shed grit and coins leave a grey film that tells you exactly how long it has been since anyone did this.
  • SafetyThe charging cable and the umbrella drip line must not share a spot. Check what else is on the outlet feeding the dock, because a lamp, a seasonal heater and a four-port charger on one strip is this room's usual overload.
  • StandardizePhotograph the cleared tray with the folder standing beside it, print it small, and tape it inside the coat cupboard door at the height people glance while hanging a coat. The tray then teaches itself.
  • SustainWhen you sit down after dinner, the folder comes with you and you work it one sheet at a time until it stands loose and upright again.
How to clean it

Lift the tray out and clean what it was hiding, then the tray itself. Keys shed grit and coins leave a grey film that tells you exactly how long it has been since anyone did this. Work from the mirror down to the floor.

Surface or areaMethod
the mirror or framed art above the consoleStart high. Mist the streak-free glass and mirror cleaner onto a dry microfiber cloth rather than straight onto the glass, so it does not run down behind the frame, then wipe in one direction and buff dry with a second cloth. Dust the top edge of the frame with a dry cloth first.
the wall on either side of the console and the light switch plateWipe the wall flanking the console and the switch plate beside the door with a barely damp microfiber cloth, lifting the finger marks that build around the switch and the scuff where shoulders and bags brush past. Go gently on painted plaster and dry it after.
the console top on both sides of the trayClear everything off, dust or vacuum any grit loose, then wipe the whole surface with a cloth dampened with neutral pH multi-surface cleaner. Reach the back edge against the wall where dust packs into the seam.
the tray itself and the surface it was sitting onLift the tray right out. Wipe the ring of grey film off the console where it stood, then wash the tray in and out with the multi-surface cleaner, working a detail brush into the corners where key grit and coin dust collect. Dry it before the keys go back.
the upright folder and the loose papers in itTake the folder out, tap the papers square on the surface to shed dust, and wipe the folder front and spine with a barely damp cloth. Dry paper only goes back into a dry folder.
the charging dock and its cableUnplug it first. Wipe the dock body and the cradle with a dry or barely damp microfiber cloth, never a wet one, and run a dry detail brush through the port and along the cable to lift lint. Coil the cable loosely before it goes back.
the floor around and under the console legsVacuum the grit out from under the console and around each leg with the crevice attachment, then mop or wipe the sealed floor with the floor-material-compatible cleaner. Get right into the corner behind the legs where a work bag usually parks.
What you clean with
Streak-free glass and mirror cleanerNeutral pH multi-surface cleanerFloor-material-compatible cleanerColor-coded microfiber cloth setCompact vacuum with attachmentsPortable cleaning caddySmall detail brush (not in this zone's kit)
Inspect as you clean
  • A brown water ring or a spreading stain on the wall or console top directly under the hooks, the sign that wet sleeves have been dripping in the same line for a while. Flag it.
  • A charging cable with cracked, frayed, or hardened insulation, or a plug that shows a scorch mark or feels warm where it sits in the socket. Flag it.
  • A power strip behind the console that feels warm to the touch or has yellowed or discoloured around a socket. Note it.
  • A console leg or wall bracket that rocks or wobbles when you lean on the top. Flag it.
  • Green or white crust building on the dock's metal contacts, which is corrosion from damp. Note it.
The call that matters here
The paper with no verdict

What you have been avoiding here is not the keys, it is the six sheets of paper you keep shifting from one end of the surface to the other. They stay because each needs a small unpleasant action: a form, a phone call, a payment. The rule is that nothing returns to the surface without a verdict, and there are only three: act, file, recycle. If it is act, it stands upright in the folder with the date written in the corner. When any item in that folder passes fourteen days, it stops being paperwork and becomes a decision. Do it tonight, or accept honestly that you never will and recycle it. Both of those are answers. Leaving it on the surface is not.

Watch for
  • Water and electricityWet umbrellas and rain-soaked sleeves drip onto whatever sits directly below the hooks. If the charging dock or a power strip is under that line, move it out of the drip.
  • Burn or fireA phone dock, a lamp and a plug-in heater chained together behind the console is the overload that starts here. One dock, one outlet, nothing chained.
  • FallA work bag parked on the floor beside the tray is the thing you go over in the dark with your arms full of shopping.
Leave behind

The surface holds the tray and the folder and nothing else, and keys never ride out of the house in a coat pocket that lives on a hanger.

Trigger: As you take your coat off, your hands are already full and already stopped. That is the moment everything in them goes into the tray.

See Chapter 25, the ideal-state photo.

Coat and Outerwear Zone

45-75 min, most of it in Sort, since every coat has to come down and be held

Hold the outerwear you will actually wear this week, at the moment you reach for it.

Done looks like

One coat per person on the rail, one bag, hats and gloves together in a single labeled bin on the shelf above, umbrellas standing in the stand, and at least two hooks left empty.

  • SortTake every coat down. Anything that did not go outdoors once through the last cold season leaves, and so do the single gloves you kept in case the other one turned up. Off-season coats go to a bedroom closet, not to the far end of the same rail.
  • StraightenHooks for what goes on and off daily, hangers only for the wool coat that creases. Set the lowest hook at the height of the shortest person who uses it, and keep the two hooks nearest the door empty for visitors.
  • ShineBrush the shoulders of the hanging coats, wipe the shelf where dust settles on top of the hat bin, and tip out the umbrella stand. There is always water and grit in the bottom of it.
  • SafetyHang your full weight on each hook before you trust it with a soaked winter coat. Hooks driven into plasterboard without an anchor pull out later, with the coat still attached and a lump of wall behind it.
  • StandardizeWrite the contents and the season on the shelf bin, hats and gloves, winter, so whoever reaches up for it in October knows without opening the lid whether they have the right bin.
  • SustainWhen you put a wash on, walk the rail on your way past and return anything that has been left over a chair back or the bannister.
How to clean it

Brush the coats, clear the shelf dust, and tip out the umbrella stand, there is always water and grit in the bottom of it. Work from the shelf above down to the floor.

Surface or areaMethod
the shelf above the rail and its top edgeTake the bin down first. Dust the shelf top with a dry microfiber cloth, then wipe it with a cloth dampened with neutral pH multi-surface cleaner, reaching the back edge and the front lip where a grey line of dust settles unseen above eye level.
the hat and glove binEmpty it, shake it out over the bin, and wipe the inside and the outside with a damp cloth. Let it dry fully before the hats and gloves go back, since wool put away damp is what starts the musty smell.
the rail and the empty hooksSlide the coats along and wipe the rail its full length with a damp microfiber cloth, then wipe each hook and its base plate. Dry the rail so nothing rusts where the metal was wet.
the shoulders of the hanging coatsBrush each coat's shoulders and collar downward with a dry brush or the vacuum's soft upholstery tool to lift the settled dust and hair, starting with the pieces you actually wear this week.
the wall behind the hooksWipe the wall behind and below the hooks with a barely damp cloth to lift the dark scuff where shoulders and bags rub. Go gently on painted plaster and dry it after.
the umbrella standTip the stand out over a drain or outside, pour out the standing water and grit, then scrub the inside with the multi-surface cleaner and a detail brush and rinse. Stand it upside down to dry before the umbrellas go back in.
the floor beneath the coatsVacuum the floor under the hanging line and behind the umbrella stand with the crevice tool, catching the grit that drops off boots and hems, then wipe the sealed floor with the floor-material-compatible cleaner.
What you clean with
Neutral pH multi-surface cleanerFloor-material-compatible cleanerCompact vacuum with attachmentsColor-coded microfiber cloth setPortable cleaning caddySmall detail brush (not in this zone's kit)
Inspect as you clean
  • A hook whose base plate has started to pull away from the wall, a gap opening behind it or the screw head standing proud. Flag it before it lets go with a coat on it.
  • A rail bracket with a screw backing out or a rail that has begun to bow in the middle. Note it.
  • A brown damp bloom or a soft patch on the wall behind the soaked coats, where the plaster is taking on water. Flag it.
  • A musty mildew smell coming off a coat or out of the bin, the sign something went away wet. Note which piece.
  • Rust eating through the base of the umbrella stand, or a crack in it that lets water seep onto the floor. Flag it.
The call that matters here
The coat you feel guilty about

Somewhere on this rail is a good coat that cost real money and goes outside roughly never. It survives every clear-out because getting rid of it feels like admitting to the money. The rule is that coats are counted by weather, not by number. Each person gets one coat per kind of weather your climate genuinely produces: wet, cold, and something in between. A second coat in the same category has to beat the first one on a specific, nameable day. If you cannot name that day, that is the coat that leaves. The expensive one is not exempt, because the money is already spent whether it hangs here or keeps someone else warm. Give it one trial: front hook, most reachable spot, two weeks. If you did not reach for it, you have your answer and you had it before you started.

Watch for
  • Fall, cut, or crushA bin of boots or a heavy holdall stored above head height on the coat shelf lands on whoever tugs the coat underneath it. Weight goes low, light bulky things go high.
  • FallA rail loaded past what its brackets were fixed for sags first and lets go later, usually while somebody is standing directly beneath it.
  • Poison, choke, or strangleLong scarves and bag straps left on the low hooks hang as a loop at a small child's neck height. Coats on the low hooks, straps and scarves up high.
Leave behind

Two empty hooks at all times. If there is no empty hook, one coat is in the wrong room.

Trigger: On the first morning cold enough that you go looking for gloves, that is the seasonal swap. Do it that morning, standing there, rather than noting it for later.

See Chapter 14, visual controls.

Shoe and Boot Zone

30-45 min, most of it in Sort, though the boot tray takes longer to clean than you expect

Hold the footwear that comes off at the door, and keep the grit it carried in off every other floor in the house.

Done looks like

Two pairs per person on the rack, soles down, boots standing in the tray, one pair of slippers each, and a clear stretch of floor a full stride wide between the door and the rack.

  • SortPull every shoe out and pair them up on the floor. Odd shoes, outgrown children's pairs and the trainers with the split sole all go, and so does the tin of polish that has dried solid.
  • StraightenDaily pairs on the rack at a height you can reach standing up, boots in the tray because they will always come in wet, off-season pairs boxed in a closet. Nothing at all on the floor between the door and the rack.
  • ShineTake the boot tray outside and hose it. The salt and grit sitting in it is precisely what scratches your hallway floor. Wipe down the rack shelves too, they hold more dried mud than they look like they do.
  • SafetyWet soles on a hard floor are how people go down in this room. The tray has to catch the drip, and anything that walked in soaked stays on the tray until it is dry rather than being carried through the house.
  • StandardizeGive each person their own named shelf on the rack, the name written at the shelf edge, so a third pair has visibly nowhere to stand and nobody has to count.
  • SustainShoes come off just inside the door and go onto the rack before the coat comes off, while your hands are still free.
How to clean it

Take the boot tray outside and hose it, the salt and grit in it is exactly what scratches your hallway floor. Then work the rack shelves and the floor strip by the door, which hold more dried mud than they look like they do.

Surface or areaMethod
the rack shelves, each oneTake the footwear off, knock the loose dried mud into the bin, then wipe each shelf with a cloth dampened with neutral pH multi-surface cleaner, working front to back. Dry the shelves so damp mud does not cake back on.
the footwear itselfKnock the soles together outside to shed grit and stones, brush caked mud off the welts with a stiff dry brush, and wipe the uppers of the pairs you keep on the rack. Slippers get a shake and a wipe of the sole.
the boot trayCarry it outside and hose it down, then scrub the salt film and grit out of the corners with the stiff brush and the multi-surface cleaner and rinse it clear. Dry it fully before it comes back in, because a wet tray just re-wets the floor.
the wall and skirting behind the rackWipe the skirting board and the lower wall behind the rack with a barely damp cloth to lift the mud spray and scuff. Dry it after.
the floor under the rackPull the rack out or reach under it and vacuum the grit trap that builds in the dark underneath, then wipe the sealed floor with the floor-material-compatible cleaner.
the clear floor strip between the door and the rackVacuum the full stride-wide strip to lift the grit walked in, then mop it with the floor-material-compatible cleaner and let it dry before anyone crosses it wet.
What you clean with
Floor-material-compatible cleanerNeutral pH multi-surface cleanerCompact vacuum with attachmentsColor-coded microfiber cloth setPortable cleaning caddyStiff dry brush (not in this zone's kit)
Inspect as you clean
  • A hairline crack or a split in the boot tray letting water seep onto the floor under it. Flag it, and note any dark stain that has formed underneath.
  • A rack shelf that sags, or a joint that has worked loose so the whole rack rocks. Note it.
  • Rust blooming on the metal frame where salt water keeps pooling. Flag it.
  • A dark damp stain or a soft patch on the skirting behind the tray. Note it.
  • The floor finish worn dull or gone grey and rough under the tray, where repeated salt has eaten the seal. Flag it.
The call that matters here
The worn out pair you keep for the garden

Everyone has a pair with the tread rubbed smooth that survives on the grounds that it is fine for mucky jobs. Count them honestly and there are usually three such pairs and about one muddy job a year. The rule is that the sole decides, not the memory. Turn the shoe over. If the tread has gone smooth or the heel has worn through on the outer edge, that shoe is no longer safe on a wet step or a slick pavement, which means it is not gardening footwear, it is rubbish with sentimental value attached. Keep exactly one dirty-job pair per person and store it where the dirty jobs happen, not at the front door. The pair you would be embarrassed to be seen in is not a spare. It is a decision you already made and have not carried out.

Watch for
  • FallShoes left inside the arc of the door swing are the reason people go down while carrying bags with both hands. That strip of floor stays clear at full stride width, permanently.
  • Poison, choke, or stranglePolish, suede cleaner and waterproofing spray are solvents, and they usually sit in an open basket at exactly toddler height. Move shoe care up onto a shelf or out of the room.
  • Fall, cut, or crushA tall shoe rack loaded heaviest on the top shelves tips forward when a child climbs it to reach their own boots. Heavy pairs on the bottom shelf, and fix the rack to the wall if it is narrow.
Leave behind

Two pairs per person at the door, soles down, everything else lives somewhere that is not the entryway.

Trigger: On the night the bins go out, the boot tray goes out with them and comes back empty.

See Chapter 24, the checklist.

Entry Console or Bench

30-45 min, most of it in Sort, because the drawer holds far more than its size suggests

Somewhere to sit while you lace a boot, and somewhere to set a bag down for thirty seconds.

Done looks like

Three items or fewer on the top, a seat with nothing on it at all, and a drawer where every divided section has one named category and you can see the base of the drawer between things.

  • SortTip the drawer out onto the bench. Dead batteries, pens that do not write, single earbuds, sunscreen from three summers ago and takeaway menus all go now. Anything you would not want while wearing your outdoor shoes belongs in a different room.
  • StraightenDivide the drawer into named sections and hold to them: dog lead and waste bags, sun cream and insect spray, a torch, one pen. Bags and the vacuum go underneath the bench, never on the seat.
  • ShineTake the drawer fully out and tip the sand and crumbs out of its corners. Wipe the seat itself, which collects the same grit as the floor because people sit on it in outdoor clothes.
  • SafetyFix the console to the wall if it is taller than it is deep, because children climb open drawer fronts like a ladder. Move the box cutter and the scissors out of the shallow drawer that opens at toddler head height.
  • StandardizeWrite the three permitted top items on a card and tape it inside the drawer, so the cap survives everyone who did not read this book.
  • SustainWhen you clear the dinner table, carry the plates past the bench and take the top back down to three on the way through.
How to clean it

Take the drawer fully out and tip the sand and crumbs from its corners, then wipe the seat, which collects the same grit as the floor because people sit on it in outdoor clothes. Work top down, back to front.

Surface or areaMethod
the wall and back edge behind the consoleWipe the wall behind the console top and the back edge of the surface with a barely damp microfiber cloth, clearing the dust line where the top meets the wall.
the console topClear it to three items or fewer, dust or vacuum it, then wipe the whole top with a cloth dampened with neutral pH multi-surface cleaner. Dry it off.
the seatVacuum the seat and its seams with the upholstery tool to lift the outdoor grit, then wipe a hard seat with the multi-surface cleaner, or spot-clean a padded one with a barely damp cloth. Let it dry before anyone sits.
the drawer, taken fully outPull the drawer right out of its runners, tip the sand and crumbs from the back corners over the bin, lift out any divider insert, and wipe the base and the sections with a damp cloth. A detail brush clears the corners the cloth misses.
the drawer runners and the empty cavityWith the drawer out, wipe both runner tracks with a barely damp cloth and vacuum the empty cavity, lifting the grit that has dropped down behind the drawer.
the drawer fronts and the legsWipe the drawer fronts and the console legs with a damp cloth, getting the handles and the kick line at the bottom where shoes scuff.
the floor beneath the consoleVacuum under and around the legs with the crevice tool, then wipe or mop the sealed floor with the floor-material-compatible cleaner.
What you clean with
Neutral pH multi-surface cleanerFloor-material-compatible cleanerCompact vacuum with attachmentsColor-coded microfiber cloth setPortable cleaning caddySmall detail brush (not in this zone's kit)
Inspect as you clean
  • A console that rocks or tips toward you when you lean on the top, the sign it is top-heavy and not yet held to the wall. Flag it.
  • A drawer runner screw backing out so the drawer drops or drags when it is pulled open. Note it.
  • A hairline split opening in the seat, or a splinter lifting on a wooden bench. Flag it before someone sits on it.
  • A wobble in a leg joint or a cracked or seized caster. Note it.
  • A dark water ring or swelling on the top where a wet umbrella or bag keeps landing. Flag it.
The call that matters here
What the drawer is for

This drawer becomes the second junk drawer within a month of being emptied. It happens every time, because it is the nearest place to set down something you do not want to carry any further. The decision being avoided is not what to throw away. It is admitting the drawer needs a job at all. The rule: this drawer serves the door and nothing else. One test question per item, would you need this while wearing your shoes, either on the way out or in the first ten seconds back in. Dog lead, yes. Sun cream, yes. Spare car charger, yes. The screwdriver, the birthday candles and the case for a phone you no longer own all fail, and they fail even though they genuinely have nowhere better to live. Find them somewhere better today, in another room, with a name on it. If you do not, they will be back in this drawer by the weekend and so will four of their friends.

Watch for
  • Fall, cut, or crushA narrow console with drawers is a climbing frame to a three year old, and it comes over forwards with the drawers out. Strap it to the wall and keep the heaviest contents in the bottom drawer.
  • Poison, choke, or strangleLoose button batteries, coins and small magnets roll into the back corners of this drawer and it opens at exactly the height a toddler can reach. Batteries go in a lidded tin or out of the room entirely.
Leave behind

Three items on the top, an empty seat, and every drawer section holding one named category.

Trigger: You already open this drawer to put the dog lead back after a walk. While it is open, glance at the top and take it back to three.

See Chapter 23, the written standard.

Door, Mat, and Immediate Floor

15-30 min, most of it in Shine, and honestly the mat is the whole job

Catch grit, water and road salt at the threshold so none of it reaches the rest of the floors.

Done looks like

A coarse mat outside the door and an absorbent one inside, both lying dead flat with no curled corners, nothing whatsoever on the floor inside the door's swing, and a door that latches on the first push.

  • SortNothing is stored on this patch of floor, so the recycling bag, the parcel waiting to go back and the sports kit all move tonight. Give outgoing returns one named spot in another room and carry the rest to where they actually belong.
  • StraightenTwo mats and only two. Coarse outside to scrape, absorbent inside to dry. The inside mat needs to be long enough that both feet land on it while walking, and most are not, which is exactly how the grit gets past.
  • ShineVacuum the inside mat on both faces, then take it outdoors, beat it and wash it. Run a cloth along the bottom edge of the door and along the threshold strip, because that dark line is all the grit the mat never caught.
  • SafetyA curled mat corner and a mat that slides on hard floor are the two ways this threshold puts somebody down. Grip or tape the edges flat, and dry the floor immediately when someone comes in soaked rather than leaving it to evaporate.
  • StandardizeRun a thin line of tape around where the mat should sit, so a mat kicked out of position is obvious to anyone walking in rather than only to you.
  • SustainThe mat is on your route back to the cupboard with the vacuum, so it gets done last, every time, as part of putting the vacuum away.
How to clean it

Vacuum the inside mat on both faces, then take it out, beat it and wash it. Run a cloth along the bottom edge of the door and the threshold strip, because that dark line is all the grit the mat never caught. Work the door down to the floor.

Surface or areaMethod
the door face, top rail down to the kick plateDust the top rail first, then wipe the whole inside face of the door with a cloth dampened with neutral pH multi-surface cleaner, working top to bottom and paying the panels and the kick line at the base extra attention where shoes and bags mark it.
the door frame and the architraveWipe the frame and the architrave with a cloth dampened with the neutral pH multi-surface cleaner, working the top ledge where dust settles and the strike-side edge that hands push against. Clear the top corners where cobwebs gather.
the door handle, letterbox, and any glass panelWipe the handle and the letterbox flap with the multi-surface cleaner. Any glass panel in the door gets the streak-free glass cleaner misted onto a cloth, wiped in one direction, and buffed dry.
the bottom edge of the door and the threshold stripOpen the door and run a damp cloth along the very bottom edge of the door and into the threshold strip, lifting the dark grit line the mat never reaches. Dry the threshold after.
the inside mat, both facesVacuum the top face, flip it and vacuum the underside where grit sifts through, then take it outdoors, beat it against a wall or rail, and wash it if it is washable. Return it only once it is fully dry, or it just holds water against the floor.
the outside coarse matLift it, shake or beat the grit and salt out of it, and hose it if it is the rubber-backed sort. Stand it up to dry before it goes back flat.
the immediate floor inside the door swingWith both mats up, vacuum the whole patch inside the door swing, including where the mats sat, then mop the sealed floor with the floor-material-compatible cleaner and let it dry before anyone steps on it.
What you clean with
Floor-material-compatible cleanerNeutral pH multi-surface cleanerCompact vacuum with attachmentsColor-coded microfiber cloth setPortable cleaning caddyStreak-free glass and mirror cleaner (if the door has a glass panel; not in this zone's kit)
Inspect as you clean
  • A mat with a corner that has curled and stopped lying flat, or backing that has gone brittle and is shedding crumbs, both signs the grip is failing. Flag it.
  • A hinge that has dropped so the door scrapes the frame, or a hinge screw standing loose in its hole. Note it.
  • A swelling, a dark line, or a soft patch along the bottom of the door or the threshold where rain keeps sitting, the start of rot. Flag it.
  • A door that no longer latches on the first push, or a threshold seal squashed flat so daylight and draught come under it. Note it.
  • A water stain spreading on the floor just inside the threshold, where wet feet land before the mat. Flag it.
The call that matters here
When the mat is finished

A mat looks perfectly fine long after it has stopped working, and the decision being dodged is spending money to replace something that is not visibly broken. So test it instead of looking at it. Walk in from outside on a dry gritty day, wipe your feet the way you actually wipe them, then lift the mat and look at both the floor beneath it and the fibres themselves. A working mat holds grit down inside its pile and drops a clear line of dirt when you shake it out. A finished mat has fibres flattened all in one direction, and the grit sits on top of them and walks straight off on the next pair of shoes. If it fails that test, replace it this month rather than next winter. A worn mat is not saving you anything. It is quietly sanding the finish off every floor between this door and the kitchen.

Watch for
  • FallA curled mat edge at a doorway catches the toe of someone stepping in with their eyes on their phone or their arms full. Flat edges, gripped or taped, checked every time you vacuum.
  • Fall, cut, or crushA door with a loose hinge screw or a failing closer swings harder than expected and catches fingers against the frame. Tighten the hinge screws while you are down there with the cloth.
Leave behind

Nothing is stored on the floor inside the door's swing, ever, and both mats lie flat inside their taped outline.

Trigger: Whoever comes home last and locks the door straightens the mat with a foot before turning the key.

See Chapter 21, the hazard hunt.

Room 02 of 20

Kitchen

7 zones

The kitchen is the busiest room in the house, and the one 6S pays back fastest, because you cross it a dozen times a day and feel every extra step. Start at the sink: it is the room's recovery point, and every other zone eventually drains into it, so nothing else can be judged fairly while dirty dishes have nowhere to be. Then work outward to the counter and the hob, and save the refrigerator for a morning when you have real time.

Where to startDo the Sink and Dishwashing Zone in one sitting and hold it empty for a week before you touch another zone. A sink with dishes in it makes every counter decision look impossible; a sink you can see the bottom of makes them obvious.
Cleaning noteGrease first, then germs. Disinfectant cannot reach through a film of cooking oil, so wash food surfaces, the hood, and the splash wall with warm water and dish soap first, then apply the disinfectant and give it the full time its label asks for.
The trapThe counter is where the kitchen loses. It is flat, it sits in the middle of the house, and it accepts anything: mail, keys, school forms, a phone, the bag from the pharmacy. Every one of those belongs somewhere else, and until they get sent there you will keep re-cleaning a surface you never get to use.

Primary Prep Counter

45-75 min, most of it in Sort, deciding what has earned the surface

The stretch of counter where food is actually cut, mixed, and plated.

Done looks like

One clear run of counter at least as wide as your largest cutting board, holding only the board, the knife block or strip, and the salt. The kettle and the toaster stand back against the wall with their cords coiled, and there is no mail, no fruit bowl, and no charging phone anywhere on the surface.

  • SortTake everything off the counter and set it on the dining table, then let only three things back: what you cut on, what you cut with, and what you season with. The vitamins, the second cutting board, the bread bag and the pile of receipts find homes elsewhere or leave.
  • StraightenPut the board where you already stand without thinking, then build outward from it. Knives on a wall strip or in a block on your dominant side, oil and salt behind the board rather than beside it, mixing bowls in the cabinet directly underneath so you can reach one without turning around.
  • ShineLift the toaster and the kettle instead of wiping around them, because crumbs collect under the appliance feet and never move. Run a damp cloth along the seam where the counter meets the backsplash, and shake the toaster crumb tray out over the bin.
  • SafetyKnives belong in a block, on a magnetic strip, or in a bladed sleeve in a drawer, never loose among the utensils and never blade-up in the drying rack. Keep the kettle and toaster cords off the sink side of the counter, where a wet hand meets them.
  • StandardizeMark the clear run so it is not a matter of opinion. A short strip of tape under the counter lip, or the outline of the board itself, tells everyone in the house where the work surface starts and that it stays empty.
  • SustainWhen you start the dishwasher, the board goes back down onto bare counter. If it will not sit flat, something has arrived that does not live here.
How to clean it

This is where the food actually gets cut, so it earns a real wipe, not a wave around the clutter. Lift the appliances instead of cleaning around them, because the crumbs you never see are the ones living under their feet.

Surface or areaMethod
the backsplash and the seam where the counter meets itWork top to bottom. Spray the backsplash with neutral pH multi-surface cleaner and wipe down with a microfiber cloth so anything you loosen falls onto the counter, not behind you. Then lay the damp cloth into the counter-to-backsplash seam and drag it the full length to lift the grease line that collects there.
the knife block or the magnetic stripWipe the block and its slots or the strip with a barely damp neutral pH cloth, then dry it at once so no moisture sits against the steel. If it is a block, tip it upside down over the bin first to shake out the crumbs that fall down the slots.
the toaster, the kettle, and the counter under their feetLift each appliance right off the counter rather than wiping around it. Pull the toaster crumb tray out and shake it over the bin, then wipe the tray dry. Wipe the cool exterior with a damp cloth, keeping water off the plug and element, coil the cord, and wipe the bare stretch of counter where the feet stood before you set it back.
the open run of prep counterClear it completely. For everyday food residue use the neutral pH cleaner on a microfiber cloth. Where oil or a greasy splatter has built up, switch to the food-area degreaser, let it sit for the dwell time on the label, then wipe and follow with a clean damp cloth so no film is left where food will touch.
the cutting board and the front edge and overhang of the counterWash the board on both faces and stand it on edge to dry rather than leaving it flat in a puddle. Then run the cloth along the front lip and the underside overhang of the counter, where hands grip and splashes dry into a band you only see from below.
What you clean with
Neutral pH multi-surface cleanerFood-area degreaserColor-coded microfiber cloth setPortable cleaning caddy
Inspect as you clean
  • A scorch mark or a heat bloom on the counter or wall directly behind the toaster or kettle, telling you heat is pooling where it should not.
  • A cracked, brittle, or fraying cord on the toaster or kettle as you lift the appliance and coil it.
  • The sealant bead along the counter-to-backsplash seam lifting, crumbling, or gone dark, so water can get behind it.
  • A soft, swollen, or lifting patch in the counter surface near that seam, the sign water has already been getting in.
  • A loose rivet or a wobbling handle on the knife you pull, or a hairline split opening along the cutting board.
The call that matters here
The stand mixer question

The hard one is the appliance you genuinely love and rarely use: the mixer, the bread machine, the espresso setup. Count real uses over the past month, not the ones you intended. Anything you reach for less than a couple of times a week comes off the counter and goes to a low cabinet, ideally on a pull-out. Anything you have not switched on in a year leaves the kitchen entirely. And if your honest reason for keeping it out is that lifting it from a cabinet would stop you using it, you have just proved the machine is not part of your cooking, and it is worth more sold than stored.

Watch for
  • Fall, cut, or crushA chef's knife left blade-up in the drying rack, or slid under suds in a full sink, is the most common serious cut in a home kitchen.
  • Burn or fireA toaster pushed under a low cabinet or against a paper towel roll puts heat and flame right where nothing can escape it.
Leave behind

The clear run stays clear: one board, one knife block or strip, one seasoning within reach, and nothing parked on the work surface overnight.

Trigger: When you press start on the dishwasher, the counter clears.

See Chapter 14, visual controls.

Cooking Zone

45-75 min, most of it in Shine, because degreasing the hood and the grates is slow work

The step of space either side of the burners where cooking happens with your hands busy and heat already on.

Done looks like

Oil, salt, and pepper within one reach of the burners. A utensil crock holding no more than six tools, every one of which you used this month. Pot holders on a hook you can find without looking, the spices you reach for weekly at the front of the rack, and a clear landing space beside the hob wide enough to set down a hot pan.

  • SortEmpty the utensil crock into a box and cook for a week, returning to the crock only what your hand actually reaches for. The warped plastic turner, the corn holders, and the third pair of tongs are still in the box at the end of the week, which is your answer.
  • StraightenArrange by the moment of need, not by shape. Oil, salt, and the turner within one reach of the burner, spices in a shallow drawer or tiered rack where you read the lids from above rather than shuffling jars, pans stored on the side you plate from.
  • ShineLift the burner grates off and soak them while you wash the splash wall and the underside of the hood with dish soap. Take the hood filter out and run it through hot soapy water, because a saturated filter stops pulling steam and starts dripping it back down.
  • SafetyTurn every pan handle inward so it cannot catch a sleeve or a small hand reaching up. Keep the tea towel, the paper roll, and the oil bottle off the burner side of the hob, and know where the fire blanket or extinguisher is before a pan is smoking.
  • StandardizePhotograph the hob and one step of surround in its ready state and tape the photo inside the nearest cabinet door, so ready is a picture anyone can match rather than a standard only you hold.
  • SustainWhile the pan heats, the spice you just used goes back to the front of the rack and any utensil on the counter goes back in the crock.
How to clean it

Grease is the whole job here, and it hides upward. Get the burner grates soaking first so they clean themselves while you tackle the wall and the hood, and remember the hood filter, because a saturated one stops pulling steam and starts dripping it back at you.

Surface or areaMethod
the extractor hood, its underside, and the grease filterStart at the top. Lift the metal filter out and run it through hot water with the food-area degreaser, letting it soak while you work, because that soak does what scrubbing cannot. Spray the underside and face of the hood with degreaser, give it the label dwell time, then wipe down with a microfiber cloth and finish with a clean damp pass.
the splash wall behind the hobWork top to bottom and back to front. Spray the splash wall with degreaser, let it sit, then wipe from the top down so the loosened grease runs toward the hob and not onto clean wall. Repeat on any spot where a fine spatter has baked hard.
the burner grates and capsLift the grates and caps off and set them to soak in hot soapy water at the start. Once softened, scrub the baked rings with a detail brush, rinse, and dry them fully before they go back so they do not rust onto the hob.
the hob surface, the burner wells, and the control knobsWith the grates off, degrease the exposed hob and work the burner wells where boil-overs bake into a ring. Pull the knobs off if they lift, wash them, and run a detail brush around the knob stems where sticky grime collars them.
the oven door front, its handle, and the control panelWipe the oven door front and its glass window with a neutral pH cloth, then a clean damp buff so no film clouds the glass you check a roast through. Wipe the handle where hands pull it, and clean the control panel and dials with a barely damp cloth rather than a wet one, so nothing runs in behind the knobs.
the utensil crock, the pot-holder hook, and the front of the spice rackEmpty the utensil crock, wash and dry it, and wipe each tool before it goes back. Wipe the pot-holder hook and the fronts and shoulders of the spice jars with a damp neutral pH cloth, since airborne grease settles into a tacky coat on everything standing near the burners.
the clear landing space beside the hobFinish with the stretch of counter you set hot pans down on. Degrease it, wipe it, and dry it, so the one surface a hot pan lands on is never slick.
What you clean with
Food-area degreaserColor-coded microfiber cloth setNeutral pH multi-surface cleanerSmall detail brush setPortable cleaning caddy
Inspect as you clean
  • A hood filter so grease-soaked it stays soft and drips, and a fan behind it that sounds strained or barely moves air.
  • A burner that lights slow, burns yellow and sooty, or a grate that rocks on its seat.
  • Scorching, discoloring, or bubbling paint on the splash wall or the hood underside where heat is building.
  • A chip, a crack, or a spidered line in the hob surface or its enamel, found once the baked-on ring is cleared.
  • A frayed or heat-stiffened cord on any appliance parked on the burner side of the counter.
The call that matters here
The spice rack you are not honest about

Every cook keeps jars bought for one recipe from one holiday. Open them and smell them, one by one, over the bin. Ground spices fade first, and if a jar smells of dust rather than of itself, it is no longer seasoning anything you cook, and keeping it does not recover what you paid. Then make the harder call underneath: keep spices for the food you cooked in the last year, not the food you imagine cooking. If you want that cuisine back, buy the two spices fresh on the day you cook it. A rack you can read is worth more than a rack that is complete.

Watch for
  • Burn or fireOil left heating unattended, and a tea towel or a paper roll stored within reach of a live burner, are the two ways a kitchen fire actually starts.
  • Fall, cut, or crushA pan handle turned out over the front edge of the hob is at exactly the height a toddler grabs, and it comes down with the contents.
Leave behind

Oil, salt, and your turner within one reach of the burner, no more than six tools in the crock, handles turned in, and a clear landing space beside the hob at all times.

Trigger: Every time you put a pan on to heat.

See Chapter 22, prevent and prepare.

Sink and Dishwashing Zone

30-45 min, most of it under the sink, where the cleaners have been breeding

The washing point for dishes, hands, and produce, and the place the whole kitchen resets from.

Done looks like

An empty sink with a dry basin and a wiped drain flange. One brush and one sponge standing upright where they drain rather than lying in a puddle. The drying rack either empty or holding only what is still wet. Under the sink, a single tray holding dish soap, dishwasher tablets, and one all-purpose cleaner, with the pipes visible around them.

  • SortPull everything out from under the sink and line it up on the floor. Combine the three half-empty bottles of the same spray, bin what has separated or lost its label, and move out anything that is not dish, sink, or plumbing related, because that cabinet gets damp and is the wrong home for spare bulbs, paper goods, or vases.
  • StraightenSoap and brush live in a caddy you can lift with one wet hand, so wiping under them takes no planning. Dishwasher tablets go in a container beside the machine rather than across the room, and the towel gets a bar within reach of somebody already standing at the basin.
  • ShineThe sponge is the dirtiest object in the room: wring it out and stand it on edge after every wash, and retire it the day it smells while dry. Scrub the drain flange, wipe the rubber seal all the way around the dishwasher door, and pull the dishwasher filter out and rinse it, because a filter that has never come out since the machine was fitted is why the glasses come out gritty.
  • SafetyNever store bleach beside an ammonia or toilet cleaner under this sink, and never combine them in the basin; keep them apart, capped, and labels facing out. If small children live here or visit, this is the one kitchen cabinet that genuinely earns a latch.
  • StandardizeWrite the empty-sink standard on a card taped inside the cabinet door: dishes in the machine or washed, basin wiped, brush standing, towel hung. “A standard is the best way you know today, written down.”
  • SustainThe last thing before the kitchen light goes off, the sink gets emptied and the basin gets wiped dry.
How to clean it

This is the point the whole kitchen resets from, so it has to be genuinely clean, not just rinsed. The sponge is the dirtiest object in the room and the dishwasher filter is the reason your glasses come out gritty, so neither gets skipped.

Surface or areaMethod
the tap, its base, and the aeratorWork top to bottom. Wipe the tap and handle with a neutral pH cloth, then push a detail brush around the base collar where limescale and grime ring it. Where the water has slowed, this is where scale is building at the aerator tip, so brush the tip too.
the basin, the drain flange, and the overflowClean the whole basin with neutral pH cleaner, then scrub the drain flange and the underside lip of it with a detail brush where a dark film grips. Run the brush into the overflow slot too, which grows its own slime unseen.
the soap dispenser, the dish-soap bottle, and the surround they stand onLift the pump dispenser and the dish-soap bottle and wipe the tacky ring of dried soap under each one, then wipe the bottles themselves and the strip of surround where they stand. Run a damp cloth along the sealant bead around the sink rim while you are there.
the sponge, the brush, and the drying rackWring the sponge out hard and stand it on edge where it drains rather than leaving it in a puddle, and retire it the day it smells while dry. Stand the brush bristles up. Empty the drying rack and wash its tray and the channel that carries water back to the sink.
the dishwasher door seal and the filterWipe the rubber gasket all the way around the dishwasher door, pulling gently into its folds with a damp cloth where black specks hide. Then twist the filter out of the floor of the machine and rinse it under the tap, clearing the mesh, because a filter that has never come out is why things come out gritty.
the under-sink cabinet, its tray, and the pipesTake the cleaning tray out and wipe the cabinet floor with a neutral pH cloth, working right into the back corners. Wipe down the trap and the visible pipes while you are in there, then dry the base before the tray goes back so nothing sits on a damp board.
What you clean with
Neutral pH multi-surface cleanerColor-coded microfiber cloth setSmall detail brush setPortable cleaning caddy
Inspect as you clean
  • A damp patch, a water stain, or a warped, darkening board on the cabinet floor under the trap, the quiet sign of a slow leak.
  • Soft black mold in the folds of the dishwasher gasket or in the sealant bead running around the rim of the sink.
  • Rust, a green crust, or a bead of moisture forming on a pipe joint or the compression fitting under the sink.
  • A sponge or brush that smells while it is dry, telling you it is spent no matter how it looks.
  • Swelling, blistering, or dark staining along the front edge of the cabinet base where splashes land and soak in.
The call that matters here
Whether soaking is allowed

The real decision in this zone is not what to keep, it is whether you let the basin be a staging area. Soaking is the word a kitchen uses for postponing, and one soaking pan gives every other dish permission to join it. Settle it with a deadline rather than a ban: a pan may soak, but only until the current dishwasher cycle ends or until you next leave the room, whichever comes first. If a dish cannot meet that deadline, it gets washed now or goes in the machine dirty. The rule holds because it names a moment, and moments arrive on their own.

Watch for
  • Water and electricityA kettle, a socket, or a charging phone within splash distance of the basin, handled with wet hands, is the electrical risk this room actually has.
  • Poison, choke, or strangleBleach mixed with an acidic or ammonia cleaner in the basin releases a gas that will drive you out of the room, and both bottles usually live under this sink.
Leave behind

Empty sink, dry basin, one brush and one sponge standing, cleaners on one tray with the plumbing visible.

Trigger: When the coffee brews in the morning, the sink gets checked and cleared.

See Chapter 21, the hazard hunt.

Upper Cabinet Zone

30-45 min, most of it in Sort, and the mug count is the slow part

Light storage at eye level and above for the dishes you reach for every day.

Done looks like

Everything used daily sits between shoulder and eye height, one category per stack, no plate stack more than about six high. Every mug can be lifted without moving another mug. The top shelf holds only what you need less than once a month, and the platter for eight lives up there.

  • SortCount the mugs, then count the people who drink from them. The conference giveaway, the chipped one, and the one nobody ever chooses off the shelf come out first, because chips and crazed glaze are the easy calls and they shorten the argument about the rest.
  • StraightenPut dinner plates and everyday bowls at eye level on the side nearest the dishwasher or the table, glasses beside them, and send the serving platters and the punch bowl to the top shelf, where a step stool is an honest once-a-year cost.
  • ShineTake each shelf completely empty before wiping, because the band of dust at the back is how you find out it has not been cleared in years. Wipe the underside of the cabinet nearest the hob, where a greasy film settles that you only see when you look at it from an angle.
  • SafetyHeavy and breakable do not belong above head height. The stack of casserole dishes or the cast iron lid stored overhead is the one that eventually comes down on somebody, so move that weight low. Use a real step stool, never a chair edge and never an open drawer front.
  • StandardizeLabel the shelf edge rather than the contents: plates, bowls, glasses, mugs. Then anyone emptying the dishwasher can put things away correctly without asking you where they go.
  • SustainEmptying the dishwasher is the check. If a clean plate or mug has nowhere obvious to go, the shelf is over its limit and something on it is due to leave.
How to clean it

Take each shelf completely empty before the cloth touches it, because the band of dust at the back is how you learn how long it has really been. The greasy film on the cabinet underside nearest the hob is the tell that this zone lives downwind of the cooking.

Surface or areaMethod
the tops of the cabinets and the top interior shelfStart above the doors. Vacuum the flat tops of the cabinets with the brush attachment to lift the felt of dust and grease that gathers where no one looks, then wipe with a neutral pH cloth. Empty the top shelf, the once-a-month storage, and wipe it before the platter for eight goes back.
the interior shelves and the dust band at the backEmpty each shelf completely. Wipe front to back with a neutral pH cloth, giving the rear lip and back wall a second pass where the dust band sits. Let the shelf dry before the stacks return.
the underside of the cabinet nearest the hobLook up at it from an angle to find the greasy film, then wipe it with the food-area degreaser and finish with a clean damp pass so nothing tacky is left overhead.
the glass cabinet fronts and any glazed doorsClean glass fronts with the streak-free glass and mirror cleaner sprayed onto the cloth, not the glass, and buff dry so no lamplight streak shows. Do the inside face too if the door is glazed.
the cabinet doors, handles, hinges, and shelf-pin holesWipe the door fronts and the handles, where finger grease darkens the timber over time. Run a detail brush around the hinges and into the shelf-pin holes and seams where dust cakes into corners a cloth skips.
What you clean with
Neutral pH multi-surface cleanerStreak-free glass and mirror cleanerFood-area degreaserSmall detail brush setCompact vacuum with attachmentsColor-coded microfiber cloth setPortable cleaning caddy
Inspect as you clean
  • A shelf that flexes or bows under its stack, or a shelf pin sitting loose in its hole.
  • A hinge screw pulling out of the cabinet side, or a door that no longer sits square when closed.
  • A water stain or a ring on a shelf under a rarely moved item, hinting at a leak from a pipe or roof above.
  • A greasy film on the cabinet underside that keeps coming back fast, a sign the extractor is not clearing the air.
  • A soft, swollen, or lifting patch on the cabinet bottom above the kettle, where steam has been rising into it.
The call that matters here
The second set of dishes

Almost every kitchen holds two sets: the one you eat off and the good one, or the one from before you moved. Pick one everyday set, sized to the people who eat here plus two, and give it the shelf between shoulder and eye height. Then decide the second set honestly. If it has not been on a table in two years and nobody has asked to inherit it, it goes now, whole, to somewhere it will be used. If it is genuinely somebody's grandmother's, sentiment gets to keep the objects but does not get to keep the shelf you reach into daily: box it, label it, and store it outside the kitchen. What you cannot do is keep both sets at eye level and then blame the cabinet for being full.

Watch for
  • Fall, cut, or crushGlassware stacked at or above head height fails onto a hard floor and onto whoever opened the door.
  • FallReaching the top shelf from a chair, a counter edge, or an open drawer is how people fall in kitchens, and it happens while holding something breakable.
Leave behind

Daily dishes between shoulder and eye height, one category per stack, top shelf reserved for what you use less than monthly.

Trigger: Every time the clean dishwasher gets emptied.

See Chapter 14, visual controls.

Lower Cabinet and Cookware Zone

45-75 min, most of it in Sort, once everything is out on the floor

Deep, low storage for the heavy pieces: pots, pans, lids, bakeware, and the big bowls.

Done looks like

Every pan comes out and goes back in one motion. Pans nested no more than three deep or stood on edge in a divider, lids in a rack or on a door rail instead of loose, baking sheets standing on end, and the two pans you cook with nightly at the front of the cabinet nearest the hob. Nothing has to come out to reach anything else.

  • SortEmpty the entire cabinet onto the floor, which is the only way you will ever find the third saucepan and the wok bought for one weekend. Keep what actually cooked something in the past year. Nonstick worn through to the metal in the middle is not a spare, it is finished, and it goes to metal recycling rather than to the back of the shelf.
  • StraightenNest by size but cap any stack at three, with a cloth or a sheet of paper between coated surfaces. Lids stand in a rack or on a rail inside the door, and baking sheets go on edge in a divider so you stop lifting three to reach the fourth.
  • ShineWipe the shelf floor itself, where oil creeps down from pan bases and holds dust into a tacky film that resists a dry cloth. While everything is out, twist every pan handle and tighten any rivet or screw that moves.
  • SafetyKeep the cast iron and the full stockpot on the cabinet floor, never above waist height, and lift them from a squat rather than bending over the open door. If a stack slides when the door is closed hard, it will one day land on a foot, so build the stack so it survives a slam.
  • StandardizeSize the divider slots to your actual pans and write the pan on the front edge of each slot, so the wide skillet slot cannot quietly become the place lids get dropped.
  • SustainWhen the pans are dry, they go back into their named slots. A pan that lives on the hob overnight is telling you its slot is too hard to reach, so fix the slot.
How to clean it

Everything comes out for this one, and the prize is the shelf floor itself, where oil creeping down from pan bases holds dust into a tacky film a dry cloth just slides over. With the cabinet empty, it is the one moment you can see every handle and every hinge at once.

Surface or areaMethod
the shelf floor and the tacky oil film on itEmpty the cabinet fully. Attack the shelf floor with the food-area degreaser or a strong neutral pH pass, let it sit for the dwell time so it can cut the tacky film, then wipe and follow with a clean damp cloth. A dry cloth alone will only skate across it.
the interior walls and back of the cabinetWipe the side walls and the back panel with a neutral pH cloth, working top to bottom so grit falls to the floor you will do last. Reach right into the back corners where a dropped lid or a dust drift hides.
the door interior and any lid railWipe the inside of the door and the lid rack or rail mounted on it, since splashes carry onto the door every time a pan goes in wet. Dry the rail so lids do not sit against a damp edge.
the pans, lids, and bakeware themselvesWhile everything is out, wipe each piece before it returns and stand baking sheets on end to dry. Twist each pan handle as you wipe it, so grease comes off the grip and rivets and a loose one makes itself known under your hand.
any pull-out shelf runners and the toe-kick vent at the baseWipe the runners of a pull-out shelf and clear grit from the track with a detail brush so it glides. Finish by vacuuming the toe-kick and the plinth vent at the very base of the units, where floor dust and crumbs pack in.
What you clean with
Food-area degreaserNeutral pH multi-surface cleanerSmall detail brush setCompact vacuum with attachmentsColor-coded microfiber cloth setPortable cleaning caddy
Inspect as you clean
  • A loose rivet, a wobbling handle, or a cracked helper handle on a pan as you twist each one.
  • The tacky oil film returning to the shelf floor faster than it should, telling you a pan is going away greasy.
  • A damp patch or a water stain on the cabinet floor along the wall that carries the plumbing.
  • Rust spots or orange bloom starting on cast iron, a carbon-steel pan, or a bare baking sheet.
  • A pull-out shelf runner that binds or drops, or a mounting bracket working loose from the cabinet side.
The call that matters here
The third frying pan

Cook a normal week and count the most pans you had going at once. That number plus one is your cap, and everything beyond it goes, including the expensive one you never liked using. The money is already spent, and keeping the pan does not get any of it back; it only costs you the low shelf. Then handle the guest argument separately: if a piece earns its place twice a year for visitors, and a roasting tray you already own could do the same job, keep the tray and release the piece. A cookware set kept for a hypothetical dinner party takes up the easiest shelf in the kitchen every day of the year for two evenings of use.

Watch for
  • Fall, cut, or crushA tall nest of pans or a cast iron pot stored above waist height comes down on hands and feet the moment the stack shifts.
  • Fall, cut, or crushFood processor and blender blades stored loose inside a bowl in a low cabinet are found by fingers reaching into the dark.
Leave behind

Stacks capped at three, lids racked, heaviest pieces on the cabinet floor, and the two nightly pans at the front.

Trigger: When the pans come off the drying rack.

Utensil and Utility Drawers

15-30 min, quick per drawer, but Sort is where the utility drawer will stall you

The shallow drawers holding hand tools: flatware, spatulas, peelers, measuring spoons, foil and wraps.

Done looks like

Each drawer has one job and a name written inside its front edge. Flatware sits in a divided insert with nothing loose beside it. Foil, cling film, and baking paper stand on end so you can read the boxes from above. Every drawer closes flush with one push.

  • SortTip one drawer at a time onto a tea towel and put back only what you have used since the season changed. Two can openers means one leaves. The takeaway chopsticks, the rubber bands, the unlabelled key, and the loose batteries are not cooking tools and do not go back in.
  • StraightenDividers go in before anything else, and you name the drawer before you fill it. Flatware nearest the dishwasher, cooking tools nearest the hob, measuring spoons and the scale beside the baking cupboard, and wraps in the drawer you can pull open while holding a dish in your other hand.
  • ShineLift the insert right out and tip the grit, because the crumbs that jam a drawer collect underneath the divider and in the runner track. Wipe both runners and check that the drawer closes flush again before you refill it.
  • SafetyThe peeler, the mandoline blade, and the loose paring knife are exactly what your fingers meet when you rummage without looking. Give every blade a sheath, a sleeve, or its own shallow tray at the front where you see it before you reach past it.
  • StandardizeWrite the name inside each drawer front: flatware, cooking tools, wraps and bags, utility. The name is the rule, and anything that does not match the name does not go in that drawer.
  • SustainWhen you put the shopping away, the new roll of foil goes in on end, and anything that has migrated into the wrong drawer travels back to its own.
How to clean it

The crumbs that jam a drawer are not in the drawer, they are underneath the divider and down in the runner track. Lift the insert right out and tip the grit, and the drawer closes flush again for the first time in months.

Surface or areaMethod
the divided insert and the tools in itLift the whole insert out and tip the grit into the bin. Wash or wipe the insert compartments with a neutral pH cloth, and wipe each blade, spatula, and peeler before it goes back so nothing goes home sticky.
the drawer base under the insertWith the insert out, wipe the exposed drawer base, working into the corners where flour, seeds, and crumbs settle. If grease has built up on a drawer near the hob, use the food-area degreaser here and finish with a clean damp pass.
both runner tracks and the rollersWipe both runner tracks along their length and clear any grit packed against the rollers with a detail brush, since that grit is what makes a drawer grind and stick. Wipe them dry rather than leaving them damp.
the drawer front, its inner edge, and the handleWipe the inner front edge where the drawer's name is written so the label stays readable, then clean the outer front and the handle where finger grease gathers. Check it closes flush with one push before you refill it.
the foil, wrap, and baking-paper boxesWipe the tops and sides of the foil, cling film, and baking-paper boxes, which pick up a greasy dust in a kitchen drawer, and stand them back on end so you can read them from above.
What you clean with
Neutral pH multi-surface cleanerFood-area degreaserSmall detail brush setColor-coded microfiber cloth setPortable cleaning caddy
Inspect as you clean
  • A runner that binds, drops, or grinds even once cleared, or a runner bracket screw backing out of the cabinet.
  • A drawer front that no longer closes flush or sits racked in its opening, a sign the box is going out of square.
  • Rust or pitting starting on a peeler edge, a paring knife, or a mandoline blade.
  • Crumbs or grease that have gone tacky under the insert, pointing to a spill from the surface above.
  • A cracked or splitting plastic insert leaving a sharp edge where fingers rummage.
The call that matters here
The drawer everyone calls the junk drawer

A kitchen is allowed one drawer of small useful things, but it has to be a named drawer with a category and a divider, not an amnesty. Write its list: tape, string, a torch, batteries, a working pen, the takeaway menus you actually order from. Everything in there must be something you would come to the kitchen looking for. Then take an empty box, and anything you cannot name a use for while you are holding it goes in the box. Date the box, put it on a shelf, and if nothing has been pulled out of it by the end of the season it leaves unopened. The screws in the envelope and the manual for the dead printer are not kitchen items, and they are the reason the drawer no longer opens.

Watch for
  • Fall, cut, or crushAn unsheathed mandoline blade or paring knife lying loose among the spatulas cuts the hand that goes in looking for something else.
  • Poison, choke, or strangleLoose button batteries rolling in a utility drawer at child height are swallowed easily and do serious internal damage fast.
Leave behind

One category per drawer, named inside the front edge, blades sheathed, and every drawer closing with one push.

Trigger: When you put the shopping away.

Refrigerator and Freezer

60-90 min, most of it in Sort and Shine, and the freezer is where it slows right down

Cold storage that has to keep food safe and show you what you already own before you shop.

Done looks like

Raw meat sealed on the lowest shelf. Leftovers in clear containers at eye level, each with a name and a date on the lid. One bin marked for what must be eaten this week, sitting where the door opens straight onto it. The vents at the back wall clear of anything pressed against them, the door holding condiments only, and nothing in the freezer without a label.

  • SortWork one shelf at a time so nothing sits out warming while you decide. Anything frozen past recognition and unlabelled goes, and every opened jar, sauce, and dressing gets judged against the date on its lid rather than by smell, because dressings and sauces go on smelling fine long after they should have gone.
  • StraightenRaw proteins go on the lowest shelf, where a leaking package cannot drip onto anything below it. Dairy goes inside the body of the fridge rather than in the door, where the temperature swings every time it opens. Produce goes in the drawers with the humidity slider actually set, and the eat-first bin goes at the front at eye level.
  • ShinePull the crisper drawers right out and wash them in the sink, because the sticky ring under a drawer is where a forgotten cucumber ended. Vacuum the condenser coils at the back or underneath, and wipe the door gasket in its folds, since a gasket usually fails from grime long before it fails from age.
  • SafetyRaw meat sealed and stored below ready-to-eat food is nearly the whole of cross contamination prevention in one move. Keep the rear vents clear so the compartment actually holds its temperature, and put a cheap thermometer on the middle shelf so you know what the fridge is running at instead of assuming.
  • StandardizeA roll of tape and a marker live on top of the fridge, and nothing goes in without a name and a date on the lid. If it is not named and dated, it is not stored, it is abandoned.
  • SustainBefore you write the shopping list, the eat-first bin gets emptied of anything past, and the list gets written from the gaps that leaves.
How to clean it

Pull the crisper drawers right out and wash them in the sink, because the sticky ring underneath is where a forgotten cucumber ended. The parts that keep food safe, the gasket and the vents and the coils, are the ones a quick wipe always misses.

Surface or areaMethod
the top of the refrigeratorStart above. Vacuum the dust off the top of the unit with the brush attachment, then wipe it with a neutral pH cloth, since the warm air rising off the back turns that dust into a greasy felt.
the exterior doors and the handleWipe the outer doors and the handle with a neutral pH cloth, following the grain if it is brushed steel so no swirl shows, and give the handle a second pass where hands pull it dozens of times a day. Keep any abrasive off a stainless or painted front so you leave no scratch.
the interior shelves and wallsTake out and wipe the glass or wire shelves with a neutral pH cloth, working top to bottom so any drip lands on a surface still to be cleaned. Wipe the interior back and side walls, and dry the glass shelves so they do not streak.
the crisper drawers and the sticky ring beneath themPull the crisper drawers fully out and wash them in the sink with warm soapy water, then dry them. Wipe the runners and the well they sat in, clearing the sticky ring underneath where old produce has wept and dried.
the door bins, the seals, and the gasket foldsEmpty the door bins, wash them, and wipe the door liner. Then run a damp cloth all the way around the door gasket, pulling gently into each fold where black grime and mold gather, because a gasket usually fails from grime long before age.
the rear vents and the freezer interiorWipe the rear vents and the back wall clear of anything packed against them so the compartment can hold its temperature. In the freezer, wipe the interior walls and the door seal, and clear any loose frost so you can see the wall behind it.
the condenser coils at the back or underneathFinish at the coils. Vacuum the condenser coils behind or beneath the unit with the brush attachment, clearing the dust mat that makes the motor run hot and work harder than it needs to.
What you clean with
Neutral pH multi-surface cleanerColor-coded microfiber cloth setCompact vacuum with attachmentsPortable cleaning caddy
Inspect as you clean
  • Soft black mold seated deep in the folds of the door gasket, found only when you pull the cloth into them.
  • Water pooling under a crisper drawer or in the base of the compartment, the sign of a blocked defrost drain.
  • A door gasket that has gone slack, torn, or no longer springs back, so the seal is letting warm air in.
  • Thick frost building on the freezer wall, telling you the door or lid is not sealing.
  • The shelf thermometer reading warmer than it should, or a motor that runs long and hot once you reach the dusty coils.
The call that matters here
The container you keep moving to the front

The genuinely hard call here is not the obviously spoiled food, it is the container of good leftovers you have now shifted forward three times. Ask what would actually have to happen for it to be eaten, and be concrete: which meal, which day, who eats it. If you cannot answer, it is not food you are storing, it is the guilt of having cooked too much. Bin it today, then fix the cause rather than the symptom: cook a smaller batch, or portion leftovers into a lunch container the same evening you cook, before the fridge door closes. A leftover that goes in already assigned to a meal gets eaten. A leftover that goes in undecided is a decision you have only postponed at four degrees.

Watch for
  • Poison, choke, or strangleJuices from a raw meat package stored above ready-to-eat food drip onto what nobody is going to cook again, and the salad is the usual victim.
  • Fall, cut, or crushHeavy glass jars stored at the top of the door or on the top shelf fall onto a tiled floor when the door is swung, and they shatter at foot level.
Leave behind

Raw meat low and sealed, dated containers at eye level, one eat-first bin at the front, and the rear vents clear.

Trigger: On the way to write the shopping list, before the grocery run.

See Chapter 24, the checklist.

Room 03 of 20

Pantry

5 zones

The pantry is where you find out the difference between what you buy and what you actually eat. Almost everything that goes wrong on these shelves is one of two things: food you cannot see, and food you already owned when you bought it again. Start with the dry goods shelves, because that is where the oldest opened bags are hiding, and emptying them gives every other zone somewhere to put things.

Where to startTake one shelf completely off and onto the counter before you decide anything. A shelf sorted in place keeps its own history, and the bag that got pushed behind the cereal two years ago stays behind the cereal.
