  • FallReaching the top shelf from the sofa arm or a dining chair is how people fall in this room, and it happens with an armful of books already in one hand.
Leave behind

Every shelf keeps a hand's width of empty space, and the unit stays strapped to the wall.

Trigger: When you finish a book, it goes back to its group before you start the next one.

See Chapter 22, prevent and prepare.

Side Tables and Lighting

15-30 min, short, with most of it in Safety sorting out the lamp cord and whatever is loose in the little dish

Put a light, a drink and one or two personal things within reach of the chair without turning the table into a nightstand.

Done looks like

One small tray per table with no more than four items on it, a coaster under the glass, a bulb that lights and has been wiped, and the lamp cord clipped down the table leg so no cable crosses the floor to the chair.

  • SortEmpty the little dish and the drawer onto the seat of the chair. The reading glasses with the wrong prescription, the dried-out pens, the foreign coins, the loose tablets, the charger for a phone nobody in the house owns and the coasters that never made it back to the coffee table each get a verdict now.
  • StraightenOne tray per table sets the limit, and it sits on the side the person in that chair reaches from without leaning. The phone charger gets a single clip at the table edge so the cable stays up on the table instead of hanging into the walkway.
  • ShineWipe the bulb and the inside of the shade while the lamp is off and cool, then the table top and the ring marks hiding under the tray. A dusty bulb throws noticeably less light, and light is the whole reason this table has a lamp on it.
  • SafetyWater and electricity meet here more than anywhere else in the room, so the glass goes on the opposite side of the tray from the lamp base and the charger. Loose tablets in an open dish sit at chair height, which is grab height for a toddler, and the lamp cord must never cross the path between the chair and the door.
  • StandardizeFour items on the tray is the rule, and it holds because a fifth thing has nowhere to sit but the bare table top, where you can see it from the doorway.
  • SustainWhen you plug your phone in for the night, whatever does not belong on the side table travels to its own room with you.
How to clean it

A dusty bulb throws noticeably less light, and light is the whole reason this table has a lamp, so start there while it is off and cool. Then work down through the shade, the base, and the ring marks hiding under the tray.

Surface or areaMethod
The bulbWith the lamp switched off and the bulb fully cold, wipe the bulb with a dry cloth. A film of dust on the glass is quietly stealing the light this table exists to give.
The inside and outside of the shadeDust the inside of the shade where it darkens near the bulb, then the outside, brushing a fabric shade gently rather than pressing it out of shape.
The lamp base and switchWipe the base and the switch with a barely damp cloth, keeping moisture well away from the socket, and dry it.
The tabletop and the ring marksLift the tray and clean the whole top, including the ring marks hiding under it. Use neutral pH cleaner buffed dry on wood, or glass cleaner and a dry pass on a glass top.
The tray and coastersWipe the tray and the coaster that sits under the glass, since a sticky coaster is old spill you can feel.
The legs and the clipped cord runWipe the legs and run a cloth along the cord where it is clipped down the leg, then vacuum the floor the table stands over.
What you clean with
Compact vacuum with attachmentsPortable cleaning caddyColor-coded microfiber cloth setNeutral pH multi-surface cleanerStreak-free glass cleaner for a glass top (not in this zone's list)
Inspect as you clean
  • A bulb that flickers, buzzes, or is browned at the tip, or a switch gone loose in its housing
  • The inside of the shade scorched or discoloured close to the bulb
  • The cord sheath cracked or gone brittle where it leaves the lamp base
  • A table leg that rocks, a lifting veneer edge, or a pale heat bloom in the top under the tray
The call that matters here
The medicine beside the chair

The tablets are on this table because taking them is easier when they are in reach of where you sit, and that is a real reason, not laziness. It is also how a bottle of painkillers ends up at knee height in the room where visiting children play. The rule: the bottle lives in a cupboard, and what sits on the side table is a single lidded weekly pill case, closed. You keep the reach that makes you take them, and you lose the open supply. If anyone under five ever visits this house, the case goes in the drawer rather than on the top, and the drawer gets a catch.

Watch for
  • Poison, choke, or strangleLoose tablets in an open dish or an unlidded pill box sit at exactly grab height for a small child standing beside the chair.
  • Water and electricityA glass of water next to the lamp base or a phone charging on the same small table means one knocked drink reaches live contacts.
  • FallA lamp or charger cord running across the floor between the chair and the door is a trip nobody sees, because you walk that line without looking.
Leave behind

Four items on the tray, cord clipped to the leg, and nothing swallowable left uncapped.

Trigger: When you plug your phone in for the night, the side table gets cleared back to the tray.

Floor and Circulation Path

15-30 min, fast, though it stops being fast if the answer turns out to be moving furniture

Let anyone cross this room in the dark, carrying something, without touching anything.

Done looks like

A continuous clear route from the doorway past the sofa to the other exit, wide enough for two people to pass and for the vacuum to run through without being lifted. Rug edges lying flat on a grippy pad, no cord crossing the route, and nothing on the floor but furniture legs, the rug, and baskets pushed back against the wall.

  • SortWalk the route with a laundry basket and put every floor item in it: shoes, toys, yesterday's bag, the pile of post, the box that has sat beside the sofa for a month. Anything belonging to another room gets carried there before you let yourself sit down.
  • StraightenFloor baskets go against a wall or under the side table, never in the route, and each one gets a named category so it does not turn into a bin nobody empties. The route itself is permanently off limits for storage, including "just for tonight".
  • ShineVacuum the route, then move the sofa and chair legs and do underneath, because that is where pet hair and dust build up out of sight. Shake or vacuum the rug on both sides and press the gripper pad to check it is still tacky.
  • SafetyCurled rug corners, the lamp cord crossing the route and toys left in the dark are the three falls this room produces, and they happen at night to whoever is up first. Keep the route to the door clear at its full width in case you ever have to leave the house in a hurry. “Find it, and fix it now.”
  • StandardizeFull width, always, and you check it by walking the route with a full basket in both hands rather than by standing in the doorway looking at it.
  • SustainWhen the vacuum comes out, anything still on the floor when you finish goes into the basket and gets returned before the vacuum is put away.
How to clean it

This is the route you cross in the dark carrying something, so clean the whole run, not just the open middle. Move the furniture legs and do underneath, then lift the rug and check the grip under it.

Surface or areaMethod
The clear route itselfVacuum the whole run from the doorway past the sofa to the far exit, working back to front so you finish toward the door.
Under the sofa and chair legsMove the sofa and chair out and vacuum underneath, because this is where pet hair and dust build up out of sight between cleans.
The rug, both facesVacuum the top of the rug, then turn it over and vacuum or shake the underside, where grit works down and grinds at the fibres from below.
The gripper pad and rug edgesVacuum the gripper pad and press it with your hand to feel whether it is still tacky. Vacuum the rug edges and corners where they meet the floor.
The hard floor along the routeOnce the loose grit is up, damp mop the hard floor with the floor-material-compatible cleaner, following the run of the boards, and let it dry before anyone crosses.
The skirting boards along the routeWipe the skirting down the length of the route with a just-damp cloth. That low ledge collects dust and scuffs and is easy to walk straight past.
The baskets pushed against the wallWipe the outside of the baskets set back against the wall and vacuum the floor they stood on before you slide them back.
What you clean with
Floor-material-compatible cleanerCompact vacuum with attachmentsPortable cleaning caddyColor-coded microfiber cloth setNeutral pH multi-surface cleaner
Inspect as you clean
  • The gripper pad under the rug gone smooth and no longer tacky when you press it
  • A rug backing crumbling, or a thin worn patch opening up along the most-walked line
  • A floorboard that lifts or creaks underfoot, or a tile that sounds hollow when you tap it
  • A pale stain or a soft, springy patch of floor under the rug, or skirting swelling low down near it
The call that matters here
Whether there is simply too much furniture

You keep tidying this floor and it keeps failing, and the reason may not be the clutter. Measure the narrowest gap on your route with a tape. If two adults cannot pass, or you cannot get through carrying a full laundry basket in both hands without turning sideways, the room holds more furniture than floor. The avoided decision is that one piece has to leave, and it is usually the spare armchair nobody chooses or the second side table that came with a set. Choose by use, not by what it cost: whichever piece was sat on or set down on least this month is the one that goes. A room you can walk through beats a room with enough seating for a party you host once a year.

Watch for
  • FallA curled rug corner, a lamp cord crossing the route, or a toy left where you step are the three falls in this room, and all three happen in the dark to the first person up.
  • Fall, cut, or crushA route narrowed below full width means someone carrying a full basket clips a table corner or a furniture leg on the way past.
Leave behind

The route from door to door stays clear at its full width. The floor is for furniture legs and the rug.

Trigger: When the vacuum comes out, everything still on the floor rides the basket back to where it lives.

See Chapter 21, the hazard hunt.

Room 06 of 20

Family Room

6 zones

The family room carries more jobs than any other room in the house. Screens, floor play, board games, blankets, charging, and craft all compete for the same few square meters, until nobody can find a controller, a glue stick, or the second die. Start with the media zone, because its cables run under and behind everything else, and the rest of the room gets easier to think about once they are sorted.

Where to startRun the Primary Media Zone first. Its cables cross the floor into the other zones, and its cabinet is where stray controllers, chargers, and disc cases quietly collect, so clearing it lets the other five zones tell you the truth about themselves.
Cleaning noteControllers, remotes, and the charging strip are the most-handled objects in this room and almost never get cleaned. Wipe them with a barely damp cloth rather than a spray, get into the thumbstick collars and the seams around the buttons, and let everything dry fully before it goes back on the shelf.
The trapThe trap here is storing more than the room actually plays. Let the shelf hold what fits in five or six open bins and move the overflow to labeled bins in a closet, swapping a bin in when a child asks for it by name. Fewer visible choices means longer play, and a pickup that finishes inside one song instead of an argument.

Primary Media Zone

45-75 min, most of it behind the unit, untangling and labeling cables

Hold the television, consoles, and controllers so anyone in the house can start watching or playing without hunting for a remote.

Done looks like

One console per shelf with its own controllers docked beside it, disc cases standing spine out in a single row, every cable labeled at both ends, the power strip off the carpet, and nothing resting on top of a vent slot.

  • SortPull every disc case, spare remote, headset, and loose cable out of the cabinet onto the floor. Anything that pairs with a console or a television you no longer own leaves today, and that usually includes two or three remotes you have been keeping in case.
  • StraightenGive each console its own shelf with the controllers and headset that belong to it, and stand disc cases upright in one row instead of stacking them flat where the bottom four become unreachable.
  • ShineBrush the vent slots on each console and the back panel of the television with a vacuum brush head, wipe the controller grips and thumbstick collars with a barely damp cloth, and finish the screen with a dry microfiber cloth only.
  • SafetyPut your hand on the television and push gently. If it rocks, the anti-tip strap or the mount bolts need attention before you touch anything else in this room. Then move the power strip off the carpet and out of the line people walk between the sofa and the door.
  • StandardizeWhile the cabinet is open, label both ends of every cable, and tape a small card inside the cabinet door listing which controller and which headset belong to which console.
  • SustainWhen the credits roll, controllers go back on their dock and remotes go in the tray before the television goes off.
How to clean it

This is the zone where clean is really about heat and dust. Work from the screen back to the vents, then down to the shelves and the floor behind the unit, because the dust you cannot see is the dust that cooks a console.

Surface or areaMethod
The television screenStart here while your hands are cleanest. Use a dry color-coded microfiber cloth only, worked in straight passes across the glass. Never a spray and never a wet cloth, which streaks the coating and can seep behind the bezel.
The television back panel and rear ventsFit the brush head on the compact vacuum and draw it across the back panel and every vent louvre to lift the dust mat that builds where nobody looks. Finish with a dry cloth along the top edge of the frame.
The console, streaming box, and soundbar vent slots and top facesRun the vacuum brush head over the top and rear vent slots on each console, the streaming box, and the soundbar to clear the packed dust, then wipe the top faces with a barely damp microfiber cloth wrung out well. A soft detail brush gets into the slot grooves the vacuum skims over.
The handheld controllers, the remotes, and the thumbstick collarsTake a barely damp cloth over the controller and remote grips and around the thumbstick collars where hand oil and grime cake, then a dry pass. Do not let moisture run down into a stick housing or a battery door.
The disc cases standing spine outWipe the top edges and spines of the standing row with a dry cloth, since this is where the film settles first in a room where people eat.
The shelf surfaces under each deviceLift each console and box off rather than wiping around it, then clean the shelf with neutral pH multi-surface cleaner and dry it before the device goes back into its own slot.
The cable bundle and the label tagsWipe each cable down its length with a dry cloth, handling the bundle as you go so you feel every run, and clean the label tags at both ends so they stay readable.
The power strip body and the floor behind the unitVacuum the floor and the strip clear of dust, then wipe the strip body with a barely damp cloth kept well away from the sockets, and dry it fully before anything is plugged back.
What you clean with
Color-coded microfiber cloth setCompact vacuum with attachmentsNeutral pH multi-surface cleanerPortable cleaning caddySoft detail brush (not in this zone's list, needed for vent grooves)
Inspect as you clean
  • A cracked, stiffened, or scorched section on any power or video cable as it passes through your hand, especially near the plug end.
  • Dust that packs a vent solid again fast, or a console that runs hot to the touch, both of which say the airflow is being choked.
  • A mount bracket or shelf that shifts or flexes under the television's weight when you lift or lean near it.
  • A sticky drink ring or a water stain on the top of the unit, where a glass has been set over live connections.
  • Browning or a warm patch on the power strip body, or a socket that grips a plug loosely.
The call that matters here
The console with the save files

You are keeping a previous-generation console because someone's save file lives on it, and that is a genuine reason. It is also exactly why it has sat unplugged behind the newer machine for two years. Settle it in this session rather than the next one. Plug it in now, and either move the save data to a card or to the current console, or hand a controller to the child and sit through one real session to find out whether anyone still cares. If it will not power on, or nobody asks for it again after that evening, you are storing a box of plastic guarding a memory it can no longer play. Photograph the save screen if it matters to someone, then route the console, its controllers, and its cables out together in one trip. Splitting them up is how half of it ends up back in the cabinet.

Watch for
  • Fall, cut, or crushA flat screen on a low unit tips forward when a child pulls on the frame or climbs the shelf underneath it. Check the strap or the mount bolts with your hand, not your eye.
  • Burn or fireConsoles and streaming boxes vent through slots on the top and back. A blanket laid over one, a stack of disc cases on top, or a cabinet door kept shut during play traps that heat against the board.
  • FallPower and video cables that cross the open floor between the unit and the sofa catch feet, and this is a room people walk through in the dark.
Leave behind

Each console sits alone on its shelf with its matched controllers and headset, the cable card is taped inside the door, and no vent slot is covered.

Trigger: When the credits roll, before the television goes off.

See Chapter 14, visual controls.

Toy and Play Zone

60-90 min, most of it in Sort, once every bin is tipped out onto the mat

Hold the toys that are in active rotation, at a height and in a form where a child can put them away without an adult.

Done looks like

Five or six open bins with no lids on the lowest shelf, one category each, a photo of the contents taped to the front face where a child looking down sees it, the play mat rolled against the wall, and enough clear floor that two children can build without moving anything first.

  • SortTip every bin out onto the mat and make four piles: played with this month, broken, missing its other half, and outgrown. Anything with a cracked seam, a corroded battery compartment, or a missing wheel gets repaired today or leaves today, because it will not get repaired later.
  • StraightenOne category per open bin, no lids, all on the lowest shelf: blocks, vehicles, dolls and figures, pretend food, art toys. A lid is one more step than a four-year-old will take at the end of a play session.
  • ShineWash hard plastic toys in mild soapy water and dry them fully, because a damp block goes back into a closed bin and stays damp. Fabric dolls and soft blocks go through the machine in a wash bag, and the play mat gets lifted so you can vacuum the crumbs underneath it.
  • SafetyFor any child under three in the house, hold each small part against the middle of a toilet paper tube. If it passes through, that toy belongs in the older child's room, not in a bin at floor height in the shared room. Keep the tallest bin below the child's shoulder so nothing gets pulled down onto a face.
  • StandardizePhoto labels on the bin fronts, and one printed picture of the finished shelf taped at child eye height on the wall beside it. Match the picture is an instruction a five-year-old can follow without an adult refereeing.
  • SustainWhen the bedtime story is announced, the floor gets cleared first, and the story starts when the mat is empty.
How to clean it

Everything here goes in a small mouth or under a bare knee, so clean means genuinely clean and genuinely dry. Work from the top of the shelf down to the mat, and lift the mat, because that is where the crumbs hide.

Surface or areaMethod
The top of the shelf unit and its top edgesDust the top surface and edges with a dry microfiber cloth first, working left to right so nothing drops onto shelves you have already done.
The bin front faces and the photo labelsWipe the front face of each open bin and its taped photo with a barely damp cloth, then dry, so a child looking down still reads the picture.
The shelf boards the bins sit onLift each bin out and wipe the board beneath it with neutral pH cleaner, then dry it before the bin returns, since spills and crumbs collect under the bins where you never look.
The open bin interiorsEmpty each bin, wash the hard plastic inside and out in mild soapy water, rinse, and dry it completely before any toy returns, because a damp bin keeps a damp block damp.
The hard plastic toys and blocksWash in mild soapy water, rinse, and stand them to air dry fully before they go back into a closed bin.
The fabric dolls and soft blocksZip them into a mesh wash bag and run them through the machine, then dry them fully before they return to the shelf.
The play mat, both facesVacuum the top face, lift the mat rolled against the wall right off the floor, vacuum its underside and the floor beneath, then wipe a wipe-clean mat with neutral pH cleaner and let it dry before rolling it back.
The floor around and under the shelfVacuum the open floor and into the corners and the shelf's foot line, where small parts and crumbs collect out of sight.
What you clean with
Color-coded microfiber cloth setCompact vacuum with attachmentsNeutral pH multi-surface cleanerPortable cleaning caddyMesh wash bag (not in this zone's list, needed for soft toys in the machine)
Inspect as you clean
  • A cracked hard plastic edge sharp enough to catch a knee, and any toy piece that has split or snapped.
  • A small part or a wheel snapped off a vehicle found loose on the floor, which is choke sized on a floor a crawler shares.
  • A soft, stained, or musty base inside a bin where a wet toy went back damp, an early sign of mildew.
  • A shelf that rocks, or a bracket you feel move, when you lift the mat or a bin past it.
  • A photo label peeling or faded off a bin front so the category no longer reads.
The call that matters here
The gift nobody plays with

There is a large, loud, expensive thing in this room that a relative gave, and no child has chosen it unprompted in months. You keep it because of the giver, and every time you sort this zone you move it two feet and put it back. The rule that settles this: the shelf belongs to the child, not to the giver. Run one honest week, touch nothing, and watch whether any child reaches for it without being pointed at it. If none does, it goes. If you are genuinely worried about being asked, take one photograph of the child playing with it, send that photo to the person who gave it, then donate it while the good will is intact. Keeping an unloved toy on the lowest shelf does not honour anybody. It costs a bin that a favourite toy currently does not have.

Watch for
  • Poison, choke, or strangleMarbles, small figure accessories, and the wheels that snap off toy vehicles land on a floor a crawling child also uses. Anything that fits through a toilet paper tube does not belong in a floor-height bin here.
  • Fall, cut, or crushCracked hard plastic makes edges sharp enough to cut a knee, and a low shelf loaded heavy at the top will come over when a toddler pulls themselves up on it.
Leave behind

Five or six open bins on the lowest shelf, one category each, picture labels on the front face, nothing above shoulder height, and the overflow toys waiting in labeled bins in a closet rather than on this shelf.

Trigger: When the bedtime story is announced.

See Chapter 25, the ideal-state photo.

Board Game and Puzzle Zone

30-45 min, most of it in Sort, counting pieces box by box

Store games and puzzles so that anything you pull off the shelf is complete and playable tonight.

Done looks like

Boxes lying flat in stacks no more than five high, each with a strip of tape on the short edge naming the game so you can read it from the doorway, every loose piece bagged inside its own lid, and not one box on the shelf that failed its count.

  • SortOpen every box and count the pieces against the list printed on the rules sheet. Cards, tokens, and dice go into their own bags inside the box. A game you cannot finish counting is a game you cannot finish playing, and it leaves the shelf until it is settled.
  • StraightenGames lie flat and stack by height, heaviest at the bottom, tallest boxes on the lower shelf. Puzzles get their own short stack, because they are the ones that come down most often and go back least carefully.
  • ShineWipe the box lids and edges, which pick up a surprising film in a room where people eat. Check for soft or bowed corners on the bottom boxes, and shorten any stack that is crushing the box beneath it.
  • SafetyDice, tokens, and the small metal pieces from older games are exactly toddler-mouth sized, and this floor is shared with the play mat. Keep game boxes above the reach of the youngest child in the house, and never park a heavy stack on the highest shelf where it comes down on a face.
  • StandardizeTape on the short edge with the name, plus a date written on the lid the last time the box passed a count. Anyone can see at a glance which boxes are verified and which are due.
  • SustainOn game night, the count happens as the box goes back, not as it comes out, so the next person is never the one who discovers the loss.
How to clean it

The boxes pick up a greasy film in a room where people eat, and the box at the bottom of a stack quietly gives way. Clean the lids, then check the corners that carry the weight.

Surface or areaMethod
The top of the shelf and its top edgesDust the top board and edges with a dry microfiber cloth before you touch the stacks below, so grit falls where you have not cleaned yet.
The box lids and top facesWipe each lid and top face with a barely damp cloth to lift the film, then dry it straight away so the cardboard does not soften.
The box short edges and the tape name stripsWipe the taped short edge that faces the doorway with a dry cloth so the game name stays readable from across the room.
The bottom box of each stackLift the stack off, wipe the bottom box, and look over its corners before it goes back under the weight.
The shelf boards under the stacksWith the stacks lifted, wipe the shelf board with neutral pH cleaner and dry it before the boxes return, stacked no more than five high.
The shelf front edgesWipe the front lip of each shelf where hands grip to slide a box out, then dry it.
The floor beneath the shelf, shared with the play matVacuum the floor along the shelf foot to pick up dice, tokens, and puzzle pieces that have escaped an open box.
What you clean with
Compact vacuum with attachmentsColor-coded microfiber cloth setNeutral pH multi-surface cleanerPortable cleaning caddy
Inspect as you clean
  • Soft, bowed, or crushed corners on the box at the bottom of a stack that is carrying too much weight.
  • A lid film that keeps coming back fast, a sign food is being handled right over the open shelf.
  • A stray die, pawn, or small metal piece found on the shared floor, which is choke sized where the play mat is.
  • A shelf board that sags in the middle under a five-high stack.
  • A damp patch or water stain on the back edge of a shelf against an outside wall.
The call that matters here
The game with one piece missing

This is the decision the shelf has been avoiding for years, and the answer is not the same for every box. Split missing pieces into two kinds. Substitutable: a lost meeple, a token, a die, a bill of play money. Those you can replace with a coin, a button, a printed sheet, or a request to the publisher. Structural: a lost rules booklet, a board section, a card from a deck that must be complete, or a single puzzle piece. Those cannot be substituted, and the game is over no matter how good the box looks on the shelf. Here is the mechanism that makes it stick. Write the missing item and today's date on a sticky note and stick it to the lid. Any box still wearing that note when you next reset this shelf leaves the house. That turns a vague guilt you have carried for years into a deadline you can either meet or honestly fail.

Watch for
  • Fall, cut, or crushA stack of five or six game boxes pulled from a high shelf comes down as one weight, usually onto whoever reached for the bottom one.
  • Poison, choke, or strangleDice, pawns, and the small parts that scatter out of an open box during play are exactly the size a young child puts in their mouth, and this floor is shared with the play zone.
Leave behind

Every box on the shelf has passed a piece count with the date on its lid, loose pieces are bagged inside their own box, and the name is readable on the short edge from across the room.

Trigger: On game night, counted as it goes back in the box.

Blanket and Comfort Zone

15-30 min, most of it in Straighten, folding everything to one size

Hold the throws and floor cushions people actually reach for while watching or reading, in a container that tells the truth about how many there are.

Done looks like

One basket holding four throws folded to the same rectangle so the fold edges line up, two floor cushions stacked beside it, and nothing draped over the sofa back, the radiator, or the arm of a chair.

  • SortHold every throw up to the window light. Pilled through, thinned along the fold lines, or holding a smell that survives washing: those become car blankets, pet bedding, or rags, and they leave this room today.
  • StraightenOne basket, and the rim is the limit. Fold every throw to the same rectangle so the stack squares up and anyone can see at a glance whether one has gone missing or one too many has crept in.
  • ShineWash everything in the basket, including the throws that look clean, because this is where pet hair and popcorn oil concentrate. Vacuum the basket's inside corners before the clean throws go back.
  • SafetyLook at where blankets end up when nobody is folding them. A throw laid over a radiator, across a floor heater, or hung on a lamp shade is the quiet fire risk in this room, and the basket rule exists partly to keep them off those surfaces.
  • StandardizeThe count is one throw per regular seat plus one spare. Write that number on a small label inside the basket rim, so whoever is folding can see the rule without being told it.
  • SustainWhen the last lamp goes off for the night, whatever is on the sofa gets folded and dropped in the basket on the way past.
How to clean it

This basket is where pet hair and popcorn oil concentrate, so everything in it gets washed, even the throw that looks clean. Then check the warm surfaces a stray blanket drifts onto.

Surface or areaMethod
The basket exterior and rimWipe the outside and the rim of the basket with a barely damp cloth, or vacuum a woven basket with the brush head, and dry it.
The throws in the basketWash every throw in the basket, including the ones that look clean, then dry them fully before they are folded back to the same rectangle.
The floor cushionsVacuum both faces and the seams with the upholstery tool, wash removable covers, and spot clean a fixed cover, letting it dry before it is stacked back.
The basket interior cornersVacuum the inside corners and the base of the empty basket, where hair and crumbs settle, before the clean throws go back in.
The radiator top, heater grille, lamp shade, and sofa back a stray throw drifts ontoWipe the radiator top with a damp cloth and dry it, vacuum the heater grille with the brush head and never a wet cloth, dust the lamp shade with a dry cloth, and wipe or vacuum the sofa back to suit its fabric, checking each spot where a blanket tends to end up draped.
The floor around and under the basketVacuum the floor where the basket stands and the strip beside the sofa, then clean a sealed hard floor with the floor-material-compatible cleaner and dry it.
What you clean with
Compact vacuum with attachmentsColor-coded microfiber cloth setNeutral pH multi-surface cleanerFloor-material-compatible cleanerPortable cleaning caddy
Inspect as you clean
  • A scorched, melted, or stiffened patch on a throw that has been sitting on a radiator or heater.
  • A warm or discolored patch on a lamp shade where fabric has been resting against the bulb.
  • A musty smell in a throw that went into the basket damp, the start of mildew in the fibres.
  • Pet hair and an oily film building in the basket base that never quite comes out.
  • A cracked, splintered, or loosening rim on a woven basket that could snag a blanket or a hand.
The call that matters here
The ugly favourite

You own a pilled grey fleece that everyone in the house fights over, and a handsome wool throw that has been folded on the arm of the chair since the day it was bought. The instinct is to keep the wool one because it looks like the room you want, and quietly retire the fleece because it looks tired. Do the opposite, and be clear about why: this basket stores comfort, not decoration. The test is reaches, not looks. Watch a week and count which throws come off the pile. The tired fleece stays, because it is doing the job the basket exists for. The wool one goes to the guest bed, where being beautiful and unused is actually the job, or it goes out. The same rule handles the blanket a relative knitted. If someone genuinely wraps up in it, it stays in the basket. If it is an heirloom nobody unfolds, it does not belong in a family room basket at all. Fold it into the linen closet, or give it to the person in the family who will actually use it.

Watch for
  • Burn or fireA fleece throw laid over a radiator, a portable heater, or a lamp shade to dry or to be out of the way. Synthetic fabric against a hot surface is a slow fire that starts once the room is empty.
  • FallA throw that slides off the sofa arm onto the floor beside it lands exactly where the next person puts their foot when they stand up in the dark.
Leave behind

One basket, one throw per regular seat plus one spare, every throw folded to the same rectangle, and no blanket left over a radiator or a heater.

Trigger: When the last lamp goes off for the night.

Charging and Device Zone

30-45 min, most of it in Sort, matching loose cables to devices that still exist

Charge the household's phones, tablets, controllers, and headphones in one place, on a surface that can take the heat.

Done looks like

One power strip on a hard surface, four or five labeled cables each ending at a named slot, the empty slots visible during the day, and no charger plugged into any other socket in the room.

  • SortLay every cable and plug out in a line and match each one to a device that is in this house and currently working. What is left unmatched is not spare, it is dead weight, and it goes to an electronics recycling point rather than back into the drawer.
  • StraightenOne strip, one slot per device, the owner's name on the slot. Shorten each cable to the length that just reaches its slot, then clip the excess so the ends stay up on the surface instead of hanging toward the floor.
  • ShineWipe each cable end and check the last few centimetres near the plug, where the sheath cracks first. Blow or brush the lint out of the charging ports, because packed lint is why a plug stops seating properly long before the cable is actually broken.
  • SafetyNothing charges on a cushion, a blanket, or a stack of paper. Lithium batteries need air, and a battery pack that is swollen, bulging at the seam, or too hot to hold is a stop now situation, not a weekend job. Put your hand on every device on the station once during this pass.
  • StandardizeName labels on the slots, not just on the cables, so an empty slot reads as a missing device rather than as free space. Keep the strip itself in view instead of pushed behind furniture, where a scorched socket or a chewed cable would go unnoticed.
  • SustainWhen the dishwasher is started for the night, the devices come to the station and stay there until morning.
How to clean it

Lint and a stressed cable end are what fail here long before a device does. Clean each cable end, clear the ports, then wipe the surface and the strip.

Surface or areaMethod
The side-table top surfaceLift the devices and the strip off, wipe the top with neutral pH cleaner, working at any sticky drink ring, and dry it before anything returns.
The power strip body and switchBrush or vacuum the dust off the strip, then wipe the body and around the switch with a barely damp cloth kept away from the sockets, and dry it.
The cable runs and cable endsWipe each cable down its length with a dry cloth, then clean the last few centimetres near the plug carefully, feeling the sheath as you go.
The charging ports on the devicesBlow or brush the packed lint out of each charging port, because lint is why a plug stops seating long before the cable is actually worn out. A soft detail brush or a puff of air does it without pushing lint deeper, and no liquid goes near a port.
The devices themselves: phones, tablets, controllers, headphonesWipe each device with a barely damp cloth wrung out well, keeping moisture off the port and seams, then dry it and put a hand on each one as you go to feel for heat.
The named slots and the empty dock positionsWipe each labeled slot and the empty positions with a dry cloth so the labels stay readable and the station stays honest about what is charging.
The floor below and the cable dropVacuum the floor under the table and along any cable that drops to it, clearing dust and keeping the drop clear.
What you clean with
Color-coded microfiber cloth setCompact vacuum with attachmentsNeutral pH multi-surface cleanerPortable cleaning caddySoft detail brush or compressed air (not in this zone's list, needed to clear charging ports)
Inspect as you clean
  • A cracked or heat-stiffened cable sheath in the last few centimetres before the plug.
  • Lint packed so tightly into a charging port that a plug will no longer seat fully.
  • A power strip body that feels warm, or browning and scorch marks around one socket.
  • A cable end that wobbles loose in its port, or a plug that no longer grips.
  • A drink ring or moisture on the table right beside the strip.
The call that matters here
The old phone with the photos on it

The cable drawer is the easy half of this zone, and you already know the answer: match every cable to a live device, keep one spare of each connector type in a labeled bag, recycle the rest. The hard part is the drawer's other resident, the retired phone. You have kept it for two reasons that quietly contradict each other, as a backup in case something breaks, and as an archive because there are photos on it you have never transferred. It cannot serve both, and right now it is serving neither. Settle it here. A backup phone is one you have charged and switched on within the last year, and if you have not, it will be flat and out of date on the day you need it. An archive is one you transfer today, in this session, while the device is in your hand and the charger is right there. Do the transfer now, or say out loud that you never will, and then recycle the handset with the battery still in it at a proper collection point rather than leaving it in a drawer to swell.

Watch for
  • Burn or fireA tablet or battery pack charging on a cushion, under a blanket, or inside a closed bin. Any pack that is swollen, hot to hold, or bulging at the seam comes off the strip immediately and does not go back on.
  • Poison, choke, or strangleA charging cable long enough to hang from the side table down to the floor makes a loop at exactly a toddler's neck height. Shorten it or clip it flat to the surface.
  • Water and electricityDrinks land on the same side table as the charging strip. Give the station a surface where nobody sets down a glass, or move the glasses somewhere else.
Leave behind

One strip on a hard surface, one named slot per device, one labeled bag of spare connectors, no charger plugged in anywhere else in the room, and nothing charging on soft material.

Trigger: When the dishwasher is started for the night.

See Chapter 21, the hazard hunt.

Craft and Activity Zone

45-75 min, most of it in Sort, testing every marker and opening every kit

Hold craft supplies grouped by activity so a project can start in one motion and the surface can be cleared for dinner just as fast.

Done looks like

Four lidded, labeled bins (paper and card, drawing, glue and tape, kits in progress), scissors standing point down in a cup out of the youngest child's reach, one completely clear work surface, and exactly one project bin out on it.

  • SortTest every marker on a scrap of paper, because the cap tells you nothing and the scrap tells you everything. Throw the dried glue sticks, the paint that has separated and will not stir back together, and any kit whose instruction sheet is gone and cannot be found online.
  • StraightenPack the bins by what gets made, not by what the material is, so the drawing bin holds paper, pencils, and the sharpener together rather than sending a child to three shelves. One lidded bin lifts out and carries to the table in one hand, and goes back the same way.
  • ShineScrape dried glue and paint off the work surface with a plastic scraper before you wipe, get into the cracks where glitter sits, and stand the brushes bristle up so they dry without bending.
  • SafetyThe glue gun is the real hazard here. It has no obvious off signal, its tip stays hot long after the last bead, and it gets left plugged in on a surface a child can reach. Give it a fixed cooling spot on a hard tile and unplug it before you clean up, not after. Keep the craft knife, the seed beads, and anything solvent based on a high shelf, not in a floor-level bin.
  • StandardizeOne project out at a time, and the label on the front of each lidded bin names what it makes rather than what it holds. That rule is what stops the work surface from filling.
  • SustainWhen the table is being set for dinner, the open project bin closes and goes back on the shelf, whatever state the project is in.
How to clean it

Dried glue and glitter defeat a wet cloth, so scrape and lift the dry mess first, then wipe. Work from the surface down into the seams and finish on the floor.

Surface or areaMethod
The work surface topScrape dried glue and paint off with a plastic scraper first, then wipe the whole surface with neutral pH cleaner and dry it, so the next project starts on clean wood.
The cracks and seams in the work surfaceVacuum or brush the glitter and dried flecks out of the seams and joins where a cloth just skates over them, using a soft detail brush to lift what is wedged in.
The bin lids and labels: paper and card, drawing, glue and tape, kits in progressWipe each lid and its label with a barely damp cloth, then dry, so the labels stay readable and the lids seal flat.
The bin interiorsTip out and wipe the inside of each bin, since crumbs of dried glue, loose beads, and pen lids collect in the corners.
The brushes and the scissor cupWash the brushes clean and stand them bristle up to dry without bending, and wipe out the cup that holds the scissors point down.
The glue gun cooling spot on its hard tileOnce the gun is unplugged and cold, peel the dried glue strings off the tile and wipe it, so its fixed spot stays clear for next time.
The floor under and around the tableVacuum the floor thoroughly, working the corners, because beads, glitter, and paper scraps travel further here than anywhere else in the room.
What you clean with
Compact vacuum with attachmentsNeutral pH multi-surface cleanerColor-coded microfiber cloth setPortable cleaning caddyPlastic scraper (not in this zone's list, needed to lift dried glue and paint)Soft detail brush (not in this zone's list, needed for glitter in seams)
Inspect as you clean
  • A scorch mark or a melted ring on the surface where the glue gun sits and cools.
  • A glue gun cord with a cracked or heat-stiffened sheath near the tip end.
  • A leaking glue or marker bottle staining the base of a bin, or a solvent smell where a cap has failed.
  • A scissor or tool cup that tips easily, ready to spill points onto the shared floor.
  • Seed beads, googly eyes, or pompoms that have escaped onto the floor a crawling child uses, all choke sized.
The call that matters here
The half-finished project

There is a bag or a box here holding something started with real enthusiasm and abandoned somewhere in the middle, and it has held the good end of the work surface for months. The problem is that clearing it feels like quitting, so it stays and nothing else gets started. Use this test, out loud, project by project. Name the very next physical action and name the day you will do it. Not the goal, the action. Cut the second sleeve. Buy the size four needles. If you cannot produce both halves without stalling, the project is finished, and it finished a while ago without telling you. Break it down. The good materials go back into the paper, drawing, and glue bins where the next project can use them. The partly worked piece goes to somebody who will finish it, or it goes out. And you get back the one thing this zone is actually short of, which is a clear surface for the project that has enthusiasm behind it right now.

Watch for
  • Burn or fireA glue gun left plugged in on the table after everyone walks away. The tip holds enough heat to burn a hand or scorch the surface long after the last bead of glue.
  • Fall, cut, or crushCraft knives and sharp-point scissors stored blade up in a cup, or left flat on a work surface a child reaches across from the other side.
  • Poison, choke, or strangleSeed beads, googly eyes, and pompoms live in the same open room as a crawling child, and craft glue and solvent based markers belong on a high shelf rather than in a floor-level bin.
Leave behind

Four lidded bins labeled by what they make, one project bin out on a clear surface, the glue gun unplugged on its tile, and knives, beads, and solvents above the youngest child's reach.

Trigger: When the table is being set for dinner.

Room 07 of 20

Primary Bedroom

6 zones

This is the only room in the house whose job is to help you stop. Every object in it either supports sleep or competes with it, and the ones that compete arrive quietly: the charger, the chair that collects worn jeans, the box of shoes that did not fit. Start at your own nightstand, because it is small and you will see the difference from the pillow that same night, and save the closet for a morning when you are fresh.

Where to startDo your own nightstand tonight, in one sitting. It is the smallest zone in the room and the one you look at last thing and first thing, so it shows you the change twice before you touch anything bigger.
Cleaning noteCases and sheets get washed and the pillows inside them almost never do. Put the pillows themselves through the machine when the seasons turn, and while the bed is stripped run the upholstery tool over the bare mattress and along the skirting behind the headboard.
The trapThe bedroom absorbs what the rest of the house refuses. Wrapping paper under the bed, the exercise bike with a shirt on it, the box of paperwork nobody filed. None of it belongs to sleep, and it is here only because the door usually stays shut.

Bed and Bedding Zone

30-45 min, most of it in Sort, once you pull everything out from under the bed

The bed and the linens on it have one job, which is to get you through the night without waking up.

Done looks like

Bed made the same way every morning: fitted sheet, flat sheet or duvet, two sleeping pillows per person, one throw folded at the foot. Two complete sheet sets for this bed, one on it and one folded on the linen shelf. Under the bed holds either nothing or two labelled flat bins, and you can walk from either side of the bed to the door without stepping over anything.

  • SortStrip the bed and pull out everything underneath it. Sheets worn thin at the hip, blankets gone pilled and scratchy, and pillows that stay folded in half when you bend them are finished; they leave as rags or textile recycling, not as spares for a guest who never comes.
  • StraightenStore each sheet set folded inside one of its own pillowcases so the flat sheet and the fitted sheet stop travelling separately, and keep this bed's linen on one shelf instead of stacked in with guest doubles and kid singles.
  • ShineWith the mattress bare, vacuum the whole surface and the seam where the protector tucks under, then run the vacuum head down behind the headboard where dust and hair pack against the wall.
  • SafetyNothing goes under the bed that sticks out into the strip of floor you cross in the dark, and nothing heavy sits on a headboard shelf directly above where your head rests.
  • StandardizeWrite down what a made bed means in this house, down to how many pillows go on and whether the duvet is folded back or pulled flat, so it looks the same on the mornings you do it and the mornings someone else does. “A standard is the best way you know today, written down.”
  • SustainMake the bed before you leave the room, while the duvet is still thrown back and it takes one pull. Come back at ten and you are moving yesterday's clothes off it first.
How to clean it

Strip it right back and clean what the sheets hide, working top down from the headboard to the bare mattress to the floor beneath. The surface most people skip is the strip of floor and wall behind the headboard, where dust and hair pack in unseen.

Surface or areaMethod
The headboard top edge and any shelf built into itDry microfiber first to lift the settled dust, then a cloth barely damp with the neutral pH cleaner, drying as you go so no moisture sits in the join or on the wood.
The wall and floor behind the headboardRun the vacuum crevice tool down the gap where dust and hair pack against the wall, then draw a dry cloth down the skirting behind the frame.
The pillowcases, sheets, duvet cover and the folded throwStrip them all off and send them to the wash, and pull the pillow protectors off too so the layer that sits against your face goes through the machine, not just the outer case.
The sleeping pillows and the duvet themselvesWhile the covers are off, plump each pillow and stand it on edge to air, and air the duvet over the footboard so it dries out rather than trapping the night's moisture.
The mattress face, the piped seams and the protectorWith the mattress bare, vacuum the whole face with the upholstery tool, working the piped seams and any buttons where dust and skin flakes settle, then rotate or flip it end to end and vacuum the underside before the protector goes back on.
The bed frame rails, legs and slatsWipe along the top of the rails and down the legs with a damp cloth, getting the inner faces of the slats you never see once the mattress is back down.
The floor under the bed and any storage binsPull the bins out, vacuum the full strip of floor and into the corners, and wipe the bin lids before they slide back.
What you clean with
Compact vacuum with attachmentsColor-coded microfiber cloth setNeutral pH multi-surface cleanerPortable cleaning caddyLaundry detergent for the stripped bedding
Inspect as you clean
  • A yellowed patch or a dip formed on one side of the mattress, telling you the surface is wearing unevenly.
  • Dark speckling or a musty note in the mattress seam, the first sign of a pest or trapped damp.
  • A cool, damp feel or a mildew smell on the wall behind the headboard, which can mean moisture is coming through from outside.
  • A bolt or joint on the frame worked loose enough to let a rail flex when you press on it.
The call that matters here
The third sheet set

You are holding four or five sets because one was a wedding gift, one is new but scratchy, and one is the good set you have never slept on. Two per bed is the honest number: one on, one clean. Keep a third only if a child or a pet actually sleeps here and you have stripped this bed at two in the morning within the last year. Anything past three is a set you wash, fold, and never put on a bed. And the good one goes on tonight, because a sheet saved for an occasion is a sheet nobody ever uses.

Watch for
  • FallStorage bins, shoes and a charging cable in the strip of floor between the bed and the door are what your foot finds at three in the morning on the way to the bathroom. Keep that strip bare.
  • Fall, cut, or crushFramed pictures, a speaker or a stack of books on a headboard ledge or hung on the wall above the pillows sit directly over a sleeping head. Move them, or fix them into the wall properly.
Leave behind

Two sheet sets for this bed, each folded inside its own pillowcase; a made bed means the agreed pillow count and nothing else on the cover.

Trigger: When you strip the bed to wash the sheets, look underneath it before the clean set goes on.

See Chapter 25, the ideal-state photo.

Nightstand Left

15-30 min, most of it in Sort, and most of that inside the drawer

This is your side, and it holds only what you reach for between getting into bed and falling asleep.

Done looks like

On top: lamp, a lidded bottle or a coaster, one book, and a small dish holding your glasses. Five things or fewer, with no cable crossing the surface. The drawer has three divided sections with room left in each, and you can name what is in each one without opening it.

  • SortEmpty the drawer onto the bed. Dead pens, chargers for phones you no longer own, hotel earplugs still in their wrappers, and the four books you started and put down all go. A book you are not reading this week belongs on the shelf, not beside your head.
  • StraightenDivide the drawer so glasses, lip balm and earplugs each sit in their own section instead of sliding into one heap, and clip the lamp and clock cords down the back leg so nothing loops across the top.
  • ShineLift everything off, wipe the top including the ring the water glass has been leaving, then wash the lampshade and the bulb so your reading light is the brightness you actually paid for.
  • SafetyWater and a power strip do not share this surface. Move the strip off the nightstand top and onto the floor behind it, and if you drink in bed, drink from a bottle with a lid.
  • StandardizeFive things or fewer on the top, countable from the doorway. When a sixth turns up it is almost always a finished book or an empty glass; the book goes to the shelf and the glass goes to the kitchen that night.
  • SustainWhen you reach over to switch off the lamp, put the top back to five before your hand comes back. It takes seconds and you wake to a clear surface.
How to clean it

Lift every item off before you wipe, because the point is the water ring and the dust you cannot see with the lamp and the glass in the way. Shade and bulb first, top and drawer next, cord and floor last.

Surface or areaMethod
The lampshade and the bulbLift the shade off, dust it or wash a washable one in mild soapy water and dry it fully, then wipe the cold bulb with a dry cloth so your reading light is the brightness you actually paid for.
The lamp base and switchWipe with a barely damp cloth, getting into the switch and the ring where fingers turn it on night after night.
The nightstand top and the water-glass ringClear everything off, wipe the whole top with the neutral pH cleaner, and work at the pale ring the water glass has been leaving.
The items themselves: the dish, the book and your reading glassesWipe the small dish and dust the top edge of the book, then clean your reading glasses with the dry microfiber only, not the glass cleaner, since window cleaner can strip the lens coating over time.
The drawer and its divided sectionsTip out the sections, vacuum the grit and crumbs from the corners, then wipe the base and both runners.
The floor and cable run behind the legVacuum behind and beneath, then follow the lamp cord down to the floor to make sure nothing is snagged along the skirting.
What you clean with
Neutral pH multi-surface cleanerCompact vacuum with attachmentsColor-coded microfiber cloth setPortable cleaning caddy
Inspect as you clean
  • A cracked, brittle or warm section on the lamp cord where it bends behind the leg.
  • A scorch mark or brown patch on the inside of the shade, a sign the bulb runs hotter than the shade is rated for.
  • The water ring darkening into the wood rather than wiping away, meaning the finish is starting to fail.
  • A leg or joint that rocks when you lean your weight on the nightstand.
The call that matters here
The phone charger

You know the phone should not sleep here, and you have kept it because it is your alarm. That is one job, and a nine pound clock does it. Buy the clock, move the charger to the dresser across the room, and give the phone a landing spot you have to stand up to reach. If you will not commit to that, then commit to the honest version instead: the charger stays, the phone lies face down, and something else comes off the top so you are still at five. What you cannot keep doing is calling the current arrangement temporary.

Watch for
  • Water and electricityAn open glass of water sitting beside a lamp base, a charger and a power strip on one small top is a single knocked elbow away from a problem. Put a lid on the water or move the power off the top.
  • Burn or fireA lit candle on a nightstand sits level with the bedding and right where your sleeve passes when you reach for the lamp. If you want one burning at night, it burns on the dresser.
Leave behind

Five things or fewer on the top, one book at a time, and a divided drawer where every section has a name.

Trigger: When you reach over to switch off the lamp, that is the moment the top goes back to five.

Nightstand Right

15-30 min, most of it in the conversation before you start, very little in the work

This is the other sleeper's side, and it is the one zone in this room that is not yours to decide about.

Done looks like

The top is clear enough to set a mug down without moving anything first, the lamp cord runs behind the leg rather than across the floor beside the bed, and any medication in the drawer is in date, capped, and closed away from a child or a dog who can climb onto this bed.

  • SortYou do not empty this drawer. Bring a bin liner and a box into the room, finish your own side first so there is something to look at, then ask whether they want the same. What they hand you goes. What they keep stays, and you say nothing about it.
  • StraightenAgree on the shared edge only: the floor beside this nightstand stays clear because you both walk past it, and the lamp cord runs down the leg for the same reason. What fills the drawer is entirely theirs.
  • ShineDust the top, wipe the lamp base and the switch, and vacuum the slot between the nightstand and the bed frame where tissues, hairbands and dust collect where neither of you looks.
  • SafetyMedication is the one exception to leaving this side alone. Check the dates together, screw the caps down properly, and if a child or a dog gets up onto this bed, move tablets out of the open drawer into a closed box.
  • StandardizeMatch the standard, not the contents. Both tops run the same item count and the same lidded drink rule, and what fills each drawer is each sleeper's own business.
  • SustainWhen the sheets come off, both nightstand tops get cleared and wiped as part of the same job, so one side cannot quietly drift while the other holds.
How to clean it

Clean the other sleeper's side without reorganising it. Work the lamp and the top, then get the one spot both of you ignore, the narrow slot between the nightstand and the bed frame.

Surface or areaMethod
The lampshade and bulb on this sideTake the shade off, dust or wash it, and wipe the cool bulb so the light matches the other side of the bed.
The lamp base and the switchWipe the base and clean the switch, where a hand reaches in the dark and leaves a grime line.
The top and the mug ringDust and wipe the whole top, treating the ring the last mug left behind.
The framed photo and its glassLift the frame, dust the back and the stand, then mist the glass cleaner onto the cloth rather than the glass and wipe the front streak-free so it goes back clear.
The drawer interiorVacuum it out rather than soaking it, and wipe the base around whatever medication lives there without unpacking someone else's system.
The slot between the nightstand and the bed frameRun the crevice tool down the narrow gap where tissues, hairbands and dust collect where neither of you ever looks.
The floor and the cord behind the legVacuum beneath and behind, following the cord along the wall to keep it off the walking strip.
What you clean with
Streak-free glass and mirror cleanerNeutral pH multi-surface cleanerCompact vacuum with attachmentsColor-coded microfiber cloth setPortable cleaning caddy
Inspect as you clean
  • A sticky spill or residue in the drawer from a bottle that has leaked or sweated.
  • A frayed or warm patch on the lamp cord where it runs behind the leg.
  • Tarnish or a green bloom on a metal lamp base or the metal frame of the photo.
  • A drawer pull or knob that has worked loose in its screw.
The call that matters here
Whose things you are allowed to throw away

You want to fix this side because you can see it from yours, and every argument about tidiness in a shared bedroom starts right here. The rule that settles it: you clean, they cull. You may wipe, vacuum and straighten anything on this side, and you may not remove a single object from it. Finish your own nightstand to the standard, leave theirs untouched, and let the difference between the two tops be the entire argument you make. If they never change a thing, you have still fixed half the room and kept the marriage. The one item you can raise out loud is the out of date medication, because that is a safety question and not a taste question.

Watch for
  • Poison, choke, or strangleSleep aids, painkillers and prescription bottles loose in an open bedside drawer sit at exactly the height a toddler or a dog reaches from the mattress. Cap them and shut them into a box.
  • FallA lamp cord crossing the walking strip on this side catches a foot in a dark room. Run it down behind the leg and along the wall.
Leave behind

The same item count on both tops, medication in date and closed away, and the drawer contents belonging to whoever sleeps on that side.

Trigger: When the sheets go into the wash, both tops get cleared and wiped in the same pass.

See Chapter 27, the friendly audit.

Dresser Top

15-30 min, most of it in Sort, working through jewellery and pocket sediment

This is where your pockets land at the end of the day and where tomorrow's watch and rings wait for you.

Done looks like

One valet tray holding keys, wallet, watch and rings, with nothing sitting outside it. Fragrance bottles grouped on a small dish, set back from the front edge. No folded laundry, no receipts, no loose coins on the wood. An anti-tip strap runs from the back of the dresser into the wall.

  • SortWork through the pocket sediment: loose change into a jar that lives in another room, receipts binned or photographed, single earrings and snapped chains into one small bag you either take to a repair counter this month or let go. Jewellery you love but never put on moves to a box in the closet, not back onto this top.
  • StraightenOne tray, and everything that comes out of your pockets at night lands inside it. Fragrance gets its own dish so the bottles stop wandering, and folded clothes never rest here on the way to the drawer.
  • ShineTake the tray and the bottles off, wipe the top, and work at the sticky ring under the fragrance where the sprayer has been misting the wood for years. Wipe the drawer fronts and the handles while the top is bare.
  • SafetyA tall chest of drawers that is not strapped to the wall tips forward when a loaded drawer is open and a child leans on it. Fit an anti-tip strap into a stud, and keep the glass bottles back from the edge so they cannot be knocked onto a hard floor.
  • StandardizeThe tray is the edge. If the keys, wallet, watch and rings will not sit in it without stacking, thin the keyring or move a ring to the jewellery box rather than starting a second pile beside the tray.
  • SustainWhen you empty your pockets at night, everything goes into the tray and nothing goes down beside it. That is the whole habit.
How to clean it

Take everything off the top and go after the sticky fragrance ring the bottles have been hiding for years. Back edge and wall gap first, then the top, the fronts, the sides, and the floor underneath.

Surface or areaMethod
The wall gap and the top back edgeVacuum the dust from the narrow gap behind the dresser, then wipe the back edge of the top where a cloth rarely reaches.
The dresser top and the fragrance ringTake the tray and bottles off, wipe the whole top, and work patiently at the sticky ring under the fragrance where the sprayer has been misting the wood.
The valet tray and the fragrance bottlesEmpty the tray and wipe it inside and out, then clean the necks and bases of the bottles where scent has run down and gone tacky.
The drawer fronts and the handlesWipe each front and get into the handles where hands leave an oily film.
The sides and legs of the dresserWipe both sides and the legs with a damp cloth, taking off the dust that clings to a waxed finish.
The floor beneath and behindEase the dresser out if you safely can, vacuum the exposed strip and corners, then clean the floor with the floor-material-compatible cleaner.
What you clean with
Portable cleaning caddyCompact vacuum with attachmentsColor-coded microfiber cloth setNeutral pH multi-surface cleanerFloor-material-compatible cleaner
Inspect as you clean
  • A white, cloudy ring under the fragrance that no longer wipes away, meaning the alcohol has cut into the finish.
  • A veneer edge lifting or a corner joint opening on the carcass.
  • The anti-tip strap gone slack, or its wall screw sitting proud of the anchor.
  • A leg that rocks on the floor, or a caster that has cracked.
The call that matters here
The perfume you were given

There are three or four bottles standing up here and you wear one of them. The others were gifts, or they were expensive, or they were who you were five years ago. Fragrance turns. A bottle that has stood open for years smells sharper and flatter than the one you were handed, and one you have not reached for in a full year will not be reached for now. Keep the one you wear and the one you honestly alternate to. The rest go, and the guilt goes with them, because whoever gave it to you wanted you to smell good, not to store glass. The same test sorts the jewellery: worn in the last year stays in the tray, loved but unworn goes to a box in the closet, neither worn nor loved leaves the house.

Watch for
  • Fall, cut, or crushAn unstrapped tall dresser tips forward the moment a loaded drawer is open and weight goes on it, and it is heavy enough to pin a child underneath. Strap it into a stud and keep the heaviest clothes in the bottom drawers.
  • Fall, cut, or crushGlass fragrance bottles parked on the front edge break into small pieces on a hard floor beside a bed you walk to barefoot. Set them back and group them on a dish.
Leave behind

One valet tray holds every daily-carry item; if it will not sit in the tray, it does not live on the dresser.

Trigger: When you empty your pockets at the end of the day, the tray takes all of it and the wood stays bare.

See Chapter 14, visual controls.

Dresser Drawers

60-90 min, most of it in Sort and refolding, working one drawer at a time

These drawers hold the folded clothes you wear this season, split into categories you can find without digging.

Done looks like

Each drawer holds one category: underwear and socks, sleepwear, t-shirts, workout clothes. Everything folded to stand upright so you read the whole drawer from above when it opens. A hand's width of free space in each drawer, and every drawer slides shut without you pressing anything down first.

  • SortTip one drawer at a time onto the bed. Out go socks whose partners never turned up, waistbands that have given up, the six workout tops you own when you wear two, and the t-shirts from events you no longer think about. Anything stained or holed becomes a rag or textile recycling rather than a donation somebody else has to bin.
  • StraightenFold everything so it stands on its edge and the drawer reads like a row of files instead of a stack you excavate. Put dividers in for socks and underwear so the small things stop sliding under the folded rows, and move the heaviest categories, jeans and knitwear, into the bottom drawers.
  • ShineEmpty each drawer completely, vacuum the corners and the runners, wipe the base, and let it dry before anything goes back in. While it is out, check the runners and tighten any screw that has worked loose.
  • SafetyOpen one drawer at a time. Two loaded drawers out together pulls the weight far enough forward to tip the whole chest, and a child using the open drawers as steps does it faster.
  • StandardizeOne category per drawer, and a hand's width of free space is the fill line. When a drawer needs pressing to close, that category has outgrown its drawer and one item comes out the same day.
  • SustainWhen the clean laundry comes back to the bedroom, it gets folded upright and put into the drawer then, rather than living in a stack on the dresser top until the weekend.
How to clean it

Pull each drawer right out, because the grit that makes a drawer stick lives under it and in the runner, not inside it. Empty, vacuum, wipe, dry, then refold back in.

Surface or areaMethod
The inside of each drawerLift each drawer out completely, vacuum the corners and the folds of any liner, and wipe the base with a barely damp cloth, letting it dry fully before clothes return.
The runners and tracksWork the small detail brush along the metal runners and into the track, lifting the grit that makes a drawer stick and then jump when it is shoved shut, then wipe them clean.
The carcass interior behind the drawersWith the drawers out, vacuum inside the empty frame and the back panel where dust and stray socks gather.
The drawer fronts and the label edgeWipe the fronts and the inside front edge where the category names are written.
The folded contents as you reloadThis is not a wash, but as each stack goes back, shake it out and confirm nothing has been living in it.
What you clean with
Compact vacuum with attachmentsSmall detail brush setColor-coded microfiber cloth setNeutral pH multi-surface cleanerPortable cleaning caddy
Inspect as you clean
  • A runner screw backed out, or a runner bent so the drawer drops at one corner.
  • A drawer base panel bowing or splitting under the weight it carries.
  • A musty or damp smell in the empty drawer, or a tide line low on the back panel.
  • Moth casings, grazed holes in a folded wool item, or crumbled cedar that mean pests have been at work.
The call that matters here
The size you are not right now

Two of these drawers are part filled with clothes that do not fit today, so every morning starts with a small unpleasant reminder instead of a clean choice. Take them out of the bedroom entirely. One box, not two, today's date written on the lid, stored somewhere else in the house. If that box is still sealed a year from now it leaves sealed, and you do not look inside, because looking is how it all comes back. These drawers dress the body you have this morning. That is not giving up on anything. It is refusing to open a drawer and be told off by it every day.

Watch for
  • Fall, cut, or crushTwo heavy drawers open at once, or a child climbing the open drawers like steps, is how a chest of drawers comes down on someone. One drawer at a time, heaviest clothes lowest, anti-tip strap fitted.
  • Poison, choke, or strangleMothballs and cedar blocks rolling loose in a sock drawer look like sweets to a toddler standing at the open bottom drawer. Put them in a mesh bag hung at the back, or leave them out.
Leave behind

One category per drawer, folded to stand upright, and a hand's width of free space kept as the fill line.

Trigger: When the laundry basket comes back up to the bedroom, it gets folded into the drawers before you put the empty basket down.

See Chapter 13, a home chosen by how it is used.

Primary Closet

60-90 min, most of it in Sort, and the try-on pile will take longer than you planned

The closet holds the clothes you actually wear this season, visible and reachable in one look.

Done looks like

One hanger style throughout, grouped by type and then by shade, every hook facing the same way. Shoes in pairs on a rack instead of a floor heap, bags standing upright on the shelf, belts on one hook. Two fingers of space between hanging items, a bare stretch of rod at one end, and floor you can stand on.

  • SortPull out anything you have not worn in a year, anything waiting on a repair you are never going to make, and every wire hanger and plastic sheath the dry cleaner sent home. Winter coats and heavy knits go into a labelled bin or the guest wardrobe so the rod carries only what this month asks for.
  • StraightenSwitch to one hanger style so everything hangs at the same depth and the row reads flat, group by type and then by shade, and hang the short items together so the floor under them opens up for the shoe rack. Bags stand up on the shelf rather than nesting inside one another where you forget the inner three.
  • ShineClear the floor completely, vacuum it and the corners, wipe the shelf edges and the rod itself where hangers leave a grey line, and check the back wall and skirting for damp before the shoes go back down.
  • SafetyAn overloaded rod pulls its brackets out of the plasterboard, and it does it while you are standing underneath reaching in. Lean your weight on it and see, anchor the brackets into a stud, and get the shoe pile off the floor so you are not standing on a heel in the dark.
  • StandardizeThe rod is full when you cannot slide two fingers between hangers. From there it is one in, one out, and the bare stretch of rod at the end stays bare, because that is where the next coat lands.
  • SustainTurn every hanger backwards at the start of a season, and turn each one the right way round as you wear the item. When the season ends, the hangers still facing backwards have made the donation decisions for you.
How to clean it

Clear the closet to bare rod, shelf and floor, then clean from the top shelf down to the corners. The rod and the back wall are what everyone leaves, and they are exactly where trouble starts.

Surface or areaMethod
The top shelf and whatever is stored on itLift the bags and boxes down, dust the shelf top where a film has been settling onto stored things all year, and wipe the shelf edge where a grey line builds.
The hanging rodRun a damp cloth the full length of the rod to take off the fuzzy grey film that otherwise transfers straight onto a dark shoulder.
The back wall and skirtingWith the rod and floor clear, wipe the back wall and draw a cloth along the skirting, looking closely as you go.
The shoe rackLift the shoes off, wipe the rack shelves and the grit and dried mud they hold, and wipe the soles before pairs go back.
The floor and cornersVacuum the whole floor and into the corners where dust gathers undisturbed for months, then wipe any sealed flooring.
The door, frame and handleWipe the inside face of the door and the handle where it is gripped every day.
What you clean with
Compact vacuum with attachmentsNeutral pH multi-surface cleanerColor-coded microfiber cloth setPortable cleaning caddy
Inspect as you clean
  • A damp patch, a bloom of mildew or a musty smell on the back wall or skirting, the sign of moisture coming through.
  • A rod bracket screw backing out of the wall, or the bracket flexing when you press down on the rod.
  • Moth casings or grazed holes on the shoulders of stored wool coats.
  • A soft or springy board underfoot in the closet floor.
The call that matters here
The expensive mistake

There is one thing hanging in here that cost real money and does not fit your life: the suit for the job you left, the coat that is beautiful and far too warm, the shoes that turn on you after twenty minutes. You keep it because letting it go makes the money real. The money is already gone. It left the day you bought it, and keeping the garment does not bring back a penny of it; it just takes a hanger you need and gives you a small flinch every time you slide past. Set one deadline. Photograph it tonight, list it for sale, and if it has not sold in thirty days it goes to the charity shop without another conversation. The lesson is worth keeping. The garment is not.

Watch for
  • Fall, cut, or crushA rod loaded end to end with winter coats can tear its brackets out of plasterboard and bring the whole rail, and everything on it, down on you. Anchor into a stud and stop hanging past two fingers of space.
  • FallA heap of shoes and bags across the closet floor is what you step onto half awake in an unlit room. Get the floor bare and the shoes onto a rack in pairs.
  • Poison, choke, or strangleThin dry-cleaning bags cling to a face and are a suffocation risk if a small child plays in here. Pull them off at the door and bin them rather than storing clothes inside them.
Leave behind

One hanger style, grouped by type then shade, two fingers of space between items, and one in one out once the rod is full.

Trigger: When you hang up the first coat of a new season, turn every hanger on the rod backwards and start the count again.

See Chapter 21, the hazard hunt.

Room 08 of 20

Guest Bedroom

5 zones

The guest bedroom is the only room in the house with no daily user, which is exactly why it fills up. Nothing here objects when you set a box down, so the room quietly absorbs whatever the rest of the house could not decide about, and then you excavate it every time someone visits. Start with the closet rather than the bed, because the closet is where the overflow actually lives, and the bed goes quickly once the room around it is honest.

Where to startOpen the closet first and pull everything onto the bed. You cannot judge how much room a visitor actually has while half of it is hidden behind a closet door, and working on the bed forces you to finish before anyone can sleep in here.
Cleaning noteLinen that has sat folded since spring smells of the cupboard it sat in, even though it is clean. Wash the set that goes on the bed the week a guest arrives, and open the window while you make it up.
