the inside of each drawer, down to the base and corners (hidden)Empty each drawer completely. Vacuum out the sock lint and dried spit-up that gathers in the corners and along the front lip, then wipe the base with a barely damp cloth and let it dry before the clothes go back filed upright.
the drawer runnersWith the drawer out, wipe both runners clean of lint and grit so the drawer glides and closes flush again.
the closet shelf with the next-size binLift the next-size bin down, dust and wipe the shelf, and wipe the outside of the bin before it goes back up.
the door hook and the outgoing bagWipe the hook, and empty and shake out or wash the outgoing bag so it is ready to keep taking outgrown clothes rather than becoming a permanent fixture.
the floor and skirting behind and under the chest (hidden)Vacuum the floor under and behind the chest and wipe the skirting, the strip that only gets reached when you are already down there cleaning.
What you clean with
Color-coded microfiber cloth setNeutral pH multi-surface cleanerCompact vacuum with attachmentsPortable cleaning caddyWashing machine
Inspect as you clean
  • With a drawer out, note any runner screw that has loosened or a drawer front separating from the box.
  • Look at the anti-tip strap where it meets the wall and the chest, and flag it if the screw has backed out or the strap has gone slack.
  • Note a musty smell or a damp bloom in a drawer backed onto an outside wall, the early sign of moisture wicking in.
  • As you refold hand-me-downs, flag any set-in stain, split seam, or worn snap so it is caught now and not at dawn.
  • Check that the chest still sits level and does not rock when you lean weight on an open drawer, and flag any wobble.
The call that matters here
The outfit she never wore

There is a 0 to 3 month outfit in that drawer with the tags still on, given by someone who mattered, now three sizes too small. Keeping it feels like keeping the gift; giving it away feels like a small betrayal. The rule that settles it: keep two garments per size, and choose them by what your baby actually lived in, the sleeper from the first night home, the one in every photograph. The unworn gift is not a memory, it is stock, and it should go to someone else while it is still new enough to delight them. Photograph it on the way out if that helps you close the bag.

Watch for
  • Fall, cut, or crushAn unanchored chest of drawers with two drawers open will tip forward onto a child using it to climb; anchor it to the wall and close each drawer before opening the next.
  • Poison, choke, or strangleDecorative buttons, ribbon ties, and hood drawstrings on older hand-me-down clothing come off in a mouth or wrap around a neck; remove them before the garment enters the drawer.
Leave behind

One size per drawer, and outgrown clothing never spends a night in the room.

Trigger: when a snap will not close over the diaper

Feeding Station

30-45 min, most of it in Straighten, splitting clean from used

Puts a clean assembled bottle or a full set of pump parts into your hands at 2 a.m. without a single decision.

Done looks like

One bin of clean bottles assembled with teats and collars on, a second bin of used parts waiting, a drying rack standing empty, the formula scoop inside the tin with the lid shut, and a stack of burp cloths you can grab without looking.

  • SortLine up every bottle, teat, valve, membrane, and pump part on the surface at once. Teats gone cloudy or split at the slit go in the bin rather than back in the drawer, and so does every part belonging to a pump or bottle brand you have stopped using.
  • StraightenTwo bins in obviously different colors, one for clean and ready, one for used and waiting. Assemble bottles fully as they come out of the sterilizer, teat and collar on, so the middle of the night is a grab rather than a hunt for a matching collar.
  • ShineWash and sterilize on the schedule your kit calls for, then let parts air dry completely before they go into the clean bin, because a damp valve in a closed bin is where mold starts. Descale the kettle or sterilizer monthly and wipe the surface under the drying rack where drips collect.
  • SafetyKettles, bottle warmers, and sterilizers sit at roughly the same height as the changing pad, so a pulled cord brings scalding water down at child height. Loop every cable short and run it behind the appliance, and keep the kettle and the water jug well clear of the socket and the monitor plug so nothing wet stands next to anything live.
  • StandardizeWrite the formula scoop-to-water numbers you actually use on a card taped above the station, so a grandparent or a night visitor mixes it right the first time. “A standard is the best way you know today, written down.”
  • SustainWhen the kitchen light goes off for the night, the used bin goes down to be washed and the clean bin comes back stocked to four assembled bottles.
How to clean it

A damp valve in a closed bin is where mold starts, so nothing goes away wet. Clean top to bottom, from the appliances down to the surface under the drying rack, and dry everything fully before it lands in the clean bin.

Surface or areaMethod
the shelf holding the clean and used binsTake both bins off. Dust and wipe the shelf with neutral pH cleaner, then use the food-area degreaser on any spot where formula or milk has dried into a film.
the kettle, bottle warmer, or sterilizer exterior and cordsWipe each appliance body with a barely damp cloth, keeping water away from the plug and vents, and use the degreaser on any milk splash. Run the cloth along each cord, then coil each cord short again and set it back behind the appliance.
the kettle or sterilizer interior and heating elementDescale monthly: fill with the descaler or a citric-acid solution, run or soak on the maker's schedule, then rinse two or three times until the water runs clear. Look at the element or plate as you rinse.
the bottle parts, teats, collars, and valves (the items)Wash used parts in hot soapy water with a bottle brush, sterilize on your kit's setting, then stand every part separately on the rack to air dry completely. Only fully dry, assembled bottles go into the clean bin.
the drying rackEmpty the rack and wash it in hot soapy water, getting into the pegs and the base tray where water sits. Dry it before parts go back on it, because the rack itself grows a slime if it never dries out.
the surface under the drying rack (hidden)Lift the rack right off and wipe the counter beneath it with neutral pH cleaner, then dry it. This is where drips pool and where a ring of scale and mold quietly forms.
the formula tin, scoop, and burp cloth stackWipe the tin and its lid, and check the scoop is dry and shut inside with the lid closed rather than left damp. Refresh the burp cloth stack with clean ones so you can grab without looking.
What you clean with
Food-area degreaserNeutral pH multi-surface cleanerColor-coded microfiber cloth setPortable cleaning caddyDescaler or citric acid for the kettle or sterilizerBottle brush and baby-safe dish soap
Inspect as you clean
  • As you wash the parts, look inside every teat and valve for a dark speck of mold or a cloudy haze, and flag a teat that has gone tacky, split, or cloudy.
  • When you rinse the kettle or sterilizer, note scale furring the element or plate, the reason it stops heating evenly.
  • Handle each appliance cord and flag any cracked, scorched, or pinched section, and any plug that feels warm.
  • Note any rust spot on a sterilizer tray or a warmer's metal, and flag it.
  • Check the drying rack and its tray for a mold line or a soft slimy film that keeps coming back.
The call that matters here
The bottle she rejected

You now own three brands, because the first was refused and everybody swore the second one worked for them. The rule: give a new teat or bottle three honest feeds across a week, offered when your baby is calm and hungry rather than already crying, and then decide. If it fails that test, the whole system leaves the house, box and spares included. Keeping five brands on the shelf means washing, sterilizing, storing, and matching parts for five systems in order to use one, and every one of those steps happens at 2 a.m.

Watch for
  • Burn or fireA kettle, warmer, or sterilizer cord left long enough to be grabbed will pull boiling water down onto whoever is below it; keep cords looped short and set back behind the appliance.
  • Water and electricityA water jug or a wet drying rack standing beside the sterilizer socket and the monitor plug puts standing water next to live current on the same surface.
Leave behind

Clean and ready never shares a bin with used and waiting.

Trigger: when the kitchen light goes off for the night

See Chapter 14, visual controls.

Diaper and Care Backstock

30-45 min, most of it in Sort, because the wrong sizes are the entire problem

Holds the reserve that feeds the changing station, in the sizes your baby is about to need rather than the ones on offer.

Done looks like

One unopened pack of the current size and no more than two of the next size on a low shelf, each size written large in marker on the short end, a wipes carton with its count visible, creams and thermometer in one lidded box, and nothing at all stored on a shelf above the crib.

  • SortPull every pack off the shelf and read the printed size rather than trusting the picture on the front. Anything smaller than the size in use today comes off, including packs you have already opened, because a half-used pack of size 1 is still size 1.
  • StraightenWrite the size in marker on the short end of every pack so it reads from the doorway, then shelve the current size at the front with the next size directly behind it. Creams, teething gel, and the thermometer go into one lidded box rather than loose between the cartons.
  • ShineWipe the shelf down and check the undersides of the cartons for damp, since packs stored against an outside wall wick moisture up through the cardboard. While the shelf is empty, read the dates on every cream and infant medicine and bin what has passed.
  • SafetyNothing heavy goes on a shelf above or beside the crib, because a carton that works its way off lands exactly where your baby sleeps. Keep the outer plastic wrap and the pail liner roll out of reach, and keep diaper creams, teething gels, and any infant medicine in a closed box a standing toddler cannot open.
  • StandardizeWrite the minimum and maximum on the shelf edge itself: never fewer than one unopened pack of the current size, never more than two packs of any one size.
  • SustainWhen the last diaper comes out of an open pack, the reserve pack moves forward to the front and the size goes onto the shopping list in the same motion.
How to clean it

This shelf is quiet, which is exactly why it hides damp and expiry. Wipe it bare, check the undersides of the cartons, and read every date while you can see the whole shelf at once.

Surface or areaMethod
the shelf boards, emptyTake everything off the shelf. Vacuum the corners and front lip, then wipe the boards with neutral pH cleaner and dry them fully before anything returns.
the shelf-edge size labelsWipe each label and refresh the marker if it has faded, so each size still reads large from a step back and packs go back in date order.
the diaper and wipes cartons, including their undersides (hidden)Wipe the outside of each carton, then turn it and check the underside and base, the face that sits on the board against the wall. Set them back with counts visible and the current size to the front.
the lidded box of creams, gels, and thermometer (the items)Empty the box, wipe the sticky rings that cream tubes and gel caps leave inside, and wipe each tube and the thermometer before they go back. Dry the box before you close it.
the wipes carton and its dispensing lidWipe the carton and clean the dispensing lid where solution dries into a crust, then reseal it so the wipes do not dry out.
the floor and the outside wall behind the shelf (hidden)Vacuum and wipe the floor under the shelf, and run a dry hand along the wall behind it, since a backstock shelf often sits against an outside wall that sweats.
What you clean with
Neutral pH multi-surface cleanerColor-coded microfiber cloth setCompact vacuum with attachmentsPortable cleaning caddy
Inspect as you clean
  • Check the underside of every carton for damp softness in the cardboard, the sign a pack is wicking moisture up from the floor or an outside wall.
  • Run a hand along the wall behind the shelf and flag a cold damp patch or a dark bloom of mold.
  • As you handle each cream, gel, and infant medicine, read the date and flag anything past it, along with any tube that is split, weeping, or swollen.
  • Note any rust on a tin or a cap, and any pack that has been chewed or torn open low down.
  • Look at how the shelf brackets meet the wall and flag any that show a gap or movement under the loaded weight.
The call that matters here
The good deal in the wrong size

Six packs of size 2 were half price and it felt like money in the bank. Two weeks later your baby went straight from size 1 into size 3. The rule: buy no more than one size ahead, and no more than two packs of it, no matter what the price is, because babies skip sizes without warning and a bulk saving turns into six cartons you now have to find a home for. Unopened packs in an outgrown size leave this week, to a friend or a diaper bank, while they are still worth having. Opened packs go too; they are not going to fit better later.

Watch for
  • Fall, cut, or crushHeavy cartons stacked on a high shelf above or beside the crib can shift and fall into the sleep space; store weight low and never over the crib.
  • Poison, choke, or strangleDiaper creams, teething gels, and infant medicines within reach of a standing child are a swallowing risk, and the outer plastic wrap and pail liner roll are loose plastic film.
Leave behind

One size ahead, two packs deep, and nothing heavy stored above the crib.

Trigger: when the last diaper comes out of an open pack

See Chapter 21, the hazard hunt.

Books and Quiet Play Zone

15-30 min, most of it in Sort, and it goes quickly once you accept how small the number is

A short, calm set of things your baby can reach and choose without you handing anything over.

Done looks like

Twelve board books standing cover out on a low ledge, one basket of soft toys filled level with the rim and no higher, the rest boxed in the closet as rotation stock, and a clear stretch of floor big enough for an adult to lie down on.

  • SortSit on the floor at your baby's eye level and pick up every book and toy in reach. Board books stay; paper-page books, torn spines, loose stitched eyes, and any battery flap that will not screw shut come out or go up on a high shelf until later.
  • StraightenCovers face out along the ledge, because a pre-reader chooses by picture and cannot read a spine. Fill the soft toy basket to its rim and treat the rim as the limit, with everything above it going into the rotation box in the closet.
  • ShineWipe the board book covers and the ledge, since chewed corners and drool are essentially the whole cleaning job in this zone. Run soft toys through a monthly wash and check tags, seams, and stitched eyes as you pull each one out of the machine.
  • SafetyBolt the shelf or ledge to the wall, because the day your baby pulls to standing it becomes the nearest handhold and a tip-over risk. Check every light-up or musical toy for a battery compartment that needs a screwdriver, and remove anything with a loose button battery or a ribbon tag long enough to wind around a wrist.
  • StandardizeTwelve covers on the ledge, one basket level with its rim. Anything new that arrives means something goes into the rotation box the same day, not the same month.
  • SustainWhen you switch the lamp off after the bedtime story, stand the books back cover out and swap two off the ledge for two out of the rotation box.
How to clean it

Chewed corners and drool are basically the whole job here, so clean the things your baby actually mouths and reaches. Wipe the books and ledge, wash the soft toys, and check every toy as it comes back.

Surface or areaMethod
the high wall-fixed shelf and its top edgeDust the top of the high shelf first, then wipe it with neutral pH cleaner and dry it. This top edge is where the room's dust actually settles, up above a baby's reach.
the board book covers, spines, and chewed corners (the items)Wipe each board book cover, spine, and chewed corner with a barely damp cloth, since this is a mouthing surface. Use plain water or a baby-safe wipe rather than cleaner, dry them, then stand them cover out on the ledge.
the low front-facing book ledge where the books standClear the ledge, wipe the surface and the front lip where hands grab, and get into the corners where drool and crumbs dry. Dry it before the books go back.
the soft-toy basket, inside and rimEmpty the basket, vacuum it out, and wipe or spot-clean the rim and interior where drool and dust collect in the weave. Refill it level with the rim, no higher.
the soft toys themselves (the soft items)Run the soft toys through a monthly wash on their label setting and dry them fully. Check tags, seams, and stitched eyes as you pull each one out of the machine before it goes back in the basket.
the rotation-stock box in the closetWipe the outside of the box and the closet shelf it sits on, and give the stored toys a quick look before you rotate the next set out.
the clear floor play space, any rug or mat, and the skirting (hidden edges)Vacuum the whole floor space and get right into the skirting and under the ledge. If a rug or foam play mat covers the floor, vacuum the rug or lift and wipe the foam tiles top and underside where drool presses in, then damp-wipe or mop any hard floor. Finish this surface clean and dry, since it is the one your baby lies and rolls on.
What you clean with
Neutral pH multi-surface cleanerColor-coded microfiber cloth setCompact vacuum with attachmentsPortable cleaning caddyWashing machinePlain water or a baby-safe wipe for mouthed book covers
Inspect as you clean
  • As each soft toy comes out of the wash, flag a loosening stitched eye or nose, a split seam letting stuffing show, or a long ribbon tag.
  • Note any light-up or musical toy whose battery flap opens without a tool, or whose sound has gone crackly, a hint of water inside.
  • Check board books for a torn spine or a peeled corner that exposes cardboard or a staple, and flag it.
  • Look at where the ledge meets the wall and flag any bracket showing a gap or a fixing that has worked loose under a pulling-up weight.
  • Note a fraying rim or a broken weave on the toy basket that could leave a sharp end at floor level.
The call that matters here
The books you bought for yourself

Some of these are gorgeous, expensive, paper-paged, and chosen because you wanted to read them aloud. Your baby will chew them into pulp within a week of reaching the ledge. The rule: the low ledge holds only what your baby can handle unsupervised, which today means board books and nothing else. The beautiful ones move to a shelf in your room and get read from your lap, which is how you wanted to read them anyway. This is not the same as giving them up, and refusing to make the split is how you end up with neither a usable ledge nor an intact book.

Watch for
  • Fall, cut, or crushA low bookshelf or ledge that is not fixed to the wall becomes the handhold your baby pulls up on, and it comes down on top of them.
  • Poison, choke, or strangleButton batteries behind an unscrewed flap in a light-up toy, loose stitched eyes on soft toys, and long ribbon tags are all within reach at floor level.
Leave behind

Twelve covers facing out and one basket at the rim; everything else waits in the rotation box.

Trigger: when you switch the lamp off after the bedtime story

Room 11 of 20

Primary Bathroom

7 zones

The primary bathroom is the smallest room you use the most, and it carries three problems no other room has all at once: standing moisture, open chemistry, and dates that quietly pass while you sleep. Start at the vanity counter, because it is the surface you look at twice a day, and clearing it changes what the room looks like from the doorway. Everything else in here gets easier once the counter wipes clean in one pass.

Where to startCounter first, shower second. Those two are what you stand in front of every morning, and finishing them makes the under-sink cabinet worth opening. Leave the medicine cabinet for a session where you are willing to read small print on the back of a box.
Cleaning noteSpray the tub and shower walls before you touch anything else, then work the counter and the toilet while the cleaner sits. Chemistry needs time, not scrubbing. Squeegee the glass last and leave the fan running after you walk out.
The trapTreating an expired thing as stock. A separated sunscreen, a crumbling tablet, a mascara from two summers ago: these are not supplies you are saving, they are failures already sitting on your shelf. Read dates on every Sort pass in here, and take medicines to a pharmacy take-back rather than the toilet.

Vanity Counter

30-45 min, most of it in Sort, deciding what has earned counter space

To hold the handful of things you touch every morning and every night, and nothing else.

Done looks like

One tray you can lift with one hand, holding soap, the toothbrush holder, and no more than four daily products. Bare stone on both sides of the basin, a mirror with no splatter, and no cord crossing the sink.

  • SortPull everything off the counter onto a towel and put back only what your hands touched today and yesterday. The travel bottles, the sample sachets, and the hair tie graveyard go into a drawer or out of the room entirely.
  • StraightenGive the daily set one tray, on the side you reach with your dominant hand. The heat tool gets a heat-safe rest and its cord parks behind the basin rather than draped across it.
  • ShineWipe the tray, then the counter under it, then the base of the faucet where toothpaste dries into a crust. Finish with the mirror and the light switch plate, the thing in this room nobody ever wipes.
  • SafetyWater and a plugged-in dryer share one small surface in here. Keep the cord on the far side of the basin from the tap, and pull the plug at the wall the moment you set the tool down, not once your hair is finished.
  • StandardizeWrite the four-product limit on a strip of tape stuck under the tray, and tape a photo of the cleared counter inside the vanity door. A fifth bottle then has something to argue with before it lands.
  • SustainWhen you rinse your toothbrush at night, the tray goes back to match the photo before the light goes off. Same moment, every night.
How to clean it

Work the whole station top to bottom: mirror and switch plate first, then the tray and its daily few, then the tap and the stone, so every drip lands on something you have not cleaned yet. The base of the faucet and the switch plate are the two spots nobody ever wipes.

Surface or areaMethod
The mirror and the light fixture above itDry-dust the fixture and bulbs with the microfiber first so grime does not rain down later, then spray glass cleaner onto the cloth, not the mirror, and wipe the glass top to bottom in overlapping strokes. Buff dry with a second dry cloth so no film shows under the bulb light.
The light switch plate and the wall behind the tapMist a cloth with neutral pH cleaner and wipe the switch plate and its screw heads, where fingertips leave a grey bloom, keeping the cloth damp rather than wet so nothing runs into the switch. Wipe the backsplash wall down to the counter and give the caulk seam where wall meets stone a firm pass.
The tray and the daily items on itLift the whole tray off, wipe the counter under it, then wipe the tray itself and the base of the soap dish and toothbrush holder, where a soft pink film sets. Stand each item back only once it is dry.
The faucet, its base collar, and the handlesWork bathroom grime and soap-film cleaner into a detail brush and scrub the collar where the tap meets the stone, the crust of dried toothpaste, and the hard-water scale around the handles. Let it dwell a minute, then wipe and buff the metal dry so it does not spot.
The basin bowl, drain flange, and overflow holeWet the bowl, apply the soap-film cleaner, and work it around the flange and into the overflow slot with the detail brush. Rinse the whole bowl and wipe it dry so no ring is left to harden at the water line.
The stone counter on both sides of the basinClear the surface, then wipe the full run of stone with neutral pH cleaner, which will not etch the finish the way an acidic bathroom spray would. Follow the counter into the seam at the wall and out to the front edge, then dry it off rather than leaving it to air-dry into water marks.
The counter front lip and undersideRun a damp cloth along the front edge and the underside lip where hands grip, and down to the toe-kick and floor seam below, catching the hair and dust that gather where the vanity meets the floor.
What you clean with
Bathroom grime and soap-film cleanerNeutral pH multi-surface cleanerStreak-free glass and mirror cleanerColor-coded microfiber cloth setSmall detail brush setPortable cleaning caddy
Inspect as you clean
  • A caulk line at the backsplash or around the basin going black or lifting away from the stone, which is water getting in behind.
  • Dark spots creeping in from the edges of the mirror silvering, a sign moisture is sitting behind the glass.
  • Water pooling or a chalky ring under the faucet base that keeps coming back, which points to a slow seep at the tap fitting.
  • A hairline crack in the basin, or a switch plate that is cracked, warm to the touch, or loose on the wall.
  • Rust freckling on the tap fixings, or a towel ring that has begun to wobble in its screws.
The call that matters here
The tool that lives out

The straightener or the dryer sits on the counter because the outlet is right there and you use it three or four times a week. That convenience is exactly what is costing you a clear counter, and it is the decision you keep stepping around. The rule that settles it: the counter holds only what you touch every single day. Three times a week is a drawer tool, so buy the heat-safe pouch, give it a drawer, and accept the few seconds it costs you. If the drawer genuinely will not reach from where you stand to dry your hair, the honest fix is a wall-mounted holder beside the mirror, not the counter.

Watch for
  • Water and electricityA plugged-in heat tool sitting within cord reach of a full basin. Nothing stays plugged in while there is water standing in the bowl.
  • Burn or fireA hot iron set down on a towel or a cosmetic bag to cool. Give it a metal or ceramic rest it always returns to, in the same spot every time.
  • Fall, cut, or crushGlass bottles standing at the counter edge above a tile floor. Move glass to the back row against the mirror, or decant into something that does not shatter.
Leave behind

The counter carries one liftable tray and nothing beside it. Anything used less than daily lives behind a door.

Trigger: The nightly toothbrush rinse. The tray goes back to the photo before you leave the room.

See Chapter 25, the ideal-state photo.

Vanity Drawers

45-75 min, most of it in Sort, because every jar has to be picked up and read

To hold the grooming tools and products you actually reach for, arranged by when in the day you use them.

Done looks like

Every drawer divided, nothing past its open-jar window, razor blades capped or in a lidded box in one corner, and a top drawer you can read in a single look without moving anything.

  • SortEmpty one drawer at a time onto a towel and turn every tube, jar, and bottle over to find a date or an open-jar symbol. Mascara, liquid foundation, and sunscreen are the usual offenders; dead hair ties and orphan bobby pins are the usual volume.
  • StraightenDivide by routine rather than by product type: a morning compartment, an evening compartment, and one for weekly and occasional. Combs, brushes, and dental supplies stand upright in their own section so nothing has to be dug through.
  • ShineLift the dividers out and tip the drawer over a bin, because loose powder, hair, and grit collect underneath where you never look. Wipe the drawer bottom and the runners, then wipe the dividers before they go back in.
  • SafetyLoose blades and razor cartridges in a drawer you reach into blind will find a fingertip. Cap every blade, or give all razors one lidded box in a fixed corner you never grope past.
  • StandardizeWrite a count on each divider label: two lipsticks in rotation, one comb, one brush. A third lipstick has to beat one of the two already in the slot before it gets to stay.
  • SustainOn laundry day, while you are already carrying towels into the room, pull the top drawer and lift out anything empty or dried out. One drawer, one look, a week of drift undone.
How to clean it

The dirt in these drawers hides underneath, not on top. Lift the dividers right out, tip the drawer over a bin, and clean the parts you never see before you refill by day-order.

Surface or areaMethod
The drawer fronts and their pullsWipe the outer face and the handle with a neutral pH damp cloth, then run a fingernail behind the cloth along the top edge of the front where a thumb pushes it shut and a dark line builds.
The lift-out dividers and insert traysTake every divider out and wash it in warm water with the soap-film cleaner to shift the powder and product residue, then dry each one fully before it goes back.
The drawer bottom, once emptyTip the loose powder, hair, and grit into the bin, then wipe the bare bottom with a damp cloth and dry it, reaching into the back corners where the fine stuff settles.
The runners and the cabinet cavityVacuum the runner tracks and the empty cavity with the crevice tool, then wipe both glides with a barely-damp cloth and watch that the drawer slides flush as you seat it back.
The grooming tools and the razor boxWipe combs, brush handles, and the outside of the razor box with the soap-film cleaner, and use the detail brush on the teeth of combs and the crust around bottle caps before anything returns.
Heat tools and their cords, if stored hereUnplug first, then wipe the barrel and the body of any dryer or straightener with a dry cloth only, keeping all moisture away from the plug and vents. Wind the cord loosely and lay the tool in its own section rather than coiled tight against a divider.
What you clean with
Compact vacuum with attachmentsBathroom grime and soap-film cleanerSmall detail brush setColor-coded microfiber cloth setNeutral pH multi-surface cleanerPortable cleaning caddy
Inspect as you clean
  • A drawer bottom that has swelled or lifted at a corner, which means a product has been leaking underneath the divider.
  • Rust on a razor spring, a blade edge, or a hinge inside the box, the mark of damp being shut in.
  • A hairline split in a plastic divider or insert that will keep shedding sharp flakes.
  • A runner that grinds or sticks, or a mounting screw that has backed out of the cabinet side.
  • Soft black mould in a back corner where wet hair and cap drips collect.
The call that matters here
The expensive shade you never wear

There is a palette or a bottle in there that cost more than you like to remember, and you have used it twice. Keeping it is not thrift, it is paying storage on a decision that already went wrong, and you skip past it on every Sort pass because binning it means admitting the money is gone. The money is gone either way. The rule: if it is open and past its window, or you reached for something else the last three times, it leaves today. If it is unopened and still in date, it gets one season, and you write that season on a strip of tape stuck to the base so the clock is visible instead of imaginary.

Watch for
  • Fall, cut, or crushBare cartridges and blade refills loose among the brushes. A hand going in for a comb without looking meets the blade first.
  • Poison, choke, or stranglePolish remover, small scissors, and loose vitamins in a low drawer a child can pull open. Move liquids and pills to an upper drawer or fit a catch.
Leave behind

Each drawer is divided by routine, every category carries a written count on its label, and nothing stays past its date.

Trigger: Laundry day. As you carry towels in, open the top drawer and pull anything empty.

See Chapter 14, visual controls.

Under-Sink Cabinet

60-90 min, most of it in Straighten, fitting bins around the pipework

To store cleaning supplies and backstock around the plumbing without ever hiding a leak.

Done looks like

Two pull-out bins clear of the P-trap, a pale tray on the cabinet floor with nothing stacked on it, one open bottle per job, and both shut-off valves in plain sight when the doors swing open.

  • SortTake it all out and line it up on the floor by job: glass, tub and tile, toilet, drain, polish. You will find three half-full bottles of the same thing and one you cannot identify, and the one you cannot identify goes.
  • StraightenBuild the bins around the P-trap instead of fighting it, keeping the joint and the valves visible. Paper goods and bulk toiletries take the side with headroom, and sprays sit at the front where you can read a label without kneeling.
  • ShineWipe the cabinet floor and look hard for a dark ring or a soft patch in the board, which is what a slow leak looks like before it looks like water. Wipe the neck of every bottle before it goes back so the pale tray never turns sticky.
  • SafetyNever store bleach where it can tip into an ammonia-based cleaner, and never decant anything into a drink bottle. This cabinet sits at toddler height, so if small children come through the house, fit a magnetic catch or move the chemistry above the sink line.
  • StandardizeOne open container per job, backstock capped at two. Label the front edge of each bin with the job rather than the brand, so the bowl cleaner cannot quietly end up among the glass sprays.
  • SustainWhen you refill the hand soap at the basin, crouch and run your eyes over the cabinet floor and the trap joint before you close the doors. That is the entire leak check.
How to clean it

This is the one cabinet you clean partly to read the plumbing. Empty it, wipe the floor board, and look hard at what the pipes have been doing while the doors were shut.

Surface or areaMethod
The P-trap and the pipe jointsWipe the trap and each joint with a dry cloth, then run a clean dry finger around the underside of every connection, since a wet finger tells you about a seep before a puddle ever will.
The shut-off valvesWipe the valve body and the stem with a barely-damp cloth, then take the detail brush to the threads and the handle where grime cakes, so each valve stays clean and easy to read at a glance.
The pull-out binsSlide the bins right out, tip any grit, and wash them in the sink with the soap-film cleaner, drying them before they slide back clear of the trap.
The pale drip tray on the cabinet floorLift the tray out and rinse it, and wipe the neck and cap of every bottle before it returns so the tray never turns sticky and keeps showing a fresh drip against the pale surface.
The cabinet floor board and back wallWipe the bare floor board and the back and side walls with a neutral pH cloth, working into the corners and along the wall behind the trap where damp gathers first.
The door interiors and hingesWipe the inside of both doors and the hinges, and clear any splash line along the bottom door edge with the detail brush.
What you clean with
Neutral pH multi-surface cleanerBathroom grime and soap-film cleanerSmall detail brush setColor-coded microfiber cloth setCompact vacuum with attachmentsPortable cleaning caddy
Inspect as you clean
  • A dark ring or a soft, spongy patch in the floor board, which is a slow leak showing itself before it shows as water.
  • A green or white crust building on a pipe joint or a valve stem, the fingerprint of a mineral seep.
  • A drip tray or bin base that has warped or cupped, meaning liquid has been pooling under it.
  • Rust bleeding from a can base or a bracket, and mould blooming in the back corner.
  • A valve that will not turn, or a supply line that feels stiff and cracked where it bends.
The call that matters here
The pipe you cannot see

You have packed this cabinet wall to wall because it is the only storage in the room, and the P-trap is now buried behind a tower of paper towels. Here is the arithmetic you have been avoiding: a joint that weeps unnoticed for months turns a cheap washer into a rotted cabinet base and a floor that has to come up. The rule: the trap joint and both shut-off valves stay visible whenever the doors are open, and nothing sits on the tray. If that means the backstock will not fit, the backstock leaves this room, not the sightline. Spare shampoo and toilet rolls can live in a linen cupboard. A leak cannot live anywhere else.

Watch for
  • Poison, choke, or strangleBleach, drain opener, and bowl cleaner sitting at a crawling child's eye level behind one unlatched door. Fit a catch or move them above the sink line.
  • Water and electricityA drip at the trap creeping toward a socket or a plugged-in nightlight on the wall behind the cabinet. “Find it, and fix it now.”
  • Fall, cut, or crushA heavy bulk jug stacked on top of a pull-out bin, ready to come down on your hand when you slide the bin out. Heavy goes on the cabinet floor beside the tray, never above it.
Leave behind

The P-trap and both shut-off valves stay in plain sight, one open bottle per job, backstock capped at two.

Trigger: Every hand soap refill. Crouch, check the cabinet floor, then close the doors.

See Chapter 21, the hazard hunt.

Medicine Cabinet or Wall Storage

45-75 min, most of it in Sort, reading labels and dates one box at a time

To hold the medicine and first aid you would need tonight, and nothing you stopped needing years ago.

Done looks like

Three labeled bins, pain relief, stomach and cold, first aid, each holding only in-date items. Prescriptions grouped by the name on the label, the first aid bin small enough to carry one-handed to whoever is bleeding, and the whole cabinet above a child's reach with a catch on it if children are in the house.

  • SortRead every label and every date, including the small print on the boxes you kept because the box was still good. Anything expired, anything unlabeled, and anything prescribed for a condition nobody here has now goes straight into a bag for the pharmacy take-back.
  • StraightenGroup by the reason you come to this cabinet: headache, stomach, cut, cold, eyes. Put the two things you reach for at two in the morning on the shelf you can find without turning the light on.
  • ShineTake the shelves out and wash off the sticky rings left by cough syrup and the dust of old blister foil. Dry them all the way through before they go back, because a damp shelf is what turns tablets chalky in here.
  • SafetyBathroom humidity breaks tablets and capsules down faster than a bedroom shelf does, so move anything you take daily out of this room entirely. Whatever stays gets a child-secure catch, and nothing ever goes down the toilet.
  • StandardizeBins are labeled by symptom rather than brand, so a guest or a sitter can find what they need without asking which box the cold tablets are in. The first aid bin holds plasters, tape, gauze, and one pair of scissors, and it never becomes a home for spare vitamins.
  • SustainEvery time you come home from the pharmacy, the new box goes into its bin and you read the dates of everything already in that bin before you shut the door. The take-back bag then rides back with you on the next trip.
How to clean it

Take the shelves out, wash off the sticky rings, and dry everything through before it goes back, because damp is what turns tablets chalky in this room. Clean it in a light bright enough to read every label as you go.

Surface or areaMethod
The mirrored door, inside and outWipe the outer mirror with glass cleaner sprayed onto the cloth, buffing dry, then do the inside face where toothpaste mist and steam settle into a haze.
The door hinges and magnetic catchWipe the hinges and the catch with a barely-damp cloth and lift any grime out of the hinge knuckle with the detail brush so the door still shuts cleanly.
The shelves, removedTake each shelf out and wash off the sticky rings left by cough syrup and the dust of old blister foil in warm water, then dry each one all the way through before it returns.
The shelf clips, pin holes, and back wallWork the detail brush around the shelf clips and into the pin holes, then wipe the back and side walls of the cabinet, reaching the top corner where dust and damp meet.
The sorting binsEmpty the pain relief, stomach and cold, and first aid bins and wipe each one out with a neutral pH cloth, drying before the in-date items go back grouped as they were.
The bottle bases and packsWipe the base of every bottle and tin and the outside of the first aid box so nothing carries its own sticky ring back onto a clean shelf.
What you clean with
Neutral pH multi-surface cleanerStreak-free glass and mirror cleanerSmall detail brush setColor-coded microfiber cloth setCompact vacuum with attachmentsPortable cleaning caddy
Inspect as you clean
  • Rust freckling on the metal shelf clips, the hinges, or a steel cabinet back, the tell that humidity is sitting in here.
  • Black mildew spotting in a corner seam or along the underside of a shelf.
  • Condensation or a cloudy bloom trapped behind the mirror, a sign of moisture getting behind the glass.
  • A shelf that has bowed, or a shelf clip pulling loose from its pin hole under the weight.
  • A hairline crack in a glass shelf, or a door catch that no longer holds the door shut.
The call that matters here
The leftover prescription

There is a half-course of antibiotics in here, or a bottle of strong painkillers from a procedure two years ago, and you keep it because it might come in useful and because handing it back feels like waste. It is the single most common thing in this cabinet that should not be in it. The rule: a medicine belongs to a condition someone in this house has now, prescribed to that person, in date, with the label intact. Everything else goes in the take-back bag today, including the one you are quietly telling yourself is still fine. Leftover painkillers sitting behind a mirror are the most likely thing in this room to seriously hurt a visitor, and nothing about not wasting them outweighs that.

Watch for
  • Poison, choke, or strangleLoose tablets, gummy vitamins, and pushed-through blister packs at a height a child reaches by standing on a closed toilet lid. Measure the reach from what they can climb, not from the floor.
  • Fall, cut, or crushA glass peroxide bottle or a heavy first aid tin balanced on the top shelf above a hard basin. Heavy and breakable belongs at eye level, not overhead.
Leave behind

Only in-date, correctly labeled, currently needed medicine lives here, grouped by symptom and kept above child reach.

Trigger: Every trip home from the pharmacy. The new box goes in, the dates in that bin get read, and the take-back bag goes back with you next time.

See Chapter 22, prevent and prepare.

Shower or Tub

30-45 min, most of it waiting on the cleaner to work, so run the counter or the toilet meanwhile

To keep only the products actually in use within reach, on surfaces that dry out between showers.

Done looks like

One shampoo, one conditioner, one wash, and one bar per person in the caddy. A squeegee on its hook inside the enclosure, grip strips or a mat with real suction on the floor, and no dark line where the wall meets the tray.

  • SortLine every bottle along the tub edge and shake each one. The ones you cannot remember using this month, the conditioner that did nothing, and the hotel bottles all leave, and the nearly-empty ones get finished this week or poured together.
  • StraightenGive each person their own shelf or corner of the caddy instead of one mixed crowd, and hang the squeegee where a wet hand meets it without leaning out over the tub wall. Bottles go cap-down only if they truly seal, otherwise they ring the shelf.
  • ShineSpray the walls and the tray, then step out and do the counter or the toilet while the cleaner works. Come back, wipe, rinse, squeegee glass and tile from the top down, and leave the door standing open.
  • SafetyWet glaze under a film of soap is the most slippery surface in the house, so the mat or the grip strips are not optional, and a grab bar is a morning's work to fit. Razors go on a high hook, never on the tub edge where a small hand reaches for balance.
  • StandardizeHold the caddy to a bottle limit: a new bottle comes in only on the day an empty goes out. The squeegee has one hook and hangs on it wet, every time.
  • SustainLast person out squeegees the glass and the walls, props the door open, and leaves the fan running. It happens while you are already wet and standing there.
How to clean it

Spray the walls and tray and let the cleaner do the standing about while you clean the rest of the room. Come back, work top down, squeegee the glass, and leave the door open so it dries out. Start the mould early, because it needs the dwell time.

Surface or areaMethod
The showerhead and armWipe the head and arm, and where scale has closed the holes, work bathroom grime and soap-film cleaner in with the detail brush, let it dwell, then run the water hot to clear the jets.
The walls, tile, and glass screen or curtain linerSpray the whole wall surface and screen with the soap-film cleaner and leave it to work while you step out. Return, wipe from the top down, rinse, then squeegee the glass and tile so nothing dries into a film. A fabric liner goes through the washing machine instead.
The taps, valve trim, and diverterScrub the handles, the trim plate, and the diverter with the detail brush to lift limescale from the crevices, rinse, and buff the metal dry.
The caddy and the bottles in itLift the caddy off, rinse the shelves, and wipe the base of every bottle and bar dish where a soft pink film and gum collect underneath.
The caulk seam, grout lines, and cornersTreat the dark line where the wall meets the tray and the grout lines with the soap-film cleaner, give it real dwell time, then work the seams with the detail brush and rinse thoroughly.
The tub or tray floor and the non-slip mat or stripsLift the mat, wash both faces and the tray floor under it with the floor-material-compatible cleaner, rinse, and stand the mat on edge to drain rather than leaving it flat and wet.
The drain, its cover, and the squeegeeLift the drain cover, clear the hair caught below it, and wipe the cover clean. Rinse the squeegee blade and hang it back on its hook inside the enclosure to dry.
What you clean with
Bathroom grime and soap-film cleanerFloor-material-compatible cleanerColor-coded microfiber cloth setSmall detail brush setCompact vacuum with attachmentsPortable cleaning caddy
Inspect as you clean
  • Soft black mould in the caulk seam, or a pink film returning along the bottom band faster each time, a sign moisture is not clearing.
  • A grout line gone crumbly or missing, or a tile that sounds hollow when tapped, meaning water can get behind it.
  • A grab bar, towel bar, or caddy fixing that gives or shifts when you lean on it during cleaning.
  • A drain running slower each week, showing a blockage building in the trap.
  • Rust weeping from a screw or the frame, and a screen seal that has hardened and split.
The call that matters here
The bottle you are keeping out of guilt

There are four or five bottles in there you do not use. Some cost too much to admit, some were gifts, and some hold an inch you cannot bring yourself to pour away. Meanwhile the caddy never dries out, the shelf grows a ring, and you shift eight bottles to clean a space that should hold four. The rule: a product is in use if you touched it in the last two weeks of showers. If you did not, it leaves the enclosure today. The inch in the bottom is not saved money, it is what keeps mold on your shelf, so use it this week or pour it out and recycle the bottle. Gifts get no exemption for being gifts.

Watch for
  • FallSoap film on wet glaze, worst at the moment you step over a high tub wall. Grip strips or a mat that truly grips, and a grab bar if anyone in the house is unsteady.
  • Fall, cut, or crushA razor resting on the tub edge at exactly the height a child grabs to steady themselves. High hook, out of reach, every single time.
Leave behind

One of each product in use per person, no empties in the caddy, squeegee on its hook, floor grip intact.

Trigger: Whoever showers last squeegees the walls and props the door open before drying off.

See Chapter 24, the cleaning schedule.

Toilet Area

15-30 min, most of it in Shine, and much of that is the bowl cleaner doing its work

To keep the smallest and dirtiest patch of the room clean and stocked without letting it contaminate anything else.

Done looks like

Two reserve rolls within reach from a seated position and the rest of the pack in the cupboard. The brush in a holder that has been emptied and dried, a lined bin with a lid, and a completely bare cistern lid.

  • SortClear the cistern top down to the porcelain: the candle, the spare hand cream, the phone charger, the stack of reading. Everything stored up there has been sitting inside the fine spray a flush throws, and most of it does not belong in this room at all.
  • StraightenTwo reserve rolls go in a holder you can reach sitting down, and the rest of the pack goes to the linen cupboard. The brush and the bin each get a marked spot on the floor so a mop has a clean run around the pedestal.
  • ShineTreat the bowl and let it sit while you do the rest of the room, then work the outside in order: handle, lid, seat top, seat underside, rim, pedestal, and the floor around the base where drips land. One cloth, one color, this job only.
  • SafetyBowl cleaner is the harshest chemistry in the house and must never meet bleach in the same bowl. Store it high or in the under-sink cabinet behind a catch, not on the floor beside the pedestal where a child stands to reach the flush.
  • StandardizeThe toilet cloth and the toilet gloves are a color no other job in the house uses, and they hang apart from every other cloth. Two reserve rolls is the floor; dropping to one is the restock signal.
  • SustainWhoever hangs the last roll carries two more in from the cupboard on their next trip through. The empty holder does the remembering, not you.
How to clean it

Treat the bowl and let it sit while you do everything else, then work the outside in strict order from cleanest to dirtiest: handle, lid, seat top, seat underside, rim, pedestal, floor. One cloth, one colour, this job only.

Surface or areaMethod
The cistern lid and tank exteriorWipe the bare cistern lid and the tank sides with a neutral pH cloth, working around the fixings and down the back where dust cakes against the wall.
The flush handle or buttonWipe the handle or button first, while your cloth is at its cleanest, since it is touched by every hand and rarely wiped.
The lid, top and undersideWipe the lid top, then lift it and do the underside, where splash dries unseen.
The seat, top and underside, and hinge capsWipe the seat top, then the underside and the ring, and flip open the hinge caps to clear the grime that hides beneath them with the detail brush.
The bowl rim, under-rim, and water lineAfter its dwell, work the applied bowl cleaner under the rim and around the water line with the brush, scrub down into the trap, and flush. Keep the room ventilated and keep this the only product in the bowl, never mixed with another.
The pedestal base and the floor behind itWipe the pedestal from the tank down to the floor, then clean the floor around the base and the strip behind the pedestal, where drips and dust collect and where the smell in a bathroom actually starts.
The brush, its holder, and the binRinse the brush under the flush, stand it to drip, then tip and wipe out the holder so no liquid pools in the bottom. Wipe the bin lid and replace the liner.
What you clean with
Bathroom grime and soap-film cleanerColor-coded microfiber cloth setSmall detail brush setNeutral pH multi-surface cleanerCompact vacuum with attachmentsPortable cleaning caddy
Inspect as you clean
  • A damp ring or discoloured grout that keeps returning at the base of the pedestal, a sign the seal below is failing and water is seeping.
  • A hairline crack in the tank or the bowl porcelain, and any weep from the tank fittings.
  • Condensation sweating steadily down the tank onto the floor, which reads on the boards as a slow damp patch.
  • Rust on the seat hinge bolts, or a seat that has worked loose and rocks.
  • Heavy mineral scale rebuilding under the rim, or a stain trail on the floor behind that points to a slow overflow.
The call that matters here
What you keep within splash range

The cistern lid is the most tempting shelf in the room, and every flush throws a fine mist across it. The decision you have been avoiding is not whether that is true, it is which of your things you are willing to keep there anyway. Settle it with one line: nothing that touches your face, your mouth, or your food goes within arm's reach of the bowl. Toothbrushes, lens cases, hand cream, drinking glasses, and open candles all move out. What may stay is the brush, the lidded bin, the reserve rolls, and cleaner stored high. If that leaves the lid bare, that is the right answer, and it has the useful side effect that the lid finally gets wiped.

Watch for
  • Poison, choke, or strangleBowl cleaner standing at floor level beside the pedestal, and the chance of it meeting bleach in the same bowl. One product per bowl, stored high.
  • Water and electricityA phone charger run up to the cistern top so you can sit and scroll. A cord, a water tank, and a hard tile floor is a combination worth ending today.
Leave behind

The cistern lid stays bare, the toilet cloth is a color used for nothing else, and two reserve rolls is the minimum on hand.

Trigger: When you hang the last roll, two more come in from the cupboard on your next trip past.

Linen and Towel Storage

30-45 min, most of it in Sort, ranking towels honestly

To hold enough clean towels for the way this house actually does laundry, and no more than the shelf can safely carry.

Done looks like

Three bath towels per person, one in use, one in the wash, one folded on the shelf. Each guest set nested in its own pillowcase, hand towels and washcloths in separate labeled columns, and a spare bath mat rolled at the end of the shelf.

  • SortTake every towel out and rank them by which one your hand actually goes to after a shower. The thin, the stiff, the shedders, and the odd single from a lost set all leave, with the best of the rejects becoming cleaning rags and the rest going to a shelter that takes textiles.
  • StraightenFold everything to one width so the stack squares up, then keep bath towels, hand towels, and washcloths in their own columns rather than one mixed pile. Each guest set goes inside a pillowcase so the whole set lifts off the shelf in one grab.
  • ShineWipe the shelves and press the back wall for damp, because a towel put away not quite dry will scent the whole cupboard within days. Anything sour goes back through a hot wash with no softener before it returns.
  • SafetyA deep stack of linens on a shelf held by four small brackets is a load nobody ever weighed. Check the brackets and the wall fixings, and keep the heavy bath towels on the lower shelves so nothing tall comes down on your head.
  • StandardizeThe count is the standard: three bath towels per person, folded to one width, one label per column. When a column is full and clean laundry arrives, the most worn towel in that column becomes a rag that day.
  • SustainWhen you fold laundry, clean towels go to the bottom of their column so the oldest keeps coming to your hand, and you check the column count in the same motion. Nothing goes onto the shelf damp, ever.
How to clean it

Clear the shelves, wipe them, and press the back wall for damp, because a towel put away not quite dry scents the whole cupboard within days. Anything sour goes back through a hot wash before it returns.

Surface or areaMethod
The top of the unit and the topmost shelfDust the very top of the unit with the microfiber, where this room's dust actually settles, then clear and wipe the top shelf before working down.
The shelf boards, cleared one at a timeTake each shelf empty, vacuum the crumbs and lint from the corners and front lip, then wipe with a neutral pH cloth and dry the board fully before anything goes back.
The back wall of the cupboardWipe the back wall and press the flat of your hand along it while the shelves are bare, since damp there is what the towels have been drinking.
The shelf brackets and wall fixingsWipe the brackets and clear dust from the fixings with the detail brush, noticing whether each one still carries its load without movement as you clean around it.
The door interior, edges, and any ventsWipe the inside of the door and its edges, and run the detail brush through any louvre or vent slots so air keeps moving through the cupboard.
The cupboard floor and skirtingVacuum the floor of the cupboard and along the inner skirting, then wipe it, since this is where fibre dust and stray damp gather low down.
The towels and linens themselvesAs you restack, smell each folded set; a musty note means it went away damp, so send it back through a hot wash with no softener before it returns to the shelf.
What you clean with
Compact vacuum with attachmentsNeutral pH multi-surface cleanerColor-coded microfiber cloth setSmall detail brush setBathroom grime and soap-film cleanerPortable cleaning caddy
Inspect as you clean
  • A damp or discoloured patch on the back wall of the cupboard, meaning moisture is coming through from behind.
  • A bracket pulling away from the wall, or a fixing plug backing out under the weight of a deep linen stack.
  • A shelf board that has bowed in the middle, telling you the load is more than it was fixed for.
  • Mildew spotting on the underside of a shelf, and a musty smell that lingers even after the towels come out.
  • A soft or crumbling board edge low down, the kind of quiet damp that draws silverfish.
The call that matters here
The good towels you never use

You have a set kept back for guests who visit twice a year, and three thin gray towels you reach for every single day because using the good ones feels like spending them. That is a shelf running backwards, and it has been running backwards for years. The rule: rank every towel you own, use the top of that ranking daily, and let the worn ones go now instead of after another year of being the ones you actually touch. Keep exactly one guest set, in a pillowcase, and put everything else into rotation. A towel saved for a better occasion is a towel wearing out in a cupboard while you dry yourself on a rag.

Watch for
  • Fall, cut, or crushAn overloaded shelf on light brackets, or a stack tall enough to topple when you pull a towel from the middle. Check the fixings and keep the heavy bath towels low.
  • FallA step stool on a wet bathroom floor to reach the top shelf. Move the everyday linens down to shelves you can reach flat-footed.
Leave behind

Three bath towels per person, folded to one width, columns labeled by type, and nothing put away damp.

Trigger: Every laundry fold. Clean towels to the bottom of the column, and in a full column the most worn one becomes a rag.

See Chapter 27, the friendly audit.

Room 12 of 20

Guest Bathroom

5 zones

The guest bathroom is the one room a visitor spends time in alone, with the door shut and nothing to look at but your standards. It is also the room nobody in the house uses, which is its own problem: a dry drain trap, a towel that smells of the closet, a bottle of shampoo left behind two summers ago. Start at the vanity counter, because it is the first thing anyone sees and the fastest thing to put right.

Where to startDo the vanity counter first and the linen shelf last. The counter takes one trip and changes how the whole room reads; the linen shelf needs a wash cycle to finish, so put the stored towels in the machine before you touch anything else and let them run while you work the rest of the room.
Cleaning noteA bathroom used twice a year still grows a ring at the bowl's water line and still loses the water sitting in its drain traps, which is where the faint sewer smell comes from. Once a month, pour a mug of water down the sink, the tub, and the floor drain, and give the bowl a treatment with full dwell time whether anyone has used it or not.
The trapThis room quietly becomes the retirement home for products the household rejected: the conditioner nobody liked, the soap that smells wrong, the towels that went thin. Nothing gets demoted into the guest bathroom. If it is not good enough for you, it is not good enough to offer a visitor, and it leaves the house instead.

Guest Vanity Counter

15-30 min, short and even, with the longest stretch in Sort deciding what belongs to the household rather than the guest

It gives a visitor somewhere to wash their hands and set a phone down, with nothing of yours in the way.

Done looks like

A soap pump above half full, one folded hand towel on the ring where you can see it from the doorway, a box of tissues at the back corner, and enough bare counter to set down a phone and a pair of glasses. No toothbrush cup holding your family's toothbrushes, no hair clips behind the tap, no dried soap ring under the dispenser.

  • SortTake everything off the counter and put back only what a visitor would use: soap, tissues, hand towel. The travel conditioner, the spare contact lens case, and the razor that ended up here because the main bathroom ran out of room all go back where they belong or into the trash.
  • StraightenSet the soap on the side your hand reaches without crossing over the basin, tissues at the back corner clear of splash, and the hand towel on a ring or hook visible from the door. A guest should never have to open a cabinet to find a towel.
  • ShineWipe the counter, then the tap and the drain lip where toothpaste film and hard water scale build up, including the collar at the base of the tap where a chalky ring hides. Do the mirror last, top to bottom, so drips land on surfaces you have not finished yet.
  • SafetyWater and electricity: a shaver or hair dryer left plugged in beside a full basin is a live cord within splash reach, so unplug it and put it away rather than coiling it on the counter. Swap any glass tumbler or glass trinket dish for ceramic, because glass on a wet counter slides and it breaks onto a tile floor where feet are bare.
  • StandardizeThree things live on this counter permanently: soap, tissues, hand towel. Photograph that exact arrangement and tape the picture inside the vanity door, so anyone in the house can reset the counter without asking you which three.
  • SustainThe moment someone confirms they are coming over, walk in, top up the soap, and hang a fresh hand towel. Soap level and towel are the two things a visitor registers, and both are handled before you have put the phone down.
How to clean it

Work top to bottom so every drip lands on something you have not finished yet, and save the mirror for last. The tap collar and the drain lip are where hard water hides, and they are the first things a guest looks at up close.

Surface or areaMethod
the light fixture and the mirror above the basinDo this pair last, and dust before you wet anything. Wipe the cool bulbs and the fixture housing with a dry microfiber cloth so the dust does not smear down onto glass you are about to clean. Then spray streak-free glass cleaner onto a cloth, never straight onto the mirror where it runs behind the frame, and wipe from the top down in one direction. Buff dry with a second dry cloth so no film shows by the vanity light.
the wall and backsplash behind the tapMist the splash zone with the bathroom soap-film cleaner and wait about a minute for the toothpaste flecks and dried spray to soften, then wipe down and off to one side. Dry the wall so no cleaner sits in the caulk line at the counter.
the tap, the collar at its base, and the drain lipGive the tap and the chalky ring at its foot a coat of soap-film cleaner and let it dwell while you do the basin, because scale lets go far more easily wet than scrubbed dry. Work the collar with a folded cloth corner, wipe the drain lip and the overflow slot, then rinse with a damp cloth and dry to a shine so no new water spots set.
the basin bowl and the overflow holeWipe the bowl with soap-film cleaner from the back rim down toward the drain, pushing the residue ahead of you rather than round in circles. Clear the overflow hole with the cloth corner, then rinse and dry the bowl so it looks unused.
the counter surface and the caulk seam at its back edgeClear the three items off first. Wipe the whole counter with neutral pH cleaner, working from the wall edge forward to the front lip so grit travels toward you and off, and run a cloth along the caulk seam where the counter meets the wall. Dry the surface completely, since a wet counter is exactly where a guest sets a phone.
the soap pump, the tissue box, and the towel ringLift each item rather than wiping around it. Wipe the sticky neck and nozzle of the soap pump and the ring under its base, top up the pump if it has dropped below half, dust the tissue box, and wipe the towel ring and its mount before you rehang a fresh folded towel.
the cabinet front, the underside lip of the counter, and the toe kick floorWipe the cabinet doors and handles with neutral pH cleaner, then run the cloth along the underside front lip of the counter where forearms rest and hands grip. Finish low: vacuum the toe kick and the floor line at the base, then wipe the floor edge so no hair or dust rings the cabinet foot.
What you clean with
Bathroom grime and soap-film cleanerNeutral pH multi-surface cleanerStreak-free glass and mirror cleaner (not in this zone's list, and the mirror needs it)Color-coded microfiber cloth setCompact vacuum with attachmentsPortable cleaning caddy
Inspect as you clean
  • Chalky scale that comes straight back within a day or two of wiping the tap collar, which tells you how hard the water running through this room is.
  • A wobble or a little give in the tap as you wipe its base, or a damp patch that keeps reappearing at its foot.
  • The caulk seam where the counter meets the basin or the wall going dark, soft, or lifting at one end.
  • A water mark or a swollen edge on the underside lip of the counter, the kind of sign that something above it drips.
The call that matters here
Whose bathroom is this really

If a child or a housemate genuinely brushes their teeth in here, the hotel counter standard loses every time and you end up resetting it twice a week. The decision you have been dodging is which room this actually is. Pick out loud: either it is a used bathroom, in which case one named person gets a labeled caddy or drawer and the counter holds their two items alongside the guest three, or it is a guest bathroom, in which case that toothbrush moves today and nothing personal comes back. Holding a hotel standard against a resident who lives here is not a discipline problem, it is a standard aimed at the wrong room.

Watch for
  • Water and electricityAnything with a cord left plugged in next to a basin that a guest will fill. Unplug and store after every use, and keep the cord off the counter entirely.
  • Fall, cut, or crushGlass tumblers and glass decorative dishes on a wet counter. When one goes over in a small tiled room, someone is standing there barefoot.
Leave behind

The counter holds three things: soap, tissues, hand towel. Anything a visitor brings leaves with them.

Trigger: When you put away fresh towels after laundry, swap the guest hand towel in the same motion.

See Chapter 25, the ideal-state photo.

Guest Vanity Storage

30-45 min, most of it in Sort, working through years of hotel miniatures and half-used bottles

It holds the things a guest might otherwise have to ask you for, plus the cleaning kit that keeps this room ready.

Done looks like

Open the door and see two lift-out bins and nothing loose: one labeled for guest supplies holding a new toothbrush, a wrapped bar of soap, and four unopened travel toiletries in a small dish, and one labeled for cleaning holding bowl cleaner, glass spray, and cloths standing upright on a shallow tray. Four rolls of toilet paper stacked in plain view. The cabinet base is bare enough that a single drip from the trap would show.

  • SortPull everything out and be honest about the hotel miniatures: the shampoos from three trips, the lotion nobody opened, the free conditioner samples. Keep the unopened ones a guest would actually use, throw out the started ones, and send household backstock that drifted in here back to the bathroom it came from.
  • StraightenTwo bins, not five. Guest supplies in one, cleaning products in the other, both light enough to lift out with one hand so the cabinet base stays visible. Toilet paper stacks on its own, where it can be seen the second the door opens, because a guest who is hunting for it will not come and ask.
  • ShineEmpty the cabinet completely and wipe the base, looking for dark rings or softened board that mean the trap has been weeping. Wipe the neck and cap of every bottle, since dried cleaner running down the side is what makes a cabinet sticky.
  • SafetyPoison, choke, or strangle: bowl cleaner and bleach sitting under this sink are at exactly the height of a visiting toddler who has been sent in to wash their hands alone. Either lift the cleaning bin to a high shelf elsewhere or fit the cabinet with a latch, and never let bleach share a bin with an acid limescale or bowl cleaner, because a loose cap on both is all it takes for them to meet.
  • StandardizeWrite the two bin labels by hand in plain words a guest can read, Guest Supplies and Cleaning, and draw a line on the shelf at the toilet paper minimum. When the stack falls below the line it goes on the shopping list, no counting required.
  • SustainWhen you restock your own bathroom after a shop, open this cabinet on the same trip and build the toilet paper stack back up to the line.
How to clean it

Empty the cabinet right out and wipe the bare base, because this is the one surface in the room that tells you whether the trap has been quietly weeping. A cabinet goes sticky from the necks of its own bottles, so clean those too.

Surface or areaMethod
the back wall and the top interior corners of the cabinetWith everything out, vacuum the corners and the top interior first to lift dust and any hair, then wipe the back wall and corners with a cloth dampened with neutral pH cleaner. Dry it, because a cabinet that goes back damp is a cabinet that grows a smell.
the shelf base and the two lift-out binsWipe the shelf base from the back forward with neutral pH cleaner and dry it fully so a single fresh drip would still show. Wipe each lift-out bin inside and out, wipe the base of each, and let them dry before they go back.
the board around the trap and the pipesWipe the board around the trap and the pipe run gently with a dry cloth first so you can read the surface, then a damp cloth. Use the soap-film cleaner only on any scale crusted at a fitting, and dry the area so you are not adding water of your own to a spot you are trying to read.
the bottle necks, caps, and the bin basesWipe the neck and cap of every bottle where dried product has run down the side, then stand each back on its shallow tray. Wiping the necks is what keeps the whole cabinet from turning tacky. Wipe the bin bases where a bottle has left a sticky ring.
the door interior, the hinges, and the handleWipe the inside face of the door and the handle with neutral pH cleaner, and run the cloth over the hinges where dust and damp collect. Dry the handle so it is not left greasy for a guest.
the toe kick and the floor line at the cabinet baseVacuum along the toe kick and the floor line, then wipe the floor edge under the cabinet lip where dust and any escaped drip settle out of sight.
What you clean with
Bathroom grime and soap-film cleanerNeutral pH multi-surface cleaner (not in this zone's list, but the sealed cabinet interior needs it)Color-coded microfiber cloth set (not in this zone's list, and you cannot wipe the cabinet down without cloths)Compact vacuum with attachmentsPortable cleaning caddy
Inspect as you clean
  • A dark ring or a soft, spongy patch on the cabinet base under the trap, the mark of a slow weep nobody has seen because nobody opens this door.
  • Rust or a bead of moisture sitting on a pipe fitting or the shutoff valve.
  • A musty smell or a bloom of mildew in the back corner where the air never moves.
  • A bin base that lifts out damp, or a hinge screw that has worked loose in the door.
The call that matters here
The bag of hotel miniatures

The collection of tiny shampoos is the hardest thing in this cabinet, because throwing out free soap feels like waste and keeping it feels like hospitality, so it has sat there for years doing neither. Settle it with arithmetic: a guest staying two nights uses one shampoo, one conditioner, one soap, and one lotion. So keep four, unopened, the four with the least battered labels, and put them in a small dish where they read as an offering rather than a hoard. The rest go today. If binning them is genuinely beyond you, they go into a shelter donation box this week, in the car, not back on the shelf.

Watch for
  • Poison, choke, or strangleBowl cleaner, bleach, and drain opener stored under the sink of the bathroom where a visiting child gets sent alone. Latch the door or move the chemicals out of the room.
  • FallA slow weep from the trap that nobody sees because nobody opens this cabinet. It reaches the tile as a thin invisible film long before it shows as a puddle.
Leave behind

Two labeled lift-out bins and a visible toilet paper stack. Nothing loose on the cabinet base, so a leak has somewhere obvious to show.

Trigger: You already open this cabinet to get the brush for the weekly bowl clean, so run a hand across the base while you are down there.

Shower or Tub

30-45 min, Shine takes the largest share, and most of that is waiting for cleaner to work on the grout rather than scrubbing it

It has to be ready for someone to shower in tonight, even though nobody has used it since spring.

Done looks like

Three bottles in the caddy, all recognizable and more than a third full, a curtain liner or glass screen with no pink film along the bottom edge, a bath mat folded over the tub rim rather than lying wet on the floor, and grout that is the same color in the corners as it is in the middle. A non-slip surface underfoot inside the tub.

  • SortTake out every bottle and turn it over. This is where the household's rejected products retire: the shampoo nobody liked, the body wash that smells wrong, the conditioner welded to the shelf. Keep one shampoo, one conditioner, one body wash, all readable and more than a third full. The rest go, along with the old razor and the disintegrating loofah.
  • StraightenHang the caddy or fit a corner shelf so nothing stands directly on the tub floor collecting water underneath it. A bar of soap gets a draining dish, never a puddle. Put a hook inside the door at a height someone can reach with wet hands and no glasses on.
  • ShineStart with the mold, because it needs time you should not spend standing there: spray the grout lines and the bottom band of the curtain liner, go clean the rest of the room, then come back and rinse. A fabric liner goes in the washing machine rather than getting scrubbed by hand.
  • SafetyFall: your guest is stepping into an unfamiliar tub, probably at night, on a surface they cannot judge the grip of. Put a non-slip mat or adhesive strips on the tub floor and make sure there is one thing solidly anchored to grab, because a towel bar screwed into drywall will tear straight out the moment it takes a person's weight.
  • StandardizeThree bottles, one soap, one mat, one anchored grab point, and no personal items. If the shower has stood unused for a month, run it hot for a minute before a guest arrives, which clears the stale water sitting in the line and proves the drain is still running freely.
  • SustainWhoever used it last squeegees the glass or shakes out the liner and drapes the bath mat over the tub rim. A liner left bunched against the tile is exactly where the pink film starts again.
How to clean it

Start the mold clock before anything else: spray the grout lines and the bottom band of the liner, then walk away and clean the rest of the room while it works. Come back to rinse, and finish at the tub floor and the apron where the last of the water sits.

Surface or areaMethod
the ceiling above the shower and the exhaust vent grilleLook up first. Wipe the ceiling above the shower and the vent grille with a dry cloth to clear dust, then a cloth dampened with soap-film cleaner over any speckled spots. This is the highest and most humid surface, so it comes before you wet the walls below it.
the grout lines and the silicone cornersThis is your dwell job. Spray the soap-film cleaner along the grout lines and into the silicone corners, and if the film there is stubborn switch to a dedicated mildew remover. Leave it to sit while you clean the rest of the room, then come back, work it with a stiff grout brush, and rinse thoroughly so nothing is left biting the grout.
the shower head, the arm, and the wall tileWipe the head and arm with soap-film cleaner to shift the scale, then work the wall tile top to bottom, spraying and wiping in bands so the runoff always lands on tile you have not cleaned yet. Rinse the whole wall down at the end.
the bottle caddy or shelf and the three bottlesLift the caddy or clear the shelf, wipe the sticky rings under each bottle, and wipe the caddy or shelf itself. Wipe each bottle neck, and stand them back once they are dry.
the curtain liner or the glass screen and its trackSend a fabric liner straight to the washing machine rather than scrubbing it by hand. A plastic liner or a glass screen gets soap-film cleaner over its face and along the bottom edge where the pink film starts, then a rinse. Wipe the rod or the screen track, and wipe the screen dry so it does not spot.
the taps, the spout, the diverter, and the tub surroundWipe the surround from the back wall forward, then bring the taps, spout, and diverter up with soap-film cleaner, working the scale off the handles and the base of each fitting. Rinse and buff the metal dry so it shines.
the tub floor, the non-slip surface, the drain, and the rimClean the tub floor last, working the non-slip surface with soap-film cleaner and the grout brush since the grip texture holds grime. Clear the drain and stopper, rinse the whole tub from the rim down, then wipe the rim and hang the bath mat over it to dry rather than leaving it on the floor.
