Six letters a year against 500 envelopes is a lifetime supply and then some. Cap it at one open plus one spare of anything you use monthly, and a single year of use for everything else. The surplus goes to a school, a charity shop, or a neighbour this week. Not to the top shelf, and not to a box in the garage.

Watch for
  • FallReaching the top shelf from a rolling office chair. The chair moves under you the moment you shift your weight, so bring a step stool into this room and keep it here.
  • Fall, cut, or crushReams of paper and boxes of envelopes stored above shoulder height, where a slipping box lands on your head and shoulders while your arms are up.
Leave behind

One open plus one spare of anything used monthly, with min and max written on a card at the front of every shelf.

Trigger: When you open the last pack of something.

See Chapter 14, visual controls.

Room 15 of 20

Garage

7 zones

The garage takes what every other room in the house evicts, and because the door stays shut most of the week, nothing ever forces a verdict on any of it. That is why your best chisel and a box you have not opened since you moved in can sit on the same shelf. Start at the workbench, because until one solid surface is clear, everything you pick up just goes back down somewhere else.

Where to startStart with the workbench, not the pile inside the door. A cleared bench top gives you somewhere to lay out the contents of a bin while you judge them, and it is the one part of the garage that pays you back the same afternoon. The slab comes last, once the wall rails and the overhead racks have taken what belongs on them.
Cleaning noteDegrease the slab properly once, with degreaser, a stiff brush, and cat litter or absorbent left on the old stains overnight. After that a sweep with the push broom holds it, and a pale floor turns every new drip into information: you will see the gearbox weeping or the water heater starting to go the week it starts, not the month it fails.
The trapThe garage is where the rest of the house sends things it could not decide about. The chair with the wobbly leg, the sack for the charity shop that has stood by the side door since spring, the printer you replaced: none of those are being stored, they are being postponed. Anything carried in with no rail, shelf, or bin already waiting for it gets its verdict the same day, or it quietly becomes part of the floor.

Primary Workbench

60-90 min, most of it in Sort, because the bench top is buried under other zones' overflow

The one surface in the house where things get fixed, glued, cut, and assembled.

Done looks like

A bare bench top holding only the vise and the task light, one tray with the current job in it, clamps racked on the rail, and swept concrete visible under the bench.

  • SortClear the top down to bare wood. Every screw living in a jar lid, every glue bottle with a plugged nozzle, and every offcut you saved because the grain looked too good to bin gets a verdict before anything is allowed back up.
  • StraightenGive the vise its own corner and keep a full arm's length of open top in front of it, because that is the space a long board swings through. Clamps go on the rail within reach and fasteners in the drawer directly beneath. The post that comes in, the half-tin of paint, and the parcel waiting to go back are not bench items and never were.
  • ShineVacuum sawdust and metal filings out of the bench dog holes and the seam where the top meets the back wall, then wipe the vise screw down and put a drop of oil on it.
  • SafetyCheck the bench outlet and the task light cord for nicks, and confirm you can plug the sander in without a lead crossing the strip of floor you stand on. Chisels and utility blades never rest edge-up on the top, where a hand sweeps sawdust without looking.
  • StandardizeTape a photo of the cleared bench, vise and task light and nothing else, on the wall directly behind it at eye height. When you look up and see a paint tin or a coil of wire that the photo does not show, that is the next thing to leave.
  • SustainA project is not over when the glue sets. It is over when the clamps are back on the rail, the offcuts are in the scrap bin, and the top is swept, and all of that happens before the task light goes off.
How to clean it

The workbench cleans top to bottom, back wall to slab. Clear the dust and filings out of the dog holes and the seam behind the top first, then oil the vise so the one surface you fix things on is genuinely ready to work.

Surface or areaMethod
The pegboard and painted wall behind the benchStart high and at the back. Run the vacuum brush along the top edge of the pegboard and down its face to pull off the film of sawdust before it drifts onto the clean top, then wipe the wall above with a barely damp microfibre cloth.
The task light and its cord runDust the shade and arm with a dry cloth, then draw a just-damp cloth down the cord to lift the greasy grit that collects on it. Leave the cloth dry around the fitting itself, never wet.
The bench outlet and power stripWipe the faceplate and the power strip housing with a dry cloth only, and work a dry brush around the socket surrounds to lift the sawdust that packs in. Keep every drop of moisture away from the sockets themselves.
The bench top and the seam where it meets the back wallVacuum the whole top first, working the crevice tool hard along the back seam where filings pack in. Then wipe the sealed top with the neutral pH cleaner, following the grain, and buff it dry so nothing is left to rust a tool set down on it.
The bench dog holesPush the vacuum crevice nozzle into each dog hole to draw out sawdust and metal filings, or run a detail brush round the rim, since a packed hole stops a dog seating flush.
The viseWipe the jaws and body clean of grit, wind the vise fully open and closed, then lay a drop of light machine oil along the screw and slide and work it back and forth to carry the oil in.
The clamp rail and the current-job trayWipe each clamp bar as it goes back on the rail, then tip the job tray out, clear its corners, and wipe it before the parts return.
The bench drawer and the concrete under the benchPull the drawer right out, tip the grit from its back corners, and wipe both runners. Finish by sweeping and vacuuming the slab under the bench until bare concrete shows.
What you clean with
Compact vacuum with attachmentsColor-coded microfiber cloth setNeutral pH multi-surface cleanerSmall detail brush setPortable cleaning caddyLight machine oil
Inspect as you clean
  • Rust blooming on the vise screw, the bench dogs, or any bare steel on the top, the first sign the bench is holding damp.
  • A hairline crack or a soft spongy patch opening in the bench top or a leg joint, and any wobble when you lean your weight on it.
  • A white salt bloom or a spreading damp patch on the slab under the bench, which says water is tracking in under the wall.
  • Scorching, browning, or a warm smell at the bench outlet or along the light cord as your hand passes over it.
The call that matters here
The project that has been there since spring

Name the half-finished thing sitting on your bench right now. It gets one of three verdicts today: a specific weekend written on it to finish it, a breakdown that returns the timber, hardware, and hinges to stock, or the skip. The rule that settles it is that anything which has sat untouched through one full season is not a project any more, it is furniture with your intentions stacked on top. Do not solve this by lifting it onto a shelf. That only moves the decision somewhere darker and costs you the bench in the meantime, which is the one surface the whole garage depends on.

Watch for
  • Fall, cut, or crushChisels, utility knives, and handsaws loose in the bench drawer or lying edge-up on the top where you sweep with a bare hand.
  • Burn or fireRags soaked with oil, stain, or solvent left balled in an open bin under the bench. They can heat on their own with no flame anywhere near them.
  • Water and electricityThe bench outlet or a power strip sitting low on a slab that runs wet when a car comes in from the rain.
Leave behind

The bench ends every session bare except the vise and the task light.

Trigger: When you pull the last plug out of the bench outlet, that is the signal to sweep. The plug leaving the wall, not the clock on your phone.

See Chapter 25, the ideal-state photo.

Hand Tool Wall and Cabinets

60-90 min, most of it in Sort and Standardize, since tracing outlines takes longer than hanging tools

Keeps the tools you reach for weekly visible, findable, and provably all present.

Done looks like

Every hand tool hanging on its own drawn outline with no blank silhouettes, one hammer and one adjustable wrench at chest height nearest the door, saw teeth sheathed, and the cabinet latched.

  • SortPull every hammer, screwdriver, plier, and wrench onto one sheet on the floor and count the duplicates honestly. Pliers rusted shut, wrenches with rounded jaws, and drivers with chewed tips are scrap metal, not spares, and keeping them means hunting past them forever.
  • StraightenHang by how often your hand reaches, not by tool family. Tape measure, utility knife, and the number two driver go at chest height nearest the door, because those three come off the wall for jobs that take under a minute. Pipe wrenches and socket sets sit lower and further along.
  • ShineWipe each tool with an oiled rag as it goes back up, and wipe the pegboard behind it. Rust starts at the handle end, where hands leave salt.
  • SafetyHandsaws and chisels get a sheath or a blade guard before they touch the wall. Seat every pegboard hook properly, because a heavy wrench knocked off a shallow hook lands on a foot, and put a latch on the low cabinet if children ever come out here.
  • StandardizeTrace the silhouette of every tool onto the board in marker or paint. An empty outline tells you the tenon saw is missing without you having to remember that you own one.
  • SustainThe driver goes back on its outline before you fold the step ladder away, not at the weekend. A tool that spends the night on the bench top is one the wall has already started to lose.
How to clean it

Every tool comes off the wall, gets an oiled wipe, and goes back on its own drawn outline. Rust starts at the handle end where your hands leave salt, so that is exactly where you look as you clean.

Surface or areaMethod
The top of the wall cabinetReach the top of the cabinet first with a dry cloth or the vacuum brush, because the dust that settles up there drops onto everything below the moment you disturb it.
The pegboard behind the toolsLift each tool off, vacuum the pegboard face and the holes, then wipe the board with a barely damp cloth and let it dry fully before anything goes back.
The pegboard hooksWipe each hook and reseat it firmly in its holes as the tool returns, clearing any grit from the hole that would stop it sitting tight.
Each hand toolWipe every tool down with an oiled rag as it goes back onto its outline, working the handle end where skin salt sits, then set it so its drawn silhouette shows at a glance if it is missing.
The cabinet doors, handle, and latchClean the door faces and the handle with the neutral pH cleaner, and work a detail brush into the latch and hinge seams where oily dust builds up.
The cabinet interior shelvesEmpty each shelf, vacuum the corners, wipe the boards, and dry them before the tools go back sorted.
The floor and skirting under the cabinetVacuum along the skirting under the cabinet where filings and small offcuts gather out of sight.
What you clean with
Compact vacuum with attachmentsSmall detail brush setColor-coded microfiber cloth setNeutral pH multi-surface cleanerPortable cleaning caddyLight machine oil
Inspect as you clean
  • Rust starting at the handle end of chisels, pliers, and wrenches, where skin salt sits, before it creeps up the blade.
  • A pegboard hook that has worked loose in its holes, or a hole worn wide enough that the hook rocks.
  • A soft, swollen, or stained patch on the pegboard, which means damp is coming through the wall behind it.
  • A cabinet latch that no longer catches on the first push, and any droppings or gnaw marks behind the cabinet.
The call that matters here
The third hammer

You almost certainly own three claw hammers, four adjustable wrenches, and a fistful of Phillips drivers, and the reason you have never thinned them is that each one, held on its own, still works. Working is not the test. The test is which one your hand actually goes to. Keep that one, plus exactly one deliberate spare parked where a second person would need it, the kitchen drawer or the boot of the car. Every remaining duplicate goes today to a friend, a tool library, or the metal skip. A hammer you keep because it still works and never choose is taking the hook that would have made the hammer you love findable in one glance from the door.

Watch for
  • Fall, cut, or crushUnsheathed saw teeth and chisel edges hung at hand height, and heavy wrenches on shallow hooks directly above where you stand to work.
  • Poison, choke, or strangleAn unlatched low cabinet holding loose box-cutter blades, screws, and drill bits within a toddler's reach.
Leave behind

Every hand tool has a drawn outline, and a blank outline means you find that tool before you close up.

Trigger: When you carry the toolbox back in from a job, its contents go on the wall before you set the box down on the floor.

See Chapter 14, visual controls.

Power Tool and Battery Zone

60-90 min, most of it in Sort, since every pack has to be tested in its own tool

Holds the powered tools, their packs, and their chargers so a tool is always ready to run and nothing is left charging unattended.

Done looks like

One shelf per battery system with its charger mounted at the front edge and no pack sitting on it, every drill and saw in its case or on a labelled hook with guards down, and bits, blades, and sanding discs in the shallow drawer underneath.

  • SortLine every pack up and run it in the tool it belongs to until you know which ones still hold a charge. Packs that die in a minute, chargers for the drill you sold, and the sander with the taped cord all leave today.
  • StraightenGroup by battery system rather than by task, so one shelf carries the tools, packs, and charger of a single platform and you stop carrying a green pack to a red tool. Blades, drill bits, and sanding discs go in a shallow drawer directly beneath the tools that take them.
  • ShineBrush or blow the motor vents on each drill and saw clear of packed dust, and clean the battery contacts and the charger terminals. A vent clogged with plaster dust runs a motor hot enough to shorten its life.
  • SafetyPacks come off the charger once they are full and sit at partial charge on a shelf well clear of the fuel can and the rag bin. Confirm every blade guard springs back freely on its own, and retire any tool with a cracked case rather than pushing it behind the others.
  • StandardizeOne shelf label per battery system, charger mounted at the front edge, so a pack left charging shows as a lump on the shelf edge you can see from the doorway without walking in.
  • SustainPulling a full pack off the charger is part of finishing the job, the same as putting the drill back in its case. Nothing sleeps on a charger, whatever the manual claims about smart cut-off.
How to clean it

Dust is what turns a warm tool into a hot one, so the motor vents come first. Work back to front along each shelf, clearing vents and contacts before you touch the slab underneath.

Surface or areaMethod
The charger units at the shelf front edgeWipe each charger case, then clear the terminal slots with a dry brush or a puff of air, never a damp cloth, so nothing conductive is left in the contacts.
The shelf boards under each battery systemLift the packs and cases off, vacuum the board, and wipe it with the neutral pH cleaner, drying it fully before anything sits back down.
Each drill, driver, and saw bodyBrush or blow the motor vents clear of packed dust first, working from the intake side through, then wipe down the housing and grip. A vent left clogged runs the motor hot enough to shorten its life.
The blade guards and bladesHold the retracting guard open, wipe the blade and the inside of the guard clean of resin and dust, then let it spring back and clean the outside.
The battery packs and their contactsWipe each pack down and clean the contact rails with a dry brush or a touch of electrical contact cleaner, lifting any grit or corrosion from between the terminals.
The shallow bits, blades, and discs drawer underneathTip the drawer out, vacuum the filings from the corners and the runner track, and wipe it before the bits and discs go back sorted.
The slab under the shelfSweep and vacuum the floor under the shelf, especially the strip where meltwater runs off a car onto the concrete.
What you clean with
Compact vacuum with attachmentsColor-coded microfiber cloth setNeutral pH multi-surface cleanerPortable cleaning caddySoft brush or compressed airElectrical contact cleaner
Inspect as you clean
  • A battery pack that feels warm, looks swollen, or has a split case, spotted while you wipe it down.
  • Green or white corrosion crusting on the battery contacts or the charger terminals.
  • A cracked or heat-discoloured tool housing, or a charger case that has browned around its own vents.
  • Damp or rust spreading on the slab under a charger base, where water off the car reaches the power.
The call that matters here
The tool whose batteries no longer exist

Somewhere on that shelf is a good tool on a dead platform: the drill from the brand you left behind, one tired pack, and a charger nobody makes any more. You keep it because the tool itself is fine, and it is. Decide this with money rather than loyalty. Price one replacement pack today. If a pack costs more than half what a current equivalent tool costs, or you cannot buy one at all, the tool is finished, so sell it with its charger while it still means something to somebody, or take it to electrical recycling. Running two battery systems is a cost you can defend. Running three means a hunt through three shelves every time you want to put a hole in a wall.

Watch for
  • Burn or fireLithium packs left charging overnight on a wooden shelf, or stored alongside the petrol can and the rag bin.
  • Fall, cut, or crushA circular saw stored with its guard wedged open, or a table saw blade left raised where a hand reaches across the shelf for a pack.
  • Water and electricityCharger bases sitting on the slab where meltwater runs off the car, and extension leads daisy-chained to feed the whole shelf.
Leave behind

No battery sleeps on a charger, and no tool goes back on the shelf with its guard held open.

Trigger: When you lock the side door for the night, your route takes you past the charger shelf, and anything still sitting on a charger comes off on the way.

See Chapter 22, prevent and prepare.

Automotive Care Zone

45-75 min, most of it in Sort, reading labels bottle by bottle

Keeps the fluids, cables, and cleaning kit for the cars you actually own upright, labelled, and separate from everything the household touches.

Done looks like

Every fluid bottle standing upright on a clean drip tray with its opened date written on the shoulder, jumper cables and tyre gauge together in one bag by the door, rags in a closed metal can, and not a single bottle for a car nobody here drives.

  • SortLine every bottle up and read the label out loud. Oil in a weight your current engine does not take, coolant for the van you sold, and the half bottle whose label has soaked away into unreadable grey all come out of the row.
  • StraightenFluids stand upright on a shallow drip tray at waist height, where a weeping cap shows as a ring rather than soaking into a shelf. Jumper cables, tyre gauge, and lug wrench live together in one bag right by the door, because you go for those with a dead engine, in the dark, in a hurry.
  • ShineWipe every bottle shoulder and check the tray for the ring that says something is seeping. Wash the wash mitts and microfibre towels on their own, never with house laundry, so grit and old wax stay out of the good towels.
  • SafetyPetrol and any can that has ever held it stays out of the house envelope, capped tight, and never on a shelf near the water heater's pilot flame. Polish and oil rags go into a metal can with a lid, not the plastic bin.
  • StandardizeLabel the tray shelf with exactly what belongs on it, and mark every bottle with the month you opened it. Brake fluid pulls water out of the air and coolant goes off once the seal is broken, and the date is the only way you will ever know which is which.
  • SustainYou already lift the bonnet to check the oil. While it is up, look along the tray for a fresh ring, and an empty bottle goes straight into the bin instead of back into the row.
How to clean it

Wipe every bottle shoulder and read the drip tray as you go, because the tray tells you what is seeping before the floor does. Car cloths and mitts wash on their own, never with the house laundry.

Surface or areaMethod
The shelf of fluid bottlesLift each bottle, wipe its shoulder and cap where oil and coolant weep down, and stand it back squarely on the tray with its dated shoulder facing out.
The shelf board under the bottles and trayWith the bottles and tray lifted off, wipe the shelf board itself with the neutral pH cleaner, lifting the greasy film and any fluid that has crept under the tray unseen.
The drip tray under the bottlesSlide the tray out, wipe it clean, and read what is on it as you go, since a fresh ring shows exactly which bottle has started to seep.
The jumper cables and tyre gauge bagWipe the cable clamps and coil the leads back into their bag, then wipe the gauge before it goes in beside them.
The metal rag canWipe the outside of the can and check the lid closes flat, leaving the oily rags sealed inside it rather than loose on the shelf.
The wash mitts and microfibre towelsWash the mitts and drying towels on their own load, never with house laundry, so old wax and grit stay out of clean fabric, and dry them fully before they go back.
The slab where the car parks and dripsSweep the bay, then work oil-absorbent granules into any fresh spots on the concrete, leave them to draw the oil up, and sweep them off before wiping the area.
What you clean with
Neutral pH multi-surface cleanerColor-coded microfiber cloth setCompact vacuum with attachmentsPortable cleaning caddyOil-absorbent granules
Inspect as you clean
  • A fresh ring on the drip tray under one bottle, the sign it has started to seep.
  • A sweet-smelling puddle anywhere on the slab, which is leaking antifreeze and deadly to a pet or a child.
  • An oil stain spreading on the concrete under where the car sits, telling you the car itself is dropping fluid.
  • A bottle whose printed label has washed or faded illegible, and any rust weeping from the base of the rag can.
The call that matters here
The fluids for the car you no longer own

The hardest bottles here are the two thirds full ones. They cost real money, they have not gone off, and they fit nothing on your driveway. The rule is that every bottle must match a vehicle parked at this address, and no match means no keep. Offer them this week to the neighbour who drives that model, and take whatever is unclaimed to your council's household hazardous waste collection, never down a drain. Then write the opened date on every bottle from today onward, because the only reason this shelf grew this deep is that nobody could tell which ones were still good, so nothing was ever thrown out.

Watch for
  • Burn or firePetrol vapour pooling low near the water heater pilot, and polish or oil rags heating in a closed plastic bin.
  • Poison, choke, or strangleAntifreeze tastes sweet, and a spilled puddle or a decanted unlabelled bottle will kill a dog or a curious child.
  • Fall, cut, or crushA trolley jack stored still loaded, or a car held up on a jack with no axle stands under the sills.
Leave behind

Every fluid stands upright on the tray, wears an opened date, and belongs to a car on this driveway.

Trigger: When the bonnet is already up for the oil check, that is when the tray gets its look.

See Chapter 21, the hazard hunt.

Sports and Recreation Zone

60-90 min, most of it in Sort, and the slow part is matching each item to a person who still plays

Holds the gear for the sports this household currently plays, with the in-season kit easiest to grab.

Done looks like

Bikes on wall hooks with bare floor beneath them, one labelled bin per activity, this season's kit at the front of the shelf, and every helmet in the garage belonging to a head that still wears it.

  • SortCarry every helmet, ball, racket, and pair of cleats out onto the driveway and match each one to a person who plays that sport now. Cleats a size too small and the paddle from that one river trip do not come back through the door.
  • StraightenBikes hang on wall hooks nearest the door so nobody unhooks three to get one out on a Saturday morning. Balls go in a tall bin or a bungee-front rack rather than loose on the floor where they roll under the car, and this season's kit sits at the front of the shelf with the off-season behind it.
  • ShineAir out and wipe down helmet liners and shin pads, hose the mud off cleats and dry them right through before they go back, and leave the cooler propped open with a rag of baking soda inside so it does not come out smelling of last summer.
  • SafetyA helmet that has taken one real impact is finished even if the shell looks perfect, and so is one past the date moulded inside it. Overhead bike hooks must be rated and driven into studs, never drywall anchors, and never hung where a car door swings open into a hanging wheel.
  • StandardizeOne labelled bin per activity, and the label carries the sport plus the name of the person it belongs to. Gear that fits no named bin has no owner in this house and does not stay in it.
  • SustainThe front and back of the shelf trade places the first weekend you turn the heating on, and again the first weekend you leave the windows open all day.
How to clean it

Air out the liners, hose the mud off the cleats, and never pack a single thing away damp. Work down from the bikes on the wall to the bare floor beneath them.

Surface or areaMethod
The bikes on the wall hooksWipe the frames down and lift the chain grit off with a rag, working top to bottom, then wipe the hooks themselves before the wheels go back up.
The shelf of this season's kitClear the shelf, vacuum it, wipe it with the neutral pH cleaner, and set the in-season gear back at the front where it is grabbed.
The helmet liners and shin padsTake the liners out where they lift, air them, and wipe the pads and shells with a cloth and a mild gear disinfectant, then let them dry all the way through before they go in the bin.
The muddy cleats and bootsHose the caked mud off the cleats outdoors, knock the studs clean, and dry them right through before they return to the shelf.
The cooler interiorWash the cooler inside, dry it, then leave it propped open with a rag of baking soda inside so it does not come back smelling of last summer.
The labelled activity binsTip each bin out, wipe the grit and sand from the corners, and refit the lid before it goes back on the shelf.
The floor under the bikesVacuum the bare floor under the hanging bikes where dried mud and grass drop off the tyres.
What you clean with
Portable cleaning caddyCompact vacuum with attachmentsColor-coded microfiber cloth setNeutral pH multi-surface cleanerGarden hoseBaking sodaMild gear disinfectant
Inspect as you clean
  • A hairline crack in a helmet shell or a crushed, compressed liner, the sign that helmet is finished even if the shell looks fine.
  • A bike wall hook working loose in its anchor, or the wood around it starting to split.
  • Rust or seized links on a bike chain, and frayed or torn straps on helmets and pads.
  • Mildew growing in the cooler seal or in the corners of a bin that went away damp.
The call that matters here
The gear from the sport you stopped playing

Skis, golf clubs, a good tent, the road bike. Each was expensive, each still works, and each stands for a version of your week that has not actually happened in years. That is what makes this the slowest shelf in the garage, and no amount of restacking will settle it. The rule is two full seasons of the right weather with no use, which means you are not on a break, you have stopped. Sell it while the bindings and the groupset are still current, or hand it to someone just starting out, which is a better ending than a decade under a dust sheet. The one exception worth honouring is a named date: if you can finish the sentence "I will use this on ___", keep it and write that date on the bin. If you cannot finish the sentence, it goes this month.

Watch for
  • Fall, cut, or crushA bike coming off an overhead hook onto a parked car roof or a person underneath, and skis stored edge-out at head height.
  • Burn or fireCamp stove canisters and lantern fuel stored in among the sports bins instead of outside with the other flammables.
  • FallStanding on the cooler rather than a ladder to reach over the ball rack for the tent bag.
Leave behind

Every bin carries a sport and a person's name, and this season's kit sits at the front of the shelf.

Trigger: The first weekend you turn the heating on, the front and back of the shelf trade places.

Lawn and Garden Tool Zone

60-90 min, most of it in Sort and Safety, because the chemical shelf needs its own careful pass

Stores the yard tools and outdoor supplies so long handles stay off the floor and garden chemicals stay latched away from everything else.

Done looks like

Every long-handled tool hanging head-up on the rail with nothing leaning in a corner, the hose coiled on its reel beside the door it goes out of, and all garden chemicals in their original labelled containers behind a latch above head height.

  • SortOut go the rake with three tines left, the hose with the split you have meant to patch for two summers, and the cracked nursery pots kept for seedlings you never started. Count your spades honestly, because most garages hold four and dig with one.
  • StraightenLong handles hang head-up on a rail so no fork stands loose in a corner waiting to be trodden on. The hose lives on a reel beside the door it goes out of, not coiled on the slab. Fertiliser, weed killer, and seed share one shelf with a lip on it, well away from the cooler and anything else that touches food or skin.
  • ShineScrape caked soil off the spade and hoe blades, wipe them with an oily rag, and run a file along the spade edge so it bites next time. Empty and rinse the spreader hopper, because leftover fertiliser eats the metal from the inside out over a winter.
  • SafetyGarden chemicals stay in their original labelled containers on a latched shelf above child and pet height, never decanted into a drink bottle. String trimmers and hedge shears go up with guards fitted and cutting ends pointing away from the aisle you walk down.
  • StandardizeA painted or taped strip on the wall behind each long handle, so a bare strip is a question you answer before you close the door. One sign on the chemical shelf reading "garden chemicals only, nothing else on this shelf."
  • SustainThe mower needs to cool before it goes away anyway, and that is the window when the spade gets scraped, wiped, and hung back on its strip, instead of being found rusted solid next spring.
How to clean it

Scrape, oil, and sharpen the blades as they come off the rail, and rinse the spreader before old feed eats it from the inside out. The chemicals stay latched and above head height the whole time you work around them.

Surface or areaMethod
The tool rail and the long-handled toolsWipe the rail and the wall behind it high up first, then lift each spade, rake, and hoe down to clean it, so nothing rests handle-first in a corner.
The spade and hoe bladesScrape the caked soil off each blade with a wire brush or scraper, wipe the metal with an oiled rag, and run a flat file along the cutting edge so it bites next time before it goes back head-up on the rail.
The hose and its reelWipe the reel, coil the hose back onto it evenly, and rinse any dried soil out of the nozzle.
The spreader hopperEmpty the hopper completely, rinse it out with the hose, and dry it, because leftover fertiliser eats the metal from the inside over a winter.
The string trimmer and hedge shearsWipe the cutting heads down, clear grass from the guards, and hang them with the cutting ends pointing away from the aisle you walk.
The latched chemical shelfWipe the shelf front and the outside of each container while the shelf stays latched and above head height, reading every label as you go.
The aisle floorSweep the aisle, then mop it with the floor-material-compatible cleaner so soil and spilled feed do not get tracked around the garage.
What you clean with
Compact vacuum with attachmentsNeutral pH multi-surface cleanerFloor-material-compatible cleanerColor-coded microfiber cloth setWire brush or scraperLight machine oilFlat fileGarden hose
Inspect as you clean
  • Rust pitting along a spade or shear edge, and a handle splitting or working loose where the head meets the shaft.
  • Fertiliser crust eating the inside of the spreader hopper into flaking metal.
  • A garden chemical container that is weeping, crusted, or swollen on the latched shelf, flagged without decanting or handling it further.
  • A perished hose washer or a split fitting dripping at the reel.
The call that matters here
The half-used chemicals

The shelf holds a bag of feed with the top rolled over, a jug of concentrate that moved here from your last house, and something in an unlabelled sprayer that you are fairly confident is weed killer. Start with the unlabelled one, because it is the easy call: you cannot safely use what you cannot name, so it goes to hazardous waste this month without being opened again. For the rest, apply one season of intent. If you will not genuinely use it before the next growing season ends, you are storing a poison above your children's heads for no reason. Take it to a hazardous waste day, or offer it to a neighbour in its own labelled container. Never pour any of it down a drain, into a gutter, or onto the ground behind the shed.

Watch for
  • Poison, choke, or strangleConcentrated weed killer decanted into an unlabelled bottle, and slug pellets or granular feed left where a dog can reach and will eat them.
  • Fall, cut, or crushRakes and forks standing loose in a corner waiting to be stepped on or to topple handle-first, and unguarded hedge shear blades on an open shelf.
  • Burn or fireThe mower's petrol can sharing a shelf with fertiliser, which is an oxidiser and should never sit next to fuel.
Leave behind

Every long tool hangs on its taped strip, and nothing but garden chemicals lives on the latched shelf.

Trigger: While the mower is cooling after a cut, that is when the blades get scraped, wiped, and hung.

See Chapter 21, the hazard hunt.

Bulk and Overhead Storage

60-90 min, most of it in Sort, and every bin has to come down to the floor to be judged

Holds the things you genuinely need once or twice a year, high up and out of the way, without becoming the place decisions go to hide.

Done looks like

Every overhead bin labelled in letters you can read standing on the slab, a last-opened month and year on the end of each one, the heaviest bins on the lowest rack, nothing overhanging the parking bay, and a small map taped beside the light switch.

  • SortBring the bins down to the floor before you judge them, because nobody sorts honestly from the third step of a ladder with one arm through a lid. Start with the holiday bin that has not come down in three Decembers and the box marked "misc".
  • StraightenHeavy bins go on the lowest rack and light bulky things, folding chairs and the artificial tree, go highest. Group by the time of year they come down, so one December trip up the ladder brings the lights, the tree, and the wrapping bin down together.
  • ShineWipe the lids. The depth of dust on an overhead lid tells you exactly how many years it has been since anything came out of that bin. While you are up there, look at the rack underside and the ceiling above it for the brown stain that means the roof is letting water in.
  • SafetyNothing goes overhead that you cannot lift down with both hands on a stable ladder, and nothing you might need in the dark or in a hurry. Check that the rack bolts land in joists, not just in the plasterboard, and that loaded bins sit fully inside the rails rather than overhanging the roof of a parked car.
  • StandardizeEvery bin gets a large label you can read from the slab plus the month and year it was last opened. A map taped beside the light switch says which bay holds what, so nobody climbs a ladder simply to find out.
  • SustainThe moment a bin is on the floor with its lid off, write the new month and year on the end before it goes back up. A bin whose newest date is two Decembers old comes down and gets opened at the next clear-out rather than being lifted straight back.
How to clean it

The depth of dust on a lid tells you how long a bin has sat untouched. While you are up there, read the roof deck above the rack for the brown stain that means water is getting in.

Surface or areaMethod
The roof deck and ceiling above the rackFrom a stable step, wipe the underside of the roof deck and the ceiling above the rack and clear the cobwebs, so a new stain would show against clean board.
The rack frame and its undersideWipe the rails and the underside of the rack with a damp cloth, working the settled dust off the steel.
The rack shelf decks under the binsOnce a level is cleared, wipe the plywood or wire deck the bins sit on with a damp cloth, since grit ground under a base scratches the bin and hides damp.
The bin lidsDust each lid with an extendable duster or a cloth, noting the depth of dust as you go, since it tells you how many years since anything came out of that bin.
The bin exteriors and labelsWipe the bin sides and the label face so the letters read from the slab, and update the last-opened month if you have opened one.
The rack uprights and bolt platesWipe down the uprights and clear the grit from the bolt plates where the rack meets the wall and the ceiling.
The slab under the parking bay edgeSweep and vacuum the floor under the rack, especially the strip right at the edge of the parking bay.
What you clean with
Compact vacuum with attachmentsColor-coded microfiber cloth setNeutral pH multi-surface cleanerPortable cleaning caddyExtendable dusterStable step ladder
Inspect as you clean
  • A brown water stain spreading on the roof deck, the rack underside, or the ceiling, the sign the roof is letting water in.
  • A bin gone soft, stained, or damp at its base, and any musty smell when you lift a lid.
  • Rust, or a bend or sag, in a rack upright or a rail carrying the load.
  • A rack bolt that has drawn part-way out of its hole, or that lands in plasterboard rather than a joist, and rodent droppings sitting on a lid.
The call that matters here
The box you have never opened

There is at least one box up there that moved in with you and has not been opened since. You keep it because opening it costs an afternoon and might cost more than that. Here is what settles it. Bring it down, open it on the floor with three things beside you: a rubbish sack, a donation box, and one empty container. Whatever you truly want to keep has to fit inside that single container and has to have a living person's name attached to it. Anything you cannot name an owner for goes today. Do not tape it up and send it back overhead, because a box that goes back unopened has just bought itself another five years and taught the rack that this is allowed.

Watch for
  • FallWorking from a ladder rocking on a sloped slab, or coming down with a bin in both arms and no free hand for the stile.
  • Fall, cut, or crushAn overloaded rack, or bins stacked so they overhang the rails directly above where people get in and out of a car.
Leave behind

Every overhead bin is labelled to be read from the slab and carries the date it was last opened.

Trigger: When a bin comes down, the new date goes on the end of it before it goes back up.

See Chapter 24, the checklist, schedule, and name.

Room 16 of 20

Workshop

6 zones

This is the room 6S came from, and the only one in the house where a bad standard can cost you a finger rather than an afternoon. The workshop's mess is never really about storage; it is about three verdicts you keep postponing, on offcuts, on half-finished projects, and on paint. Start at the PPE station, because everything else in here involves a blade, a solvent, or a cloud of dust, and you want your glasses findable before you spend a Saturday handling any of it.

Where to startTake the Safety and PPE Station first even though it is the smallest zone in the room. It sets the terms for the other five: once the glasses hang by the door and the extinguisher gauge reads green, you can work through the blades and the solvents without improvising.
Cleaning noteVacuum, never sweep. A broom launches the finest sawdust back into the air, where it settles on wet finish and into your lungs, and the fines are the part that actually burns. Empty the dust extractor at the end of every session rather than the start of the next one.
The trapYou will sort the fasteners twice before you touch the offcut pile once, because tipping out a jar of screws feels like progress and putting good wood in the fire bucket feels like waste. The zones you keep skipping are the zones holding the room back.

Main Workbench

60-90 min, most of it in Sort, deciding what happens to the half-finished projects living on the bench

The one surface where a project actually gets built, cut, glued, and repaired.

Done looks like

A bare bench top except the vise and the task lamp, one project tray holding the current job's parts, and a clear run of bench in front of the vise long enough to lay a board across. Every chisel and square back on its traced outline, the dog holes empty, and no offcuts or glue squeeze-out under the bench.

  • SortClear the bench top and the shelf beneath it completely, then give every half-finished project a verdict: back on the bench with a date, or broken down into parts and stock. Snapped blades, glue bottles gone solid, and brackets you cannot identify leave now.
  • StraightenHang the tools your hand reaches for mid-cut, the marking knife, the combination square, the block plane, within arm's reach of the vise, and put everything you use once a project on the wall behind. Traced silhouettes mean a missing chisel shows up before you finish vacuuming.
  • ShineVacuum the bench top and the dog holes rather than brushing them out, then scrape dried glue drips off the surface before they telegraph into the next workpiece. Wipe the vise screw and give it a drop of oil.
  • SafetyCheck the vise jaws still bite a test piece without slipping, and move the lamp cord so it does not lie across the part of the bench a handsaw crosses. A chisel lying edge up in an open tray is what your hand finds when you reach across the bench without looking.
  • StandardizeTrace the outlines on the tool wall in permanent marker so a gap reads as a missing chisel, then take one photo of the cleared bench and tape it at eye level where you stand, because that photo is what bare means here.
  • SustainThe last part of every session is the bench clear down, done before you take your safety glasses off, because once the glasses come off you are finished for the day and you know it.
How to clean it

Clean the bench to read it. Vacuum the top and the dog holes instead of brushing dust into the next joint, scrape the glue drips before they telegraph into a workpiece, and finish at the vise with a wipe and one drop of oil.

Surface or areaMethod
The tool wall or pegboard behind the benchStart high and at the back. Brush the board and each hook downward, then run the vacuum crevice nozzle along the top of every tool outline where sawdust settles unseen, so it does not shower onto the bench the next time you knock the wall.
The task lamp, its head and armWith the bulb cool, wipe the shade, the head, and the arm joints with a barely damp microfiber cloth, because dust drawn to a warm shade bakes on and dulls the light you cut by. Keep the cloth off the bulb and switch.
The vise, its screw and slide waysVacuum the grit out of the jaws and the slide ways, wind the vise fully open and closed to clear the screw, then wipe the screw clean and work one drop of light machine oil along the thread.
The bench top and its dog holesVacuum the whole top and suck the dog holes out with the crevice nozzle rather than brushing, which only moves the grit around. Then scrape dried glue drips and squeeze-out flat with a plastic scraper before they press into your next board, and wipe the sealed top with neutral pH cleaner on a damp cloth.
The bench apron and front edgeWipe the front edge and apron with a cloth and neutral pH cleaner, where forearms rest and where the fine dust rolls off the top and clings.
The under-bench shelf and legsClear the offcuts and dropped hardware off the lower shelf, vacuum the shelf and around the leg feet, then wipe the shelf down with a damp cloth.
The floor in the standing zoneVacuum the shavings and offcuts from the strip of floor you stand on, working them out from under the bench, then damp-wipe a sealed floor with the floor-material-compatible cleaner.
What you clean with
Compact vacuum with attachmentsColor-coded microfiber cloth setNeutral pH multi-surface cleanerPortable cleaning caddyPlastic scraper or putty knifeLight machine oilFloor-material-compatible cleaner
Inspect as you clean
  • A wobble or a racking sway when you lean your weight on the bench, which points to a leg joint or a stretcher bolt working loose.
  • The lamp cord or a tool flex with a cracked, stiff, or frayed section where it bends over the bench edge.
  • Rust bloom on the vise screw, the bench dogs, or any bare steel fitting, a sign the shop is holding damp.
  • A scorch mark or a heat-glazed patch on the top that says a tool or a workpiece has been running hot on this spot.
The call that matters here
The project that has been on the bench since spring

You have a half-assembled something occupying your best surface, and you have not touched it in months because the next step is one you are unsure how to do. Here is the rule that settles it: say out loud what the next physical action is and what date this month you will do it. If you cannot name both in one breath, the project is finished. Take it apart, put the hardware back in the fastener drawers, put the stock back on the rack, and let the bench go back to being a bench. Keeping it assembled is not keeping the project alive, it is only keeping the surface unusable.

Watch for
  • Fall, cut, or crushChisels and marking knives lying edge up in an open tray, and a vise whose jaws have worn smooth enough to release a workpiece mid-cut.
  • Water and electricityThe task lamp or a tool cord running across the bench or down onto a damp concrete floor where you stand to work.
Leave behind

One project on the bench at a time, and the bench ends every session bare except the vise and the lamp.

Trigger: When you reach up to switch off the task lamp, the bench must already be clear, so the clear down happens before the light goes out.

See Chapter 25, the ideal-state photo.

Power Tool Storage

60-90 min, most of it in Sort and Safety, because every guard and every cord gets handled individually

Keep every corded and cordless tool guarded, complete with its own accessories, and ready to pick up and use.

Done looks like

Each tool in a labeled home with its guard closed and its blade wrench or chuck key attached to it, not floating in a drawer. Batteries sitting on a shelf off the chargers, no bare circular saw blade loose in a drawer, and no cord repaired with electrical tape anywhere in the zone.

  • SortLine up the duplicates and be honest about the second drill and the sander you replaced but kept. Battery packs that no longer hold a charge through one job are not spares, they are recycling, and taped cords are not tools.
  • StraightenStore each tool with the parts that make it usable: the circular saw with its rip fence and blade wrench, the router with its collet spanners and the bits it actually takes. Hunting for a collet spanner is how a router ends up used without one.
  • ShineBlow or brush the motor vents clear of packed sawdust, take the pitch off saw blades with resin remover so they stop burning cuts, and pull the clogged sanding pads.
  • SafetyWork the retracting guard on every saw with your thumb and confirm it snaps back on its own; a sticky guard is the injury waiting in this zone. Flex each cord at the strain relief where the sheath cracks first, and never leave a corded tool stored plugged in.
  • StandardizeLabel each tool's home on the wall or the shelf edge, and set one charge rule for the batteries: partial charge, off the charger when the job ends, resting on a shelf that is not wood.
  • SustainWhen a battery goes onto the charger at the end of a job, the tool it came off goes onto its hook in the same motion. The two actions travel together or neither happens.
How to clean it

The dust these tools breathe is the dust that shortens them. Blow the motor vents clear, take the pitch off the blades so they stop burning cuts, and pull the clogged sanding pads.

Surface or areaMethod
The shelf tops and the top of the tool cabinetWork from the top down. Vacuum the sawdust off the highest shelves and the cabinet top first, so nothing you disturb falls onto tools you have already cleaned.
The motor housings and cooling ventsWith each tool unplugged or its battery out, blow or brush the packed sawdust out of every motor vent and air intake, then wipe the housings with a barely damp cloth kept clear of the vents. Dust in the vents is what turns a warm motor into a hot one.
Saw blades and cuttersWipe the pitch and resin off each blade with a resin remover and a cloth so the teeth cut clean instead of burning, then stand them back guarded rather than loose.
Sander pads and dust portsPull the clogged sanding pads, clear the dust ports with the vacuum, and brush the accessory bins out.
The battery shelf and chargersVacuum the sawdust off the battery shelf and around the chargers, then wipe each contact face with a dry cloth so grit does not sit on a live terminal. Keep all moisture away from the contacts.
Tool cords and drawer interiorsWipe each cord down its full length with a dry or barely damp cloth as you coil it, and vacuum the grit and filings out of the drawers the accessories live in.
The cabinet base and the floorVacuum the cabinet floor and the strip in front of it, where the heaviest sawdust settles, then damp-wipe the sealed floor.
What you clean with
Compact vacuum with attachmentsColor-coded microfiber cloth setNeutral pH multi-surface cleanerPortable cleaning caddyResin and pitch removerCompressed air or a blower
Inspect as you clean
  • A battery pack warm to the touch, swollen, or with a split or bulging casing while it sits on the charger.
  • A brown scorch stain or a hot electrical smell around a motor vent.
  • Blade teeth chipped or missing, or a blue heat-discolored patch on the blade plate.
  • Rust on a saw table, a chuck, or an arbor, or a chuck that no longer grips a bit square.
The call that matters here
The expensive tool you never use

Somewhere in this zone is a tool that cost real money and has not cut anything in two years, and you keep it because selling it would be admitting the purchase was wrong. Two questions settle it. Has it done work in the last twenty-four months? And if the job came up tomorrow, would you happily hire one for a day instead? If the answer is no and yes, sell it now, while it still fetches something and while the batteries in it are still worth having. A tool losing value on your shelf is not an asset you are holding, it is a decision you are storing.

Watch for
  • Fall, cut, or crushA blade guard that has stopped springing shut on its own, and loose blades stored flat in a drawer where your hand goes in first.
  • Burn or fireLithium battery packs left on chargers indefinitely, especially sitting on a wooden shelf under a bench full of sawdust.
  • Water and electricityCracked cord sheathing at the strain relief, used on an unearthed outlet or standing on bare damp concrete.
Leave behind

Every tool rests guarded, unplugged, and stored with the accessories it needs to work.

Trigger: When the shop door gets locked at the end of a session, no charger is left running behind it.

Fastener and Hardware Zone

45-75 min, most of it in Straighten, since tipping out the jars is quick and labeling the drawers properly is not

Let you put your hand on the right screw straight away, so a project never stops for a hardware run.

Done looks like

One fastener type and size per compartment, with a sample screw glued to the outside of each drawer face so you match by eye rather than by reading. Deck screws separate from drywall screws, no coffee tin of mixed hardware anywhere on the shelf, and a marked minimum line inside the compartments you empty most.

  • SortEmpty the mixed jars onto a tray and keep only what you can name confidently by size and type. Rusted screws, bent nails, and the anchors that came free with a shelf you no longer own are not a stock of hardware.
  • StraightenSort by type first and size second, with the sizes you drive weekly at hand height and the specialty hinges and threaded rod up high. The sample fastener on the drawer face is the label that still works when your hands are dusty.
  • ShineVacuum the drawers of metal filings and grit, and look for rust bloom in the bottom corners; if you find it, the cabinet is against a cold outside wall and needs moving before you refill it.
  • SafetyA wall cabinet full of steel fasteners is far heavier than it looks, so fix it into studs rather than plasterboard anchors before you load it. Spilled screws on a concrete floor put you on your back holding something sharp.
  • StandardizeOne compartment per size, no exceptions and no shared drawers, with a minimum line marked inside so a low bin announces itself the moment you slide it open.
  • SustainThe handful of leftover fasteners in your pocket goes back into its compartment before the tools go back on the wall, or into the scrap metal tin if you cannot name it.
How to clean it

Vacuum the drawers out and read the corners while they are empty. Metal filings and grit are what jam the runners, and a bloom of rust in a bottom corner is the cabinet telling you where it sits.

Surface or areaMethod
The top of the small-parts cabinet and any shelf above itDust and vacuum the top of the unit first, since it gathers the fine grit that sifts down onto everything the moment you open a drawer.
The drawer faces and their glued sample screwsWipe each drawer face and the sample fastener glued to it with a barely damp cloth, so you can still match a screw by eye rather than by reading a smeared label.
The drawer and compartment interiorsLift out any loose bins, vacuum the metal filings and grit from each compartment, and run the small detail brush into the corners where washers and swarf pack down.
The bins, jars, and their lidsWipe the outside of each bin and jar and clean any thread or lid gone stiff with grime, then check the level against the minimum line before it goes back.
The mounting rail and the wall behind the cabinetWipe the wall and the mounting rail behind the cabinet while you can reach them, and clear the dust off the fixings.
The floor beneath the cabinetVacuum the floor under and in front of the cabinet, where a single dropped screw waits for a bare foot.
What you clean with
Compact vacuum with attachmentsSmall detail brush setColor-coded microfiber cloth setNeutral pH multi-surface cleanerPortable cleaning caddy
Inspect as you clean
  • Rust bloom in the bottom corners of a drawer, which says the cabinet is against a cold outside wall and holding damp.
  • The cabinet standing proud of the wall, or a mounting screw that has begun to back out under the weight of steel.
  • A drawer runner sagging or dragging, a sign the drawer is carrying more than it was built for.
  • Condensation or a damp patch on the wall behind the cabinet.
The call that matters here
The inherited jar of mixed hardware

There is a jar or a tin, probably from a relative's garage, holding a few kilograms of unsorted screws, nuts, and washers, and it has followed you through two houses because there is obviously good hardware in there. Price the alternative first: a full box of the screws you actually drive costs less than the evening you would spend sorting. So give it one sitting. Pick out only the sizes you already have a compartment for, and when the sitting ends, whatever is left in the jar goes to metal recycling that same day. Do not tip the remainder back into a smaller jar. That is exactly how it survived the last two moves.

Watch for
  • Fall, cut, or crushA loaded small-parts cabinet pulling off the wall, or a top-heavy drawer unit tipping when you open the highest drawer full of bolts.
  • Poison, choke, or strangleLoose screws, washers, and wall anchors on the floor at the height where a toddler or a dog finds them first.
Leave behind

One size per compartment, a sample fastener on the front, and a minimum line inside.

Trigger: When you empty your pockets at the end of a job, the loose hardware goes back into its compartment rather than onto the bench.

See Chapter 14, visual controls.

Material Rack

60-90 min, most of it in Sort, and nearly all of that on the offcut pile

Hold the wood and metal you will genuinely use, stored so it stays straight and does not fall on anyone.

Done looks like

Boards lying flat and supported along their length with the longest and heaviest at the bottom, sheet goods standing on edge against the wall rather than leaning out into the room, and an offcut bin with its size floor written on the front in marker. Nothing stored directly on the floor, and the route from the bench to the door clear along its full width.

  • SortTake the offcut bin apart piece by piece against a written size floor. Twisted or badly cupped boards, anything with hidden nails or an unknown coating, and pressure treated scrap all leave, and the coated stock does not go on the fire.
  • StraightenGroup by length and thickness so you can pull a board without unstacking three others, put pipe, trim, and dowel upright in a vertical bin, and support flat stock at intervals close enough that nothing sags between brackets.
  • ShineSweep out the gap behind the rack where sawdust, spider webs, and mouse droppings collect out of sight, then wipe surface rust off steel stock and give it a light oil before it goes back.
  • SafetyFix the rack into structural framing, not just the wall skin, and load it heaviest at the bottom. A full sheet of ply leaning at too shallow an angle will slide flat without warning, and it will take out whatever is in front of it.
  • StandardizeMark the lengths on the rack rails so stock returns to the right bay, write the size floor on the offcut bin, and mark a fill line above which the bin is full and the shortest piece leaves.
  • SustainBefore you unplug the saw at the end of a cutting session, every offcut goes either into the bin or into the fire bucket. Not onto the bench, not onto the floor, not on top of the rack.
How to clean it

The mess here hides behind the rack, not on it. Sweep out the gap where sawdust, webs, and droppings gather, and wipe the surface rust off your steel stock before it goes back.

Surface or areaMethod
The top of the rack and its highest armsStart at the top. Brush the cobwebs and settled dust off the top rail and the highest arms with a long-handled brush before you touch the stock below.
The gap behind the rackSweep or vacuum the strip of floor behind the rack that never sees daylight, where sawdust, spider webs, and mouse droppings collect out of sight.
The rack arms and bracketsWipe each arm and bracket, and clear the sawdust that packs into the angle where the arm meets the upright.
The timber and boardsWipe the dust off the faces and edges of the boards as you restack them, running a hand along each one to feel for grit before it goes back.
The steel and metal stockWipe the surface rust off steel stock with a cloth and lay on a light film of oil before it goes back, since bare steel on an open rack rusts from the shop air alone.
Sheet goods standing against the wallWipe down the faces of the sheet goods and clear the grit from the floor where their bottom edge stands.
The offcut binTip the offcut bin out, vacuum the sawdust from the bottom, and refill it only up to the size marked on the front.
The floor and the route to the doorVacuum or sweep the full width of the route from the rack to the door so nothing is left low to trip on.
What you clean with
Compact vacuum with attachmentsColor-coded microfiber cloth setNeutral pH multi-surface cleanerPortable cleaning caddyStiff broom or long-handled brushLight machine oil
Inspect as you clean
  • Mouse droppings, gnawed edges, or fresh webs behind the rack, the signs something has moved in where you cannot see.
  • Surface rust spreading on steel stock, or damp staining and a bow in a board that says water is reaching the rack.
  • A rack bracket bending, a weld cracking, or the upright pulling away from the wall under load.
  • A sheet leaning at too shallow an angle and starting to creep, which you often hear as a faint slide before you see it.
The call that matters here
The offcut that is still good wood

This is the hardest decision in the workshop and you already know it. Every short piece in that bin is genuinely usable, which is exactly why the pile never shrinks, and the cost of keeping it is invisible while the cost of tossing it feels sharp. Set two limits and let them decide for you instead. A size floor, anything shorter than your forearm or narrower than your palm, and a bin cap, one bin and no overflow. When the bin is full, the shortest piece in it leaves to make room for the newest. You are not judging whether a piece is good wood. You are only judging whether it is better than the worst piece already in the bin.

Watch for
  • Fall, cut, or crushSheet goods sliding off a shallow lean, and long boards pulled from the middle of a stack bringing the ones above down onto your feet.
  • Burn or fireA dry offcut pile stacked against the wall near a heater, water heater, or extension lead.
Leave behind

One offcut bin with a written size floor and a fill line, and nothing stored on the shop floor.

Trigger: When the saw gets unplugged, the offcuts get their verdict.

See Chapter 21, the hazard hunt.

Finishing and Chemical Zone

45-75 min, most of it in Sort and Safety, with a trip to hazardous waste disposal on the same day

Store paint, glue, stain, and solvent so they stay usable and so they never start a fire.

Done looks like

Every product in its original labeled container inside a closed metal cabinet, each lid dated in marker, water based paint on one shelf and solvents and thinners on another. Oily rags in a self-closing metal can with the lid down, a drip tray under the solvent shelf, and no aerosol standing on a sunny sill or near a pilot light.

  • SortOpen and judge each container. Paint with a thick skin or a sour smell is finished, wood glue that has gone stringy or granular will not hold a joint, and cured polyurethane in a half bottle is a rock. Take all of it to household hazardous waste, never the bin, never the drain.
  • StraightenSeparate what should never meet: solvents and thinners on their own shelf, water based finishes on another, and hardeners kept away from the resins they cure. Keep the stir sticks and the brushes for each family with them.
  • ShineWipe the rim of every can before the lid goes back on, because a rim full of dried finish is why the can skins over. Clean brushes right through to the ferrule and wipe down the spill tray.
  • SafetyA rag soaked in oil finish generates its own heat as it cures and can ignite with nothing touching it, so it goes in a lidded metal can or gets spread flat outdoors to dry. Keep flammable liquids away from any pilot light or heater, and ventilate before you spray, not after.
  • StandardizeDate every container with a marker the day it arrives, and write the shelf layout on the inside of the cabinet door so the solvents and the water based finishes stay apart even when you are putting things away in a hurry.
  • SustainWhen you wash out a brush at the end of a finishing job, that is when the can gets its date checked and the rag goes into the metal can.
How to clean it

Clean this zone can by can. Wipe every rim before the lid goes back so the can does not skin over, clean your brushes right through to the ferrule, and wipe down the spill tray.

Surface or areaMethod
The top of the metal cabinet and its upper shelfDust and wipe the top of the cabinet first, then the upper shelf where the water based paints stand.
The cans, tins, and containersWipe the rim and the shoulder of every can before the lid goes back on, because a rim packed with dried finish is why a lid stops sealing and the can skins over.
The solvent shelf and the drip tray beneath itWipe the solvent shelf, then lift the drip tray out and clean it, so the next drip lands on bare metal you can see rather than into a crust of old spills.
Brushes and application toolsClean each brush right through to the ferrule with the matching thinner or brush cleaner, work it dry, and hang it bristle down.
The oily rag canWipe the outside of the rag can and check that the self-closing lid falls shut on its own weight.
The cabinet floor and the shop floor beneath itVacuum and wipe the cabinet floor, then the strip of floor in front, watching for the sheen of a slow seep.
What you clean with
Neutral pH multi-surface cleanerPortable cleaning caddyCompact vacuum with attachmentsColor-coded microfiber cloth setMineral spirits or matching brush cleanerClean shop rags
Inspect as you clean
  • A can rim skinned over, a lid rusting into the seam, or a container bulging or swollen out of shape.
  • A ring or a stain on the shelf under the solvents that says something is weeping through a seam or a tired gasket.
  • An aerosol gone warm or bulging, or a label rubbed illegible so you can no longer tell water based from solvent.
  • Liquid pooled and standing in the drip tray, and the smell of solvent in the air with every lid supposedly shut.
The call that matters here
The wall of half-used paint

You are storing a row of part cans against the possibility of a touch-up, including colours from a wall you painted over and a house you no longer live in. Almost none of it will still brush out matching in two years anyway. So keep only colours currently on a surface in this house, and keep them properly: one small sealed jar of each, labeled with the room and the finish, not the original half-empty tin sitting open to the air. Everything else goes to hazardous waste on the same day you sort it, because a box of condemned paint sitting by the door for a month is just the same problem, relocated.

Watch for
  • Burn or fireOil finish rags balled up in a bin liner or draped over the bench, and solvent stored within reach of a water heater flame.
  • Poison, choke, or strangleThinner or turpentine decanted into an unlabeled drink bottle, and spraying or brushing solvent based finish in a closed shop with the door shut.
Leave behind

Original containers, dated lids, incompatibles on separate shelves, and rags in a lidded metal can.

Trigger: When the brush goes under the tap, the rag goes in the can and the lid goes down.

See Chapter 22, prevent and prepare.

Safety and PPE Station

30-45 min, most of it in Sort and Standardize, checking dates and writing them where you can read them

Make protective gear so easy to reach that putting it on is faster than skipping it.

Done looks like

Safety glasses on hooks at the door you come in through, one labeled pair per regular user, hearing protection beside them, and dust masks in a dated box. An extinguisher mounted on the wall with its gauge in the green and clear floor in front of it, and a first aid kit that still has burn dressings and more than three plasters in it.

  • SortScratched, hazed safety glasses are the reason people stop wearing safety glasses, so bin them. Perished gloves, respirator cartridges past their date, and a first aid kit you have quietly been raiding for household plasters all get replaced rather than rearranged.
  • StraightenHang the gear at the door on the path you walk in, not on the far wall past the bench, and sized to the actual people who work here. Silhouettes behind each item show at a glance which pair is missing.
  • ShineWash the lenses in soap and water rather than wiping them on a dusty shop rag, which is what scratches them in the first place. Wipe the sweat and sawdust off the earmuff cushions so they still seal.
  • SafetyLift the extinguisher, check the gauge sits in the green, and confirm nothing has crept into the space in front of it. Then put your hand on the shop's power cutoff and confirm you could reach it with sawdust in your eyes and the lights off.
  • StandardizePut an inspection date on masking tape on the extinguisher bracket and the first aid kit lid, and hang your safety glasses over the plug of the saw so you cannot power up without handling them first.
  • SustainThe gear goes back on the board as you switch off the shop light, never into a drawer or a jacket pocket, so the next person to walk in finds a full board.
How to clean it

Clean the gear so it still protects. Wash the lenses in soap and water instead of a dusty rag that scratches them, and wipe the sweat and sawdust off the earmuff cushions so they still seal.

Surface or areaMethod
The hooks and the board by the doorDust the hooks and the board the gear hangs on, and vacuum the ledge above where sawdust drifts down onto clean glasses.
The safety glasses and gogglesWash each pair of lenses in mild dish soap and warm water and dry with a clean microfiber cloth, never a shop rag, which is what puts the scratches there in the first place.
The hearing protectionWipe the sweat and sawdust off the earmuff cushions and the headband so the cushions still seal against your head, and wipe out the earplug case.
The dust mask boxWipe the outside of the dust mask box, and check that the masks are still boxed and dated rather than sitting loose and dusty.
The extinguisher and its bracketWipe the extinguisher body and bracket, and clean the gauge face with a streak-free glass cleaner so you can read the needle at a glance.
The first aid kitWipe the outside of the kit, open it, and clear any grit that has worked its way in among the dressings.
The floor in front of the stationVacuum and wipe the clear floor in front of the extinguisher and the door so nothing creeps into the space you need to reach fast.
What you clean with
Mild dish soapColor-coded microfiber cloth setStreak-free glass and mirror cleanerCompact vacuum with attachmentsNeutral pH multi-surface cleanerPortable cleaning caddy
Inspect as you clean
  • Earmuff cushions gone hard, cracked, or split so they no longer seal against your head.
  • Lenses so scratched or hazed that you would be tempted to lift them off to see the cut line.
  • The first aid kit low on burn dressings or down to its last few plasters, or dressings past their date.
  • The dust mask box empty, or masks that have sat open and loaded with the dust they are meant to stop.
  • Corrosion on the extinguisher body, or a bent, missing, or unsealed safety pin.
The call that matters here
The visitor rule

The genuinely awkward decision here is not your own gear, it is what happens when your brother-in-law or your fourteen year old wanders in while the saw is running and wants to help. You have been handling it case by case, which means you have been handling it badly. Decide it once and hang it on the wall: anyone who works in this shop has their own labeled set of glasses and muffs, and there is one spare set on a hook marked for visitors. If a visitor will not wear it, they stay on the near side of the doorway and watch. Saying that once, in advance, is far easier than saying it while holding a running grinder.

Watch for
  • Burn or fireAn extinguisher with the gauge out of the green, no inspection date on it, or a stack of timber leaning against the wall in front of it.
  • Fall, cut, or crushSafety glasses so scratched that you take them off to see the cut line, which is the moment the chip flies.
Leave behind

A full silhouette board at the door, one labeled set per user plus a visitor set, with dated extinguisher and first aid checks.

Trigger: When your safety glasses come off your face, they go straight onto their hook, never into a pocket or a drawer.

See Chapter 24, the checklist.

Room 17 of 20

Mudroom

6 zones

The mudroom is where the weather stops. Everything the house wants to keep outside arrives here first: wet boots, muddy paws, dripping umbrellas, the dog towel that never quite dries, and the coat wall that grows a layer every October. Start at the hooks, because almost everything lying on the mudroom floor is there for one reason, which is that the wall above it ran out of room.

Where to startDo the hook wall before anything else, and do it with the family standing there so every coat gets handed to its owner rather than judged by you. Once each person has a named column at their own reach, the boots, bags and bins underneath stop migrating onto the floor.
Cleaning noteGrit is what wears this room out, and you beat it with three layers at the threshold: a stiff bristle mat outside to scrape, an absorbent mat inside to soak, and a lipped tray for anything still dripping. Shake the mats and rinse the tray on the same day, because grit ground underfoot sands the finish off a floor.
The trapThe mudroom is one room from the door, so it quietly becomes the holding bay for the whole house: the returns, the charity bag, the football kit for a season that finished in May. Lidded boxes make this worse, because a lid hides the drift. Open bins and bare hooks keep the truth visible.

Family Hook Zone

45-75 min, most of it in Sort, because every coat has to be handed to a person

Holds the coats and bags that leave in the morning and come back the same evening.

Done looks like

Two coats and one bag per person, each on their own named column, the lowest hooks set at the height of the shortest person in the house, and bare floor along the whole run of wall beneath them.

  • SortTake every coat, bag, hat and scarf off the wall into one pile and hand each item to its owner to keep or release. Anything nobody has worn since the last change of season leaves the door and goes up to a wardrobe.
  • StraightenGive each person a vertical strip of wall rather than a shared rail, and set their hooks at their own reach so a child never has to ask for their coat. Bag on the lower hook, coat on the upper, so a backpack is never lifted over a wet jacket.
  • ShineWipe the rail and the wall behind it where shoulders and sleeves leave a grey smear, then vacuum the narrow strip of floor directly under the hooks where hood fluff and door grit gather.
  • SafetyA hook carrying four wet coats will pull a screw straight out of plasterboard, so check that every hook sits in a stud or a proper anchor before you reload the wall. Keep scarves, cords and drawstrings off the lowest hooks where a small child plays alone.
  • StandardizeWrite each name on its column at that person's eye height, and say the limit out loud so everyone hears it: one coat, one bag, one hat. “A standard is the best way you know today, written down.”
  • SustainWhen you hang your own coat up at the end of the day, look along the wall and move anything sitting on the wrong column back to its owner's.
How to clean it

Work this wall from the top down. Wipe the rail and the wall behind it where shoulders and sleeves leave a grey smear, then finish on the narrow strip of floor under the hooks where hood fluff and door grit gather.

Surface or areaMethod
The wall and any backboard above the railStart high with a dry microfiber cloth to lift loose dust, then go over the painted wall and the backboard with a cloth just damp with neutral pH multi-surface cleaner. Work in downward strokes so the grime you loosen falls toward the floor you clean last, and give the grey shoulder-height smear a second pass.
The hook rail and the hooks themselvesUnload the wall completely first. Wipe the full length of the rail and each individual hook with the damp cloth, getting into the throat of every hook and the screw heads where fluff packs in. Dry the metal off with a second cloth so it does not sit wet against the wall.
The hanging coats and bagsTake each coat down and brush the shoulders and collar with a soft clothing brush, following the weave, to lift the hair and street dust that settles on the tops. Turn bag straps and hoods out and brush them too before they go back on their named column.
The skirting board behind the runRun the damp cloth along the top edge and face of the skirting board behind where the coats hang, since it collects a felted band of dust and hood fluff that the coats hide. Follow the whole length of the wall, not just the ends.
The floor strip directly under the hooksVacuum the narrow strip of floor beneath the run with the crevice tool, working into the corner where the floor meets the skirting, then damp-mop or wipe the sealed floor with the floor-material-compatible cleaner. Do this pass last so nothing you knocked down stays behind.
What you clean with
Color-coded microfiber cloth setNeutral pH multi-surface cleanerCompact vacuum with attachmentsFloor-material-compatible cleanerPortable cleaning caddySoft clothing brush (not in this zone's list)
Inspect as you clean
  • As you take each coat off its hook, flag any hook that has started to lean, wobble, or leave a dark rim of pulled plaster around its screw.
  • Look for a fine crack in the plaster running from a screw head, and flag it, since it tells you the fixing has begun to work loose under the wet-coat load.
  • Note any coat or bag that comes down smelling musty or still feeling damp, which means it never dried through and wants flagging before it goes back up.
  • Watch for a spreading stain or a soft grey bloom on the wall behind the rail, and flag it as a possible leak or damp track coming through from behind.
The call that matters here
The fourth coat

The hard one is not the ugly coat, it is the good coat you love and have not put on since March. It stays at the door because carrying it upstairs feels like admitting something. Here is the rule that settles it: the door holds only the coats this week's weather calls for. Hang everything a person owns, then at the end of one week take down any coat still hanging exactly as you left it, untouched, and move it to their wardrobe.
