It is not gone, it is just not entitled to a hook. If two coats tie, keep the one that survives rain.

Watch for
  • Fall, cut, or crushAn overloaded hook rail tearing out of plasterboard brings the rail and every coat on it down at head height.
  • Poison, choke, or strangleScarf ends and coat drawstrings dangling at toddler height are a strangling risk when a small child is alone in the room.
  • FallBags dropped under a full wall sit right in the walking line from the door, which is where people go over in the dark.
Leave behind

One coat, one bag, one hat per named column, and the floor under the hooks stays empty.

Trigger: When you hang up your own coat at the end of the day.

See Chapter 14, visual controls.

Bench and Transition Surface

15-30 min, short, but most of it spent walking other people's things back to their rooms

Somewhere to sit while you deal with your footwear, and a brief stop for things genuinely on their way out of the house.

Done looks like

A bare seat wide enough for two people to lace boots at once, no more than three objects on the surface, and one labelled basket at the end holding only what is leaving tomorrow.

  • SortClear the bench down to bare wood in one go. Most of what is on it belongs in another room, so carry those things there now rather than sliding them to the far end, where they will sit for another month.
  • StraightenProtect the seat itself as empty space and give the strays one outbound basket: the library books, the parcel to return, the form waiting for a signature. Nothing that lives in this house permanently gets to sit on this bench.
  • ShineWipe the seat and the front edge where boot heels scuff it, then pull the bench away from the wall and sweep out the drift of grit, receipts and single socks underneath.
  • SafetyNothing heavy stores on a shelf above the seat, because a bike helmet or a paint tin landing on someone bent over a bootlace is exactly how that shelf fails. Keep the seat clear enough to actually sit on, because balancing on one leg to pull off a wet boot is how people go down.
  • StandardizeThree objects maximum on the surface, and the outbound basket empties on the next trip out of the door. Photograph the cleared bench and tape the picture inside the nearest cupboard door as the reference.
  • SustainWhen you sit down to change your shoes, take one thing off the bench into the house with you as you stand up.
How to clean it

Wipe the seat and the front edge where boot heels scuff it, then pull the bench off the wall and sweep out the drift of grit, receipts and single socks that lives underneath.

Surface or areaMethod
The seat top and any cushionClear the surface to bare seat. Wipe a hard wooden or laminate top with a cloth damp with neutral pH multi-surface cleaner, following the grain. If the seat has a cushion or pad, vacuum it with the upholstery tool and lift out any crumbs caught in the piping before it goes back.
The front edge and apronGo along the front edge and the apron below the seat with the damp cloth, where boot heels and shin kicks leave scuffs and a dark rub line. Let the cleaner sit a moment on a stubborn heel mark, then wipe it off rather than scrubbing raw.
Inside the bench box or lower cubbiesIf the bench opens or has cubbies, empty it, vacuum the base and corners where sand settles, then wipe it out and let it air with the lid up until it is bone dry before anything returns.
The outgoing basket at the endLift the leaving-tomorrow basket off, tip its crumbs and grit into the bin, and wipe the inside and the rim with the damp cloth. Set it back empty of anything that is not genuinely going out.
The floor under and behind the benchPull the bench away from the wall and vacuum the drift of grit, receipts and lone socks that gathers underneath, then wipe or mop the sealed floor there with the floor-material-compatible cleaner. Slide the bench back to its marked spot only once the floor is dry.
What you clean with
Neutral pH multi-surface cleanerColor-coded microfiber cloth setCompact vacuum with attachmentsFloor-material-compatible cleanerPortable cleaning caddy
Inspect as you clean
  • When you lift a hinged lid, flag a hinge that is loose or a lid-stay that no longer holds, since that is the finger-catcher waiting to happen.
  • Rock the bench as you pull it out and flag a leg joint that gives or a seat that flexes under a press of your hand.
  • Look at the shelf brackets above the seat and flag any that are pulling from the wall or sitting proud of their fixings.
  • Note a damp patch or a musty smell inside a closed bench box, which points to wet gear being shut in and wants flagging.
The call that matters here
The lid you never lift

If your bench has a hinged storage seat, this is the decision you have been dodging: the box under the lid is full and you cannot say what is in it. Storage under a seat you sit on ten times a day is storage you will never open, because opening it means clearing the seat first. Live with it for a week and count honestly how many times you lift that lid. Fewer than three and the answer is settled. Empty it completely and refill it only with things used while sitting, spare laces, a shoe horn, a rag for wiping soles. Everything else moves somewhere you can reach without asking a person to stand up. If it is still empty in a month, leave it empty.

Watch for
  • Fall, cut, or crushA heavy hinged bench lid dropping on small fingers, and anything stored on a shelf directly above where a head bends to tie a lace.
  • FallA bench seat covered in bags forces people to pull boots off standing on one leg on a wet floor.
Leave behind

The seat is always clear, and the surface never carries more than three things.

Trigger: When you sit down to change your shoes.

See Chapter 25, the ideal-state photo.

Shoe and Boot Storage

30-45 min, most of it in Sort, pairing up and walking out-of-season pairs upstairs

Holds the footwear actually being worn this season, and gives wet pairs somewhere to drain without soaking the floor.

Done looks like

Two pairs per person on the rack, soles down and toes out, one lipped tray by the door holding whatever came in wet, and clear floor between the tray and the doorway.

  • SortBring every pair in the house down to this floor and match them up. Outgrown pairs, split soles and unmatched singles leave today, and anything for the opposite season goes up to its owner's wardrobe rather than back on the rack.
  • StraightenOne row per person, heaviest boots on the bottom row so nobody lifts them, lightest slippers on top. Cleats and caked walking boots live on the tray only, never on a shelf where the dried mud drops onto the pair below.
  • ShineTip the tray out into the garden and wash it, because dried mud on a boot tray turns to dust and gets walked straight back inside. Wipe the rack rails and vacuum the floor of the rack where the grit lands.
  • SafetyA wet boot sole on a hard floor is the surest slip in this house, so the tray sits exactly where boots actually come off, not two steps further in. Keep the line from the door to the bench completely empty, because a shoe left out in a dark hallway is what people fall over at night.
  • StandardizeTwo pairs per person at the door, wet on the tray, dry on the rack. Mark the tray's footprint on the floor with tape so it goes back to the same spot after every clean.
  • SustainWhen you take your boots off, they go soles down on the tray, and when they are dry they move up to the shelf that evening.
How to clean it

Tip the boot tray out into the garden and wash it, because dried mud turns to dust and gets walked straight back in. Then wipe the rack rails and vacuum the grit that lands on the rack floor.

Surface or areaMethod
The top of the rack and any upper shelfStart at the top with a dry cloth to catch settled dust, then wipe the top surface with a cloth damp with neutral pH multi-surface cleaner. Work back to front so grit falls onto the lower shelves you have yet to reach.
The rack rails and shelvesTake the shoes off shelf by shelf. Wipe each rail and shelf with the damp cloth, getting the dried mud that cakes along the underside of a wire rail where you never look. Dry each rail before the shoes go back soles down and toes out.
The shoes and boots themselvesKnock caked mud off outdoors, brush the welts and treads clear, and wipe leather uppers with a barely damp cloth. Stand anything still wet on the tray to drain rather than back on the rack.
The lipped boot trayCarry the tray outside, tip the salt and grit into the garden, and hose or scrub it with a stiff brush until the lip and corners run clean. Dry it off before it goes back exactly where boots come off, since a wet tray just moves the puddle.
The floor of the rack and the run to the doorVacuum the rack floor with the crevice tool where grit and gravel collect, then wipe or mop the sealed floor from the tray to the doorway with the floor-material-compatible cleaner. Keep that stretch clear once it is dry.
What you clean with
Portable cleaning caddyCompact vacuum with attachmentsColor-coded microfiber cloth setNeutral pH multi-surface cleanerFloor-material-compatible cleanerGarden hose or outdoor tap (not in this zone's list)Stiff scrubbing brush (not in this zone's list)
Inspect as you clean
  • If a boot dryer or heated mat lives here, flag any flex that is cracked, taped, or lying across the wet strip of floor where boots drip.
  • As you clear each shelf, flag a rack shelf or rail that flexes, sags, or has a spot weld starting to give under the weight of loaded boots.
  • Note black speckling or a sour smell inside a boot that has been left damp, and flag it as gear that never dried through.
  • Watch for rust or a rough burr on a metal rack edge or on studded cleats stored soles up, and flag it before a hand meets it.
The call that matters here
The boots waiting for a winter that has not come

Everyone has a pair kept for conditions that never quite arrive: the proper wellies, the hiking boots worn twice, the smart shoes for the event you keep not going to. They are expensive and undamaged, which is why you will not decide. So separate the two questions. Keeping the pair is one decision; giving it a slot at the door is another. The rule for the door is simple: a pair earns a slot only if it has been on someone's feet in the last four weeks. Everything else goes to the wardrobe with today's date on a slip of paper tucked in the toe. When the season it was bought for has come and gone with that slip still in place, you have your answer, and it is not a hard one any more.

Watch for
  • FallWet soles carried past the tray leave an invisible film on hard floor right where everyone turns towards the kitchen.
  • Water and electricityA boot dryer or heated mat with its flex lying across the same floor the wet boots drip onto.
  • Fall, cut, or crushStudded cleats stored soles up on a low shelf catch bare hands and feet at exactly the wrong height.
Leave behind

Two pairs per person at the door, wet stays on the tray, and the path from the door stays bare.

Trigger: When you step in and take your boots off.

See Chapter 21, the hazard hunt.

Pet Station

15-30 min, quick, with most of the time spent deciding what does not belong here

Everything the animal needs to get out of the door, and everything you need when it comes back through it filthy.

Done looks like

Lead clipped to collar on one hook with a full roll of waste bags attached, an old towel folded within arm's reach of the door, food sealed in a bin with the scoop inside it, and every treatment behind a closed door above the animal's nose and above a toddler's reach.

  • SortLay out every lead, collar, harness and chewed toy you can find in this room. Keep the lead you genuinely walk with plus one spare, then let the outgrown collar, the harness that never fitted and the retired long line go.
  • StraightenHang the walking kit as one connected unit, lead clipped to collar with the bag roll threaded on, so leaving the house is one grab and not a search of three rooms. Towel on top of the food bin, treats up high where a nose cannot reach them.
  • ShineWash the scoop and wipe the crumbs and oils out of the bin before you refill it, rather than tipping fresh food onto stale, and put the paw towel through the wash with the door mats so one load covers both.
  • SafetyFlea, tick and worming treatments are poison to a child and to an animal of the wrong size, so they live in a closed box above reach, never loose in the caddy beside the bags. Coil the lead on its hook instead of leaving a loop hanging low, because a hanging loop at a toddler's height is a strangling risk.
  • StandardizeRefill the bin when the level drops to the scoop, and keep one spare bag roll behind the one in use. Write the daily food amount on a label taped to the bin lid so whoever feeds gets the same answer.
  • SustainWhen you clip the lead back on its hook after a walk, check the bag roll and swap it if it is down to the last few.
How to clean it

Wash the scoop and wipe the crumbs and oils out of the food bin before you refill it, rather than tipping fresh food onto stale. Put the paw towel through the wash with the door mats so one load covers both.

Surface or areaMethod
The lead hook and the wall around itLift the lead and bag roll off and wipe the hook and the wall patch behind it with a cloth damp with neutral pH multi-surface cleaner, where nose-height smears and hair build up. Coil the lead back on rather than leaving a loop hanging.
The food bin, inside and outEmpty out any stale food before you top up, vacuum the crumbs and dusty fines from the corners of the bin, then wipe the inside dry so no oily film stays to go rancid. Wipe the lid and outside of the bin too, and reseal it.
The scoopWash the scoop in hot soapy water and dry it fully, since a damp scoop buried back in the food is what turns the next fill greasy. Store it inside the sealed bin.
The paw towel and any beddingSend the paw towel through the wash together with the door mats so one load does both, shaking it out over the bin first so you are not running loose grit and hair through the machine. If a pet bed or blanket lives here, strip its cover into the same wash and vacuum the base pad with the upholstery tool before it goes back down.
The food and water bowlsTip and wash both bowls daily in hot soapy water, scrubbing the slime ring that forms at the waterline and the dried film in the food bowl, then dry and refill. Wipe the mat or tray they stand on.
The floor under the stationVacuum the spilled kibble and hair from under and around the station, working into the corner behind the bin, then wipe or mop the sealed floor with the floor-material-compatible cleaner, since splashed water and food dust make a sticky film there.
What you clean with
Portable cleaning caddyCompact vacuum with attachmentsColor-coded microfiber cloth setNeutral pH multi-surface cleanerFloor-material-compatible cleanerDish soap for bowls and scoop (not in this zone's list)
Inspect as you clean
  • As you coil the lead, flag a clip whose spring is weak, a collar buckle that is fraying, or webbing worn thin where it takes the strain.
  • Note a food bin whose seal no longer clicks shut or whose lid is cracked, since a bin that lets air in is a bin that lets pests in, and flag it.
  • Watch for a chewed patch on the lead, the bag roll, or the towel that says the animal has been getting to them, and flag it.
  • When you move the water bowl, flag a slow damp patch or a warped floorboard under it that a daily splash has been quietly soaking.
The call that matters here
Whether the food bin belongs here at all

The mudroom pulls in everything with the animal's name on it, and the food sack is the biggest and least questioned piece. So ask where the bowl actually sits. If you feed in the kitchen, the food is stored here purely because the sack came in through this door, and every meal costs you a walk across the house carrying a scoop. This zone is for departures and arrivals only: lead, bags, towel, coat, paw wash. Feeding, grooming and paperwork belong wherever the bowl and the sink are. Move the bin to the bowl. If the answer is that the bin will not fit in the kitchen, that is a kitchen problem to solve, not a reason to keep walking.

Watch for
  • Poison, choke, or strangleSpot-on flea treatment and worming tablets stored in an open caddy at the same height as the waste bags a child is allowed to fetch.
  • FallA knocked water bowl on hard floor, right where people are already stepping in with wet soles.
Leave behind

Lead, collar and a full bag roll hang together as one kit, and treatments stay behind a closed door above reach.

Trigger: When you hang the lead back up after a walk.

Seasonal Outdoor Gear

30-45 min, most of it in Sort, on the floor matching gloves into pairs

Holds the small weather items the family grabs on the way out, for the season you are actually in.

Done looks like

One open bin per person at their own height holding this season's gloves and hat with every pair matched, umbrellas standing points down in one stand by the door, and the other season's bin closed, labelled with the month you packed it, and up on the high shelf.

  • SortTip every glove, mitten and hat onto the floor and match them into pairs. Put the singles in one bag, and read the dates on the sun cream and the insect repellent, because a bottle opened two summers ago is no longer doing the job printed on the label.
  • StraightenOpen bins with no lids, one per person, set at their own reach so a child can get their own gloves without asking anyone. Umbrellas go in the stand and never in a bin, because one wet umbrella soaks everything it lies against.
  • ShineEverything goes into off-season storage washed and bone dry, because damp wool packed away in April comes out smelling in October. Wipe out the empty bins and get the sand out of the corners before you refill them.
  • SafetyInsect repellent and sun cream sitting in a low open bin will find their way into a small mouth, so anything carrying a warning on the label goes in a closed box above child reach. Store umbrellas points down, because a spike stood upright sits at a toddler's eye height.
  • StandardizeTwo changeovers a year, spring and autumn, and the month you closed a bin goes on its lid. Write the same short order on that lid as well: gloves matched, hats washed, creams checked for date, umbrellas emptied out.
  • SustainThe first cold morning you reach into the bin for gloves and find it empty is the signal, and the changeover happens that weekend.
How to clean it

Everything goes into off-season storage washed and bone dry, because damp wool packed away in April comes out smelling in October. Wipe out the empty bins and get the sand out of the corners before you refill them.

Surface or areaMethod
The high shelf and the off-season bin lidsStart with the dry cloth on the top shelf and the closed off-season bin lids, then wipe them with a cloth damp with neutral pH multi-surface cleaner. Do this before you open anything so the settled dust does not drop onto clean gear.
The open per-person binsEmpty each bin, vacuum the sand and grit from the corners, then wipe the inside and the rim and let it dry fully. Refill with this season's matched pairs only, one person's height per bin.
The gloves, hats and scarvesAnything going into off-season storage gets washed and dried right through first. Shake this season's items out, match every pair, and set them back so the gear is dry before it is boxed, since damp wool is what smells six months on.
The umbrella standLift the umbrellas out and tip the stand over the bin, since rain carries grit and leaf litter down into the base. Wipe or rinse the inside, dry it, and stand the umbrellas back points down.
The floor under the stand and shelfVacuum the floor beneath the umbrella stand and the shelf where drips and sand collect, then wipe or mop the sealed floor with the floor-material-compatible cleaner. Dry it before the stand goes back.
What you clean with
Portable cleaning caddyCompact vacuum with attachmentsColor-coded microfiber cloth setNeutral pH multi-surface cleanerFloor-material-compatible cleaner
Inspect as you clean
  • As you empty each bin, flag a cracked lid or a bin whose corner has split, since it will not seal the off-season gear against damp.
  • Put your nose into the off-season bin as you open it and flag a musty note, which means something went in damp last time.
  • When you lift the umbrellas, flag a bent rib, a jammed slider, or a rusted spike, and note any tip left stored point up.
  • Feel the high shelf brackets as you wipe them and flag any that flex or sit loose under a packed seasonal bin.
The call that matters here
The bag of single gloves

You are keeping orphan gloves because the other half is somewhere in a coat pocket, a car door or the school lost property, and one of them was expensive. That bag has survived three changeovers already. Give it a rule instead of another reprieve: singles go in one bag with today's date on it, and the bag lives through exactly one changeover. Whatever is still unmatched when you next pack the bins goes, including the good ski glove. A right-hand glove on its own is not a glove. The one exception worth making is a survivor that fits a child who will need a bigger pair by next winter anyway, and even that gets a date on it.

Watch for
  • Poison, choke, or strangleInsect repellent and sun cream stored in an open low bin that a young child is encouraged to help themselves from.
  • Fall, cut, or crushA packed seasonal bin lifted down from a high shelf overhead, and umbrella spikes stored points up at face height.
Leave behind

Only the current season is within reach, singles get one bag and one season, and every stored bin carries the month it was closed.

Trigger: The first morning of the season you reach for gloves and the bin is empty.

See Chapter 24, the checklist, the schedule, and the name.

Cleaning and Utility Zone

30-45 min, most of it in Sort, working through part-used bottles

The tools that keep the entry floor and the door mats fit to walk on, kept where the mess actually lands.

Done looks like

Broom, mop and dustpan clipped to the wall with their heads hanging clear of the floor, the vacuum parked flat against the wall inside a marked outline, and one caddy holding the four cleaners you genuinely use, each in its own labelled bottle.

  • SortLine every bottle up on the floor and read them. Keep the floor cleaner, the glass spray, the disinfectant and the pet stain remover you actually reach for, and send out the last inch in the six other bottles, following the disposal line printed on each label.
  • StraightenClip each long-handled tool near its head so the bristles hang clear of the floor and the handle points down. A broom stood on its bristles bends them out of shape and holds on to whatever it swept last.
  • ShineRinse the mop head and let it dry fully before it goes back on its clip, cut the hair off the vacuum's brush bar, and empty the dustpan rather than storing it loaded.
  • SafetyNever keep bleach and an ammonia-based cleaner in one closed caddy, because a leaking cap in a shut box mixes them into a gas you cannot get away from fast enough. This corner also holds a mains vacuum next to wet boots and a damp mop, so the socket and the flex stay up off the floor and dry.
  • StandardizeThe mop, broom and dustpan hang, and the vacuum parks inside its outline; nothing in this corner stands on the floor and nothing goes back on its clip wet. Paint or tape that outline so the empty space reads as a job done rather than a gap.
  • SustainWhen you shake the door mats out, hang the broom back on its clip and feel whether the mop head has dried.
How to clean it

Rinse the mop head and let it dry fully before it goes back on its clip, cut the hair off the vacuum's brush bar, and empty the dustpan rather than storing it loaded.

Surface or areaMethod
The wall clips and hanging long-handled toolsWipe the broom, mop and dustpan handles and the wall clips with a cloth damp with neutral pH multi-surface cleaner. Comb the broom bristles clear of trapped hair and grit over the bin, and hang each tool head-up and clear of the floor.
The mop headRinse the mop head under running water until it runs clear, wring it hard, and stand or hang it to dry fully outside the closed corner before it clips back. A mop stored wet is what turns this corner sour.
The vacuum body and brush barEmpty the canister, then cut the wound hair off the brush bar with scissors and pull it free. Wipe the vacuum body and the wheels with a barely damp cloth, keeping moisture away from the motor housing and vents, and coil the flex loosely back onto its hooks.
The caddy and the cleaner bottlesLift each bottle, wipe the crusted ring off its base, and check the label is intact and facing out before it goes back. Wipe out the caddy itself so the next slow drip lands on clean plastic.
The dustpanEmpty the dustpan into the bin and wipe both the pan and its brush rather than storing it loaded, since a full pan just tips its contents back onto the floor next time you lift it.
The marked floor outline and the socket areaVacuum then wipe the sealed floor inside the vacuum's taped outline with the floor-material-compatible cleaner where grit drops off the wheels. Go around the socket faceplate with a barely damp cloth, never a wet one, and keep this patch dry, since it shares the corner with wet boots and a damp mop.
What you clean with
Portable cleaning caddyCompact vacuum with attachmentsColor-coded microfiber cloth setNeutral pH multi-surface cleanerFloor-material-compatible cleaner
Inspect as you clean
  • As you coil the vacuum flex, flag any cracked or scorched section of insulation, a bent plug pin, or a floor socket that looks scorched or feels warm.
  • Wipe each bottle and flag any whose label has worn off or that has been decanted into an unmarked container, so nobody has to guess what is inside.
  • Note a bleach cap and an ammonia-cleaner cap sitting together in a shut caddy, and flag it, since a slow leak in a closed box is the risk.
  • Check the mop and broom for a split handle or a head working loose from its socket, and flag it before it comes apart mid-stroke.
The call that matters here
The second vacuum

The old machine is still here because it works, roughly, and getting rid of something that works feels wasteful. Meanwhile it holds the corner and the new one lives in the walking line. A spare is only a spare if it has actually covered for the main one. So ask the question that decides it: in the last year, did the main vacuum ever break down for long enough that you went and got this one out? If the answer is no, it is not a backup, it is a machine you are storing on behalf of a future that has not asked for it. Pass it to someone who has no vacuum at all, and give the corner to the one you use every week.

Watch for
  • Poison, choke, or strangleBleach and an ammonia-based cleaner shut in one caddy together, and decanted cleaners in unlabelled bottles at child height.
  • Water and electricityA vacuum flex and a floor socket sharing a corner with a dripping mop and wet boot trays.
  • Fall, cut, or crushLong-handled tools leaned against a wall instead of clipped, which slide sideways and come down across a doorway.
Leave behind

Every tool hangs with its head clear of the floor, nothing goes away wet, and bleach never shares a container with anything else.

Trigger: When you shake out the door mats.

See Chapter 22, prevent and prepare.

Room 18 of 20

Hall Closet

5 zones

The hall closet is the darkest and deepest storage in the house, and the only one nobody owns. Everything that had to go somewhere ended up behind this door, stacked to the depth of your arm. Start with the shelf you cannot see the back of, because that is where the closet is lying to you about what it holds.

Where to startEmpty one shelf onto the hallway floor before you touch anything else. A closet this deep cannot be sorted in place; you have to see the back wall to know what you own.
Cleaning noteClosed dark spaces hold damp. Wipe the shelf boards and let them dry completely before linens or paper packs go back, and leave the door standing open for an hour after you finish.
The trapAlmost everything in this closet arrived because it had no other home, not because it belongs here. Ask of each item which room it actually serves, and walk most of it back there.

Linen Shelf Zone

45-75 min, most of it in Sort, because every set has to be unfolded to see whether it is still complete

Holds the sheets, towels, and blankets the beds in this house actually rotate through.

Done looks like

Three sheet sets per bed, each nested inside one of its own pillowcases, with the bed size written on the shelf edge underneath. Everyday towels folded to one width in a stack no taller than your forearm. Two spare blankets on the top shelf with nothing balanced on them.

  • SortUnfold every set and check it is complete and fits a bed you still own. The flat sheet whose fitted half vanished, fitted sheets with slack elastic that pop off a corner at three in the morning, and towels that smell sour an hour after drying all leave.
  • StraightenFold each set to one uniform footprint and bundle it inside its own pillowcase so a bed change is one grab, not four. Queen and king low, everyday towels at chest height, guest blankets on top.
  • ShinePull every stack right out, wipe the shelf boards with a barely damp cloth, and let the wood dry fully before linens return. Watch for the grey line along the shelf lip that means dust is drifting down onto clean sheets.
  • SafetyA tall stack on a high shelf comes down as one mass when you tug the bottom towel. Keep no stack taller than your forearm, and keep the heavy blankets at waist height rather than overhead.
  • StandardizeThe shelf label carries the count, not just the name: "Queen, 3 sets." Anyone opening this door can see the shelf is short before laundry day rather than at bedtime.
  • SustainStripping a bed sends you here for the fresh bundle, and the count on the label tells you on the spot whether you are down to your last set.
How to clean it

Clean sheets only stay clean on a clean board, so this zone comes down to bare shelf every time. Work top down, so the dust off the blanket shelf never lands on the towels you just wiped.

Surface or areaMethod
The top shelf and the two spare blankets on itLift the blankets down onto a bed or a chair outside the closet first. Run the vacuum brush head along the board to pick up the settled dust before it turns to grit, then wipe with a barely damp microfiber cloth and let the wood dry fully before the blankets return.
Each shelf board and the grey line along its front lipPull every stack right off the board. Vacuum the front lip where dust drifts down, then wipe the board with a barely damp cloth, reaching for the neutral cleaner only on an actual mark. Dry each board before linen goes back on it.
The back wall behind the stacksWipe the wall from the top corner down to the lowest shelf with a dry cloth first, then a barely damp one. This is the surface that sheds onto folded cotton, and it is the one nobody sees while the shelves are full.
The shelf brackets and the underside of each shelfRun a dry cloth over the metal brackets and along the underside of the board above, catching the fluffy dust that clings there and drops onto the stack below when you slide a sheet out.
The linens themselves, while the shelves are bareShake each folded sheet and towel out and refold to one width. Anything carrying a musty note gets a rewash now rather than going back to seal the smell into the shelf.
The closet floor and skirting under the bottom shelfVacuum the corners and along the skirting where lint gathers, then wipe the floor with a damp cloth and let it dry before you reload the low shelf.
The inside face of the door and the shelf-edge labelsWipe the inner door panel and the bed-size labels written on each shelf edge with a dry cloth, so grit sitting there does not drift back onto the clean stacks.
What you clean with
Compact vacuum with attachmentsColor-coded microfiber cloth setNeutral pH multi-surface cleanerPortable cleaning caddy
Inspect as you clean
  • The grey dust line along the shelf lip: notice whether it is heavier than last time, because a thickening line means dust is drifting in from somewhere new.
  • A yellow or brown stain spreading on a shelf board, or a persistent musty smell in one stack, can mean damp is reaching the closet from a wall or a slow drip from above. Flag it.
  • A board that bows downward in the middle under a loaded stack, or a bracket screw that has backed out and left a gap where the metal meets the wall. Note it.
  • Rust freckling on a metal bracket, or marks on wire shelving where moisture keeps settling. Flag it.
  • Small holes, gritty frass, or pale casings tucked into a wool blanket point to moth. Flag it before you refold and reshelve.
The call that matters here
The good set you never put on a bed

There is a sheet set here that is too nice to use and too nice to give away, so it has sat at the front of the shelf for years while you sleep on the worn ones. Settle it with a date: put it on a bed this month, or accept that it is a keepsake rather than a linen. Keepsakes can live in a memory box. They cannot live on the shelf you reach into every week.

Watch for
  • FallPulling a towel from the bottom of a chest-high stack brings the whole pile down onto your face and the hallway floor.
  • Fall, cut, or crushDamp-weight blankets and a full shelf of folded cotton are heavier than they look; check the shelf brackets are screwed into studs, not just into plasterboard.
Leave behind

Three sets per bed, each nested in its own pillowcase, with the size and count written on the shelf edge.

Trigger: When you strip the beds on laundry day, the fresh set comes off this shelf and you read the count as you lift it.

Cleaning Equipment Zone

30-45 min, most of it in Straighten, since the fix here is mounting a rail and getting the handles off the floor

Parks the vacuum, broom, and mop so they come out in one motion and go back without a fight.

Done looks like

The vacuum standing inside a taped floor outline with its cord wrapped and every crevice tool and brush head zipped in one bag clipped to its handle. Broom, mop, and duster hanging handle-down from wall clips with the heads clear of the floor. The closet door swinging fully open without touching anything.

  • SortStand every handle up and look at the working end. Brooms with splayed bristles that push dust rather than gather it, the mop head that no longer twists off its plate, and the old vacuum you keep for parts you have never once harvested all go out this week.
  • StraightenFit a rail and hang each tool so bristles and mop heads swing free, because heads left standing on the floor bend over and re-soil what you just cleaned. Tape an outline for the vacuum's footprint and keep the attachments in one labeled bag clipped to its handle.
  • ShineEmpty the canister, cut the wound hair off the beater bar with scissors, rinse the mop head and dry it outside the closet. Sweep out the grit that drops off the vacuum wheels into the closet floor corners.
  • SafetyA broom leaned against the wall slides and lands across the doorway in the dark. Clip every handle to the rail, and never shut a damp mop head or a wet bucket into this closet.
  • StandardizeThe taped outline is the standard: if you can see floor inside the tape, the vacuum is out and not yet put away. The attachment bag rides on the handle, never loose on a shelf.
  • SustainSwitching the vacuum off is the signal to wrap the cord, not to lean it against the wall for later.
How to clean it

The tools that clean the house get cleaned here, or they just move the dirt around. Empty the vacuum, comb the broom, and rinse the mop, and dry every wet head outside the closet before it goes back on its clip.

Surface or areaMethod
The wall clips and the hanging railWipe the clips and the full length of rail with a damp cloth. These hold the handles you grip every day, so they pass dust from hand to hand until you clean them.
The broom, the duster, and the mop hanging from the clipsComb the broom bristles clear of trapped hair and thread into the bin, pull the fluff off the duster, then rinse the mop head and stand it to dry outside the closet before it goes back handle-down on its clip.
The vacuum body, canister, and beater barEmpty the canister into the bin and wipe it out, then wipe the vacuum body with a barely damp cloth while keeping the moisture off the vents and the switch, and clear the grit caked into the wheel treads. Cut the hair wound around the beater bar free with scissors, working along the length of the bar.
The mop bucketTip out any standing water, scrub the scum ring at the old waterline with neutral cleaner and a cloth, rinse it clean, then stand it upside down to drain fully so it never goes back closed and wet.
The crevice tools and brush heads in the clip-on bagTip the bag out, knock and wipe the dust from each attachment, then zip them back in and clip the bag to the handle so nothing goes missing before the next job.
The cords, as you wind themRun each cord through a folded cloth along its whole length while you coil it, lifting off the grime that builds where a cord drags on the floor.
The taped floor outline and the closet floor cornersVacuum the grit that drops off the vacuum wheels into the corners, then wash the floor with the floor-compatible cleaner and let it dry fully before the vacuum rolls back inside its outline.
What you clean with
Compact vacuum with attachmentsColor-coded microfiber cloth setNeutral pH multi-surface cleanerFloor-material-compatible cleanerPortable cleaning caddyScissors
Inspect as you clean
  • A sour smell or a slimy feel on the mop head, or a scum ring in the bucket, tells you something went away wet. Flag the start of mould.
  • A split or a soft cracked section in the vacuum's flexible hose, at the point where it flexes most, is worth flagging before it tears through.
  • A burning smell, a hot body, or a beater bar that will not spin freely after use points to a jammed belt or a labouring motor. Note it.
  • A water stain or a damp patch on the closet floor or the wall behind the mop names a leak or rising damp. Flag it.
  • Rust creeping up a metal broom clip or a bucket handle marks where water is sitting after every use. Note it.
The call that matters here
The vacuum you keep meaning to fix

It has a split hose or a dead brush roll, and it sits here because throwing out a machine that mostly works feels wasteful. Give it a repair date written on masking tape stuck to the body. If that date passes and you have not ordered the part, you have your answer: the repair was never going to happen, and the broken machine has been standing where the working one needs to stand. Recycle it and keep the one that runs.

Watch for
  • Fall, cut, or crushUnclipped handles slide down the wall and drop across the doorway or onto whoever opens the door.
  • Water and electricityA damp mop head or a wet bucket stored against a vacuum cord in a closed closet is the one combination to refuse; check the cord for cracked insulation near the plug while you have it out.
Leave behind

Handles hang from the rail with heads clear of the floor, the vacuum parks inside its outline, and nothing goes in wet.

Trigger: When the vacuum is switched off after a clean, it is wrapped, bagged, and back inside the tape before you sit down.

See Chapter 14, visual controls.

Cleaning Supply Zone

30-45 min, most of it in Sort, working through part-used bottles and deciding which ones actually get emptied

Holds the products you clean with, grouped so you carry the right handful to the right room.

Done looks like

Two or three caddies, each labeled on the end with the room it serves, every bottle wearing its original label, bleach products and ammonia products on separate shelves, and the whole zone up out of a small child's reach or behind a latch.

  • SortLine every bottle up on the hallway floor. Anything decanted into an unmarked spray bottle gets disposed of according to its own instructions, and the specialty cleaner bought for one stain years ago goes with it. Only identical products get poured together.
  • StraightenBuild a bathroom caddy and a kitchen caddy so you carry the whole job in one hand instead of walking back here twice. Gloves and cloths ride inside the caddy with the sprays, not in a drawer down the hall.
  • ShineWipe the crusted ring off the base of each bottle before it goes back, and lay a wipeable liner on the shelf so the next slow drip lands on plastic instead of soaking into the board.
  • SafetyChlorine bleach and ammonia products release a choking gas if they leak onto each other, so give them separate shelves. Keep every product in its original labeled container, and put this zone out of a small child's reach or behind a latch.
  • StandardizeEach caddy end reads the room it belongs to. The written rule is one line: a bottle without a legible original label is not stored, it is emptied.
  • SustainSetting the caddy back on the shelf is when you notice what ran dry, and the empty goes on the shopping list before the door shuts.
How to clean it

A shelf of cleaning products quietly weeps at the caps, so this zone is about catching the drip before it soaks in. Wipe the base of every bottle and give the shelf a wipeable liner, and the next slow leak lands on plastic.

Surface or areaMethod
The shelf boards, worked from the top shelf downTake every bottle and caddy off the board. Vacuum up any spilled powder or dust, then wipe the board with neutral cleaner and dry it before anything returns.
The wipeable shelf linerLift the liner off, rinse or wipe both faces, and lay it back flat so the next slow drip sits on the plastic rather than soaking into the wood.
The base and neck of each bottleWipe the crusted ring off the base of each bottle and clean the dried run down its neck before it goes back, and check the label is still readable while you have it in hand.
The caddies, inside and outEmpty each caddy, wipe the divider walls and the handle where product gathers, and dry it before the bottles go back grouped by the room the caddy serves.
The back wall behind the bottlesWipe the wall where splashes and aerosol overspray settle into a fine film, working from the top shelf line down to the board.
The inside of the closet doorWipe the inner door panel with neutral cleaner to lift the fine film that aerosol overspray lays down whenever a trigger is fired near the open door, then dry it off.
The closet floor beneath the shelvesVacuum any powder or board dust that fell to the floor, then wipe it with neutral cleaner, checking the corners for a puddle that would name a bottle leaking on the shelf above it.
What you clean with
Compact vacuum with attachmentsColor-coded microfiber cloth setNeutral pH multi-surface cleanerPortable cleaning caddyDisposable gloves
Inspect as you clean
  • A fresh ring of dried product or a wet patch under one bottle points to a cap or a trigger that is weeping. Flag which bottle it is.
  • An aerosol tin that has swollen at the base or ends, or one that hisses when you pick it up, is a pressure warning. Flag it.
  • A cracked trigger sprayer or a cap that no longer seats down flat is a leak waiting to happen. Note it.
  • A shelf liner that has gone soft, bleached, or corroded in one spot names a leak that has been dripping there for a while. Flag it.
  • A label gone illegible from a chemical run down the bottle means you can no longer tell what is inside. Flag it before it goes back on the shelf.
The call that matters here
Eleven bottles, each a third full

You buy a new one because you cannot see what is left in the old one, and the shelf now carries four glass cleaners, three floor sprays, and a scatter of part-used others. The rule that settles it: one open bottle per job. Pour identical products together, and for the rest, accept that finishing a half bottle down the drain costs you a little while keeping it costs you the shelf you need. You did not save money by keeping them. You paid twice and stored the evidence.

Watch for
  • Poison, choke, or strangleBleach stored beside an ammonia glass cleaner, and any product in an unlabeled bottle at a height a child can reach from the hallway floor.
  • Burn or fireAerosol tins and solvent-based sprays sitting against a closet wall that backs onto a boiler or a hot water pipe; move them to the coolest shelf.
Leave behind

One caddy per room, one open bottle per job, original labels only, and bleach and ammonia kept apart.

Trigger: When the caddy is set back on the shelf after a clean, whatever ran out is written on the list in the same movement.

See Chapter 21, the hazard hunt.

Paper and Household Backstock

30-45 min, most of it in Standardize, because writing honest minimum and maximum numbers is the work that lasts

Keeps a reserve of paper goods, bulbs, and batteries so you never make a late-night emergency trip.

Done looks like

Toilet roll in one clear bin with a card on the shelf edge reading "min 6, max 24". Bulbs in a labeled bin sorted by fitting. Batteries standing in their original packs in a shallow tray, sizes readable without lifting one out. Every bin label facing the door.

  • SortTake the lot out. Loose batteries you cannot vouch for, bulbs for fittings you replaced when you went over to LED, and the bulk paper towel pack too big to lift down alone all leave now.
  • StraightenToilet roll goes into the clear bin loose so you can see the level drop, bulbs stay in their boxes grouped by fitting, and batteries stand upright in a shallow tray by size so you read AAA from AA without picking one up. Heaviest packs at waist height, every label turned to the door.
  • ShineWipe the shelf and check the tray bottom for the white crust that means a cell has leaked. Press the underside of paper packs stored against a cold outside wall to feel for damp before you restock them.
  • SafetyLoose batteries whose terminals touch metal or each other can short and heat up, and a button cell dropped on a closet floor is exactly the size a toddler swallows. Keep cells in their packs, tape the terminals of any loose 9-volt, and store nothing at floor level here.
  • StandardizeThe minimum and maximum go on the shelf edge in front of each bin, not in your head. Below the minimum puts it on the shopping list; at the maximum you stop buying it regardless of the offer.
  • SustainLifting the last toilet roll out of the bin is the moment you are below the minimum, and it goes on the list before you leave the hallway.
How to clean it

This reserve sits untouched for weeks, which is exactly how a leaked battery or a damp pack goes unnoticed. Wiping the shelf is really a chance to catch both while the bins are out.

Surface or areaMethod
The shelf boards, top downClear each shelf, vacuum the dust and any cardboard flecks a pack has shed, then wipe the board with neutral cleaner and dry it before the bins go back.
The clear toilet-roll binLift the bin out and tip the paper dust and cardboard bits from the bottom corners, then wipe it inside and out so the 'min 6, max 24' card on the shelf edge stays readable through the side.
The bulb binEmpty the bin and wipe it out with a barely damp cloth, then set the bulbs back sorted by fitting, keeping the cloth away from the contacts and metal bases as you handle them.
The battery trayLift the shallow tray out and wipe the bottom, watching for a white crust, then dry it before the packs stand back in it with their sizes facing the door.
The paper packs and the wall behind themPress the underside of each pack stored against the cold outside wall to feel for damp, and wipe the wall itself where condensation beads before you restock against it.
The closet floorVacuum and wipe the floor, keeping the habit of storing nothing down here so a rolled-away cell or a dropped bulb shows up at once.
What you clean with
Compact vacuum with attachmentsColor-coded microfiber cloth setNeutral pH multi-surface cleanerPortable cleaning caddy
Inspect as you clean
  • A white or crusty powder in the battery tray, or on the terminals of a loose cell, is a leaked battery. Flag it as you find it.
  • A battery that has puffed up or split its wrapper in the pack. Note it before it goes back among the good ones.
  • A paper pack that feels soft or damp on its underside, and a wall behind it that reads cold and wet, point to condensation off the outside wall or a slow leak. Flag it.
  • A spare bulb with a darkened base or a loose rattle inside is already spent. Note it rather than reshelving it as a working spare.
The call that matters here
The offer that made you buy twelve

Bulk buying feels thrifty right up to the point where a warehouse pack blocks the shelf and you buy more anyway because you cannot see past it. Set your maximum as the number that fits inside the bin without stacking above the rim, then hold it: if a deal will not fit the bin, the deal is not for you. And date each pack with a marker as it arrives, pulling the oldest first. Undated bulk is where the saving quietly expires.

Watch for
  • Poison, choke, or strangleButton cells and loose AAAs rolling to the closet floor where a small child finds them at eye level.
  • Fall, cut, or crushA shrink-wrapped bulk pack of paper towel stored above head height slides free the moment you tug the corner of it.
Leave behind

Every bin carries a minimum and maximum on the shelf edge, stock is dated with a marker on arrival, and the oldest pack is pulled first.

Trigger: When you take the last roll or the last pack of bulbs out of a bin, the restock goes on the shopping list on that same trip to the closet.

See Chapter 14, visual controls.

Seasonal and Guest Zone

45-75 min, most of it in Sort, because every lid has to come off and half of these bins have not been opened in years

Parks the things you use a few weekends a year: guest bedding, decorations, and travel gear.

Done looks like

Three or four lidded bins on the upper shelf, each labeled with what is inside and the month it was last closed. Guest pillows sealed in a zip bag. A shelf map taped inside the door showing which bin sits where, and nothing overhead heavy enough to need both hands.

  • SortEvery lid comes off, on the floor, in daylight. Decorations for a tree you no longer put up, the guest pillow gone flat and yellow, and the bin marked "misc" whose contents you cannot name without looking all go.
  • StraightenOne bin per occasion, not one bin per overflow. Tape a shelf map inside the door so you can send someone else for the guest bedding without them unstacking the whole shelf to find it.
  • ShineGuest bedding gets washed before it goes into the bin rather than after it comes out, because a year sealed in a box is what your guests will smell. Wipe the dust off each lid while it is open, and put your nose to the bottom of every bin for the mustiness that means something went in damp last season.
  • SafetyA heavy lidded bin lifted down from above your head is how ankles and backs go, especially when the lid blocks your view of your own feet. Keep the heaviest bins at chest height, and keep a proper step stool in this closet so nobody stands on a hallway chair.
  • StandardizeEvery bin carries its contents and the date it was last closed. Photograph each bin packed and tape the picture to the lid, so repacking has a target instead of a guess.
  • SustainTaking the guest bed apart after a visit sends the bedding to the wash and then straight back into its own labeled bin, never onto the linen shelf.
How to clean it

A bin sealed for a year keeps whatever went in with it, damp and all, and that is what a guest smells. Wash bedding before it goes in, not after it comes out, and put your nose to the bottom of every bin while it is open.

Surface or areaMethod
Each bin lid, while the bin is openWipe the dust off the top of each lid before you set it aside, so none of it tips down into the open bin you are about to refill.
The upper shelf boardsBring the bins down to waist height first, then vacuum the shelf and wipe it with neutral cleaner, drying the board before the bins go back up.
The inside of each binPut your nose to the bottom of every bin for the mustiness that means something went in damp last season, then wipe the interior walls and floor and leave the lid off until it is genuinely dry.
The guest bedding and the sealed pillowsWash the guest bedding now, before it goes into the bin, and reseal the pillows in a fresh zip bag only once they are fully dry.
The travel gear and decoration boxesWipe the outside of the hard cases and the box lids, and air any soft bag that smells closed-in before it goes back sealed.
The inside of the door and the taped shelf mapWipe the door panel, then check the shelf map taped inside it is still legible and still matches which bin now sits where.
The back wall and the closet floorWipe the wall behind the top shelf, the highest surface in this zone, then vacuum the floor and the corners underneath.
What you clean with
Compact vacuum with attachmentsColor-coded microfiber cloth setNeutral pH multi-surface cleanerPortable cleaning caddy
Inspect as you clean
  • A musty or closed-in smell at the bottom of a bin tells you damp went in with the contents last season. Flag it before you reseal.
  • Black speckling or fuzzy spots on the guest bedding, on a pillow, or on the underside of a lid is mould taking hold. Flag it.
  • Droppings, gnaw marks, or shredded nesting material in stored fabric means a mouse has been in. Flag it, and note the gap it likely came through.
  • A bin lid that has cracked or warped and no longer seats flat is letting damp and pests straight in. Note it.
  • A water stain spreading on the top shelf board or the wall above it, being the highest point in the closet, can be the first sign of a roof or pipe leak. Flag it.
The call that matters here
The bin you have moved three times without opening

It has survived a house move and two closet reshuffles, and you still cannot say what is in it. That is the decision you have been avoiding, and the avoidance is itself the answer. Open it on the floor today. For each thing inside, name the specific occasion in the next twelve months when you will use it: a birthday, a holiday, a trip already booked. Anything you cannot name an occasion for does not get the lid back on, because a sealed bin of unknowns is not storage, it is a decision you agreed to keep paying for.

Watch for
  • FallReaching for a top-shelf bin while balanced on a hallway chair, with a lid you cannot see over hiding the step down.
  • Fall, cut, or crushA bin of decorations lifted down overhead shifts its weight halfway and lands on your foot or on whoever is holding the door.
Leave behind

Every bin is lidded, labeled with contents and the date it was last closed, photographed inside the lid, and stored clean and dry.

Trigger: When the guest bed is stripped after a visit, the bedding is washed and returned to its own bin before the closet door shuts.

See Chapter 25, the ideal-state photo.

Room 19 of 20

Stair Landing

3 zones

The stair landing belongs to nobody, which is exactly why things end up there. It is also the one route in the house where a small oversight, a book left on a tread or a bulb nobody replaced, turns into a real injury. Start with the stairs themselves, because once the treads are bare and both ends are lit, the console and the picture wall go quickly.

Where to startDo the Stair and Floor Path zone first, even though it looks like the least cluttered of the three. Clearing the treads and sorting the lighting sets the limit on what the console and the wall are allowed to hold.
Cleaning noteThe handrail is one of the most touched and least washed surfaces in the house, so wipe it end to end each week, underside included. Then vacuum the crevice where each tread meets its riser and where a runner tucks against the side stringer, because that trapped grit is what wears a runner smooth and slick.
The trapThe trap is the reasonable sentence "I'll take it up next time I go." It is how a landing console fills and how the bottom three steps become a shelf. One basket, emptied every night, is the only version of that promise that survives a busy week.

Landing Surface or Console

15-30 min, most of it in Sort, deciding where the deferred items actually live

A small surface beside the stairs that holds a lamp and a landing spot, without becoming the house's holding pen for things headed to another floor.

Done looks like

Three permanent things on the console top, say a lamp, a framed photo and one tray, with the tray holding nothing that was there yesterday. The lamp cord runs down the back leg in a clip, not across the walking side. One stair basket sits on the floor at the end nearest the top step, and it is empty.

  • SortTake everything off the console top and out of its drawer in one sweep. Sort into three: things that genuinely live on this floor, things headed up or down (straight into the stair basket now), and the drift a landing collects, loose screws, dry pens, keys to nothing, mail you already read.
  • StraightenPut the tray at the end of the console nearest the top step, where your hand lands as you turn, not at the decorative centre. Clip the lamp cord to the back leg so it never crosses the side people walk.
  • ShineWipe the console top, the front edge where hips and bags push past, and the top rim of the lamp shade, which catches the dust the stairwell draught keeps lifting. Then press one corner of the console: if it rocks, shim it now, because a wobble beside an open stairwell is how a lamp goes over the edge.
  • SafetyNothing on this surface may overhang the stair side, not by a centimetre. A book with its spine past the edge ends up on a tread, and a tread with a book on it is how people fall down stairs at night.
  • StandardizeWrite the count into the surface itself: three permanent items plus one tray on top, and one basket on the floor. Photograph the console in that state and tape the photo inside the console drawer, where the next person to open it will see it.
  • SustainWhoever switches off the landing light at night carries the stair basket up or down and brings it back empty. The light switch is the trigger, so the basket never sleeps loaded.
How to clean it

Work top to bottom: the lamp and its shade, the framed photo, the tray, the drawer, then the console top and the floor beneath it. The thing people miss is the front edge, where every hip and bag drags a film past all day.

Surface or areaMethod
The lamp shade and the bulb below itStart at the top. Dust the shade from the inside face outward with a dry microfiber cloth, then run the cloth around the top rim where the stairwell draught keeps dropping a grey collar. Let a warm bulb cool, then wipe the base with a barely damp cloth, never a wet one near the fitting.
The framed photo, glass and frameDust the top edge of the frame first so nothing falls onto clean glass. Lift the frame off, spray streak-free glass cleaner onto the cloth rather than onto the glass where it can run behind the frame, buff the pane, then wipe the frame body and its bottom lip before you set it back.
The trayLift the tray right off. Tip the day's grit and loose coins out, wipe the tray inside and out, and wipe the ring of bare surface it was hiding. Dry it fully before anything goes back on it.
The console drawer or lower shelf, and its runnersIf the drawer lifts free, slide it right out, brush the grit off the runners, and wipe the drawer inside and out and the empty cavity behind it before it goes back. If it is an open lower shelf instead, lift what sits there, wipe the shelf from the back edge forward, and set things down only once it has dried.
The console top, back edge to front edgeWork a neutral pH cleaner across the top with a microfiber cloth from the back edge forward. Give the front edge two passes, because that is the line hips and bags push past and it holds more grime than the middle ever shows.
The console legs and the lamp-cord clip on the back legWipe each leg down, then run the cloth along the back leg and lift dust off the cord where it sits in its clip, cleaning the cord itself rather than working around it.
The stair basket at the end nearest the top stepEmpty the basket, wipe it inside and out, and stand it aside while you clean the floor it was covering.
The floor under and around the consoleVacuum under the console last, into the back corners the lamplight never reaches, then damp mop the exposed floor with a cleaner matched to its material and let it dry before the basket and tray go back.
What you clean with
Color-coded microfiber cloth setNeutral pH multi-surface cleanerStreak-free glass and mirror cleanerCompact vacuum with attachmentsFloor-material-compatible cleanerPortable cleaning caddy
Inspect as you clean
  • As you lift the lamp, look along the whole run of the cord for a cracked, frayed, or pinched section, and check whether the clip is still gripping or hanging loose.
  • When you press the top to wipe the front edge, notice any rock or give in a leg or joint and flag it, rather than quietly working around a wobble beside an open stairwell.
  • A pale ring or a damp patch under the tray means a glass or a wet brolly has been living there. Note it so you can trace where the water keeps coming from.
  • Check the framed glass, and any vase or bowl on the top, for a hairline crack while it is in your hands.
  • Look at the shade lining and around the bulb for a brown scorch or heat mark that says the bulb is running too hot for the fitting.
The call that matters here
The going-upstairs-anyway pile

Every landing has one: the small heap of things that belong on the other floor, waiting for a trip that would have happened anyway. The honest question you have been avoiding is whether that heap gets a container or gets banned outright. Give it a container, exactly one basket, and then enforce the part that hurts, which is that it empties nightly whether it is full or not. Here is the rule that settles the rest. If an item rides that basket three nights running without landing anywhere, it is not waiting for a trip, it has no home on the destination floor. Stop carrying it back and forth, and either give it a home tonight or route it out of the house.

Watch for
  • FallAnything set near the stair-side edge of the console, a book, a phone, a set of keys, can be caught by a passing sleeve and knocked onto the top tread, where it waits in the dark.
  • Fall, cut, or crushA glass vase or ceramic bowl on a console beside an open stairwell falls further than furniture height. It breaks across treads that people walk in socks.
Leave behind

Three permanent items plus one tray on top, nothing overhanging the stair side, and one stair basket that sleeps empty.

Trigger: When you switch off the landing light at night, the basket goes with you.

Wall and Display Zone

30-45 min, most of it in Safety, since press-testing and rehanging hardware takes far longer than dusting glass

The frames, mirror and any sconces on the stairway wall, hung so they stay put and never crowd the shoulder of someone climbing with their arms full.

Done looks like

Every frame level and hung from two points, each one the same vertical distance above the tread nose below it, so the gallery climbs on the stair's diagonal rather than fighting it. No sawtooth hanger and no single nail anywhere on the flight. Nothing projects more than a hand's width from the wall at shoulder height, and the mirror at the turn sits flat with no play when you push up on it.

  • SortTake down anything hanging from a single nail or a sawtooth hanger, any frame with cracked glass, and the pictures you honestly last looked at years ago. You watch your feet on stairs, not the wall, so this wall only earns what you stop on the landing to look at.
  • StraightenSet the spacing by measuring from each frame's centre down to the nose of the tread beneath it and holding that number constant up the flight. Equal gaps between frames, and one consistent bottom edge across the flat landing section.
  • ShineDust the top edge of each frame first, then the glass, then wipe the wall along the turn where shoulders, bags and laundry baskets rub a grey line. As you take your hand off each frame, look behind it at the wire for kinks and at the hook for a bent lip.
  • SafetyStair walls take constant vibration, and single nails and sawtooth hangers work loose in them. Everything here hangs from two points on hardware rated well above the item's weight, and the mirror goes into a stud or a proper wall anchor. Never straighten a high frame while standing on a tread. Do it from the flat landing or a properly footed ladder.
  • StandardizeOne hanging system across the whole wall, two hooks and a wire, so a replacement frame never quietly reintroduces a nail. Put a dated sticker on the back of the lowest frame recording the last time you press-tested the flight.
  • SustainHandrail day is also frame day. As you work up the rail with your cloth, push up and out on the two frames within arm's reach. Over a season the whole wall gets checked without ever becoming a job you had to plan.
How to clean it

Clean down the diagonal, top frame to bottom, glass then frame then the wall behind. The line most people walk past is the grey smear at shoulder height on the turn, where bags and baskets rub the wall.

Surface or areaMethod
The top edge of each frameBegin at the top of the flight. Dust the top edge of every frame with a dry cloth before you touch any glass, working down the stair's diagonal so grit never lands on a frame you have already finished.
The frame glassSpray streak-free glass and mirror cleaner onto the cloth, never onto the glass where it runs down behind the frame, and buff each pane until it is clear at lamplight.
The frame body and mouldingWipe the moulding and the bottom lip of each frame, where dust packs into the profile and never brushes off on its own.
The mirror at the turnClean the mirror glass corner to corner with the glass cleaner on a cloth, then wipe its frame and the wall in the hand's width all around it.
Any sconces or wall lights on the flightLet the bulbs cool, dust the fitting and shade with a dry cloth, and lift the cobwebs off the top of each arm with the vacuum's soft brush attachment. Keep every cloth dry near the fitting.
The wall along the turnWipe the wall itself with a barely damp cloth and a little neutral pH cleaner, giving the grey line at shoulder height an extra pass, since that is where bags, shoulders, and laundry baskets rub as people round the turn.
Behind each frame, the wire and hookAs you take your hand off each frame, tilt it slightly and lift the dust off the wire and the hook behind it.
The skirting at the foot of the wall and the landing floorFinish low: vacuum the crevice along the skirting, wipe the board, then damp mop the landing floor beneath with a cleaner matched to its material.
What you clean with
Streak-free glass and mirror cleanerColor-coded microfiber cloth setCompact vacuum with attachmentsNeutral pH multi-surface cleanerFloor-material-compatible cleanerPortable cleaning caddy
Inspect as you clean
  • As your hand leaves each frame, look at the wire for kinks or rust and at the hook for a bent or opening lip, and flag any frame you find hanging from a single nail or a sawtooth hanger.
  • Push gently up under the mirror as you wipe it and note any play or movement against the wall.
  • Watch the wall along the turn for a soft, bulging, or stained patch that can mean damp tracking down behind the plaster.
  • On a sconce, notice a warm or discoloured fitting, a scorched shade, or a bulb that flickers as you dust near it.
  • Flag any frame that has crept out of line or drooped since last time, since the constant vibration of a stair wall is what quietly works hardware loose.
The call that matters here
The heavy mirror you would rather not touch

There is one piece on this wall you already suspect is too heavy for what is holding it, usually the mirror at the turn or the big frame over the middle of the flight. You have left it because taking it down means finding a stud, patching a hole and repainting a stairwell you would need a ladder for. Make this call with your hands, not your eye. Grip it and push up and out. If it lifts off the wall with any play in the hook, it is being held by friction and gravity on a surface that shakes every time someone runs down. Rehang it today on two rated anchors, or take it down and leave the wall bare until the right hardware arrives. A bare stairway wall is not a failure. It is safer than heavy glass hanging over steps.

Watch for
  • Fall, cut, or crushA framed picture coming off a stairway wall lands on treads and shatters across several steps at once, in the one place you cannot sweep from a standing position.
  • FallReaching sideways to level a frame while standing on a tread puts your weight outside your feet on the least forgiving surface in the house.
Leave behind

Two hanging points and rated hardware on every item, nothing projecting more than a hand's width at shoulder height, and the mirror fixed to a stud or a proper wall anchor.

Trigger: When you wipe the handrail on your weekly pass, press-test the two frames closest to your hand.

Stair and Floor Path

30-45 min, most of it in Safety, walking the flight tread by tread and fixing what you find

The treads, runner, handrail and lighting that carry every person in the house between floors, several times a day, often with their arms full.

Done looks like

Every tread bare from nose to riser, including the bottom three steps that usually stage things. The runner lies flat with no lifted corner at the turn. The handrail runs unbroken and unobstructed from the top newel post to the bottom, with no bag, coat or dog lead hung on either post. Both switches work, so the top step and the bottom step are lit before you take them.

  • SortNothing lives on stairs. Clear the folded laundry, the shoes, the box that has sat on step four since the weekend, all of it in one trip, and put each thing where it belongs on its own floor rather than restaging it on the landing above.
  • StraightenThe one thing that must stay reachable here is the handrail, so strip both newel posts of coats, bags and leads. If a stair gate is fitted, set the top gate to swing away from the flight and never out over it.
  • ShineVacuum the tread noses, the crevice where each tread meets its riser, and along the stringer where a runner is tucked. That trapped grit is what polishes a runner slick under a stocking foot. Wipe the handrail its full length, including the underside your fingers wrap around.
  • SafetyWalk the flight slowly and test as you go: press each tread nose for movement, tug each runner edge, and grip the rail hard at top, middle and bottom to find a loose bracket. Then stand at the bottom in the dark and look at the top step, and stand at the top and look at the bottom. A burnt-out bulb over a top step is the single most dangerous thing in this room. “Find it, and fix it now.”
  • StandardizeThe standard is one sentence anyone in the house can check: bare treads, full width, lit at both ends, rail clear. Photograph the empty flight from the bottom and put that picture where the staging happens, which is almost always beside the bottom step.
  • SustainThe last person up at night takes the flight with one hand on the rail and carries whatever is sitting on a tread. If their hands are too full to hold the rail, that is the signal to make two trips.
How to clean it

Top to bottom and rail first: handrail, posts and balusters, then the treads, the risers, the runner and its tucked edges, and the landing floor last. The step almost everyone skips is the crevice where each tread meets its riser, and that grit is what turns a runner slick.

Surface or areaMethod
The handrail, full length including the undersideStart with the rail. Wipe it the whole length from the top newel to the bottom, and do not skip the underside your fingers wrap around, which carries the oil off every hand that has ever used it.
Both newel postsWipe the top and bottom newel posts, including the flat cap on top where dust settles and set-down keys leave a film.
The balusters or spindlesRun a cloth or the vacuum's brush down each baluster, catching the dust that clings to the inside faces you never see head on.
The tread noses, the tread-to-riser crevice, and the riser facesVacuum along every tread nose and right into the crevice where each tread meets its riser, since that trapped grit is exactly what polishes a runner slick under a stocking foot. Then wipe the riser faces below with a barely damp cloth, where toe scuffs and shoe marks collect the way nothing on the flat does.
The runner and its edges tucked at the stringerVacuum the runner down the middle, then along both edges where it tucks against the stringer. Spot clean any mark with a cloth only barely damp, so the backing never soaks through.
The bare tread either side of the runnerWipe the bare wood or painted strip on each side of the runner with a cleaner matched to the material, drying as you go so no tread is left damp underfoot.
The bottom three stepsClear and clean the bottom three steps that usually end up staging things headed upstairs, so they read as stairs and not as shelves.
The landing floor, the switches, and the light fittingsFinish on the flat. Vacuum and mop the landing floor, and wipe both light switches and the plate around them where every hand pushes, keeping the cloth just damp and off the switch itself.
What you clean with
Compact vacuum with attachmentsColor-coded microfiber cloth setFloor-material-compatible cleanerNeutral pH multi-surface cleanerPortable cleaning caddy
Inspect as you clean
  • As you vacuum each tread nose, notice any tread that gives or flexes underfoot, and flag it.
  • Note any runner corner that has lifted as you clean the edges, and any worn strip on a nose where the pile has gone thin.
  • While you wipe the rail's length, notice any bracket that has worked loose or any section that turns slightly in its fixing under the cloth.
  • While you clean the treads under each fitting, flag a bulb over the top or bottom step that is dim, flickering, or dead, because that is the most dangerous fault this room can hold.
  • Watch the stringer and skirting for a cracked or springy board, and the switch plates for warmth or discolouring as you wipe them.
The call that matters here
The runner that has already lifted once

If your stairs have a runner and one corner has lifted or one rod has worked loose, you have almost certainly pressed it back down and carried on. That is the decision you keep deferring, and it is the wrong shape, because a runner that has lifted once has already told you what it will do again. Choose today between two honest options and refuse the third. Either refix it properly along its whole length, with rods or full-width tape on every tread rather than a patch on the one failed corner, or take it up entirely and live with bare treads. Bare wood you know is slippery beats a runner you have quietly stopped trusting, because you adjust your step for the first and not the second. The one thing you may not do is press it down again and wait.

Watch for
  • FallA lifted runner corner, a loose handrail bracket, or a dead bulb over the top step. Each one is a fall on the hardest surface in the house, and they tend to arrive together, at night.
  • Fall, cut, or crushCarrying a laundry basket or a box you cannot see over means going down blind with no free hand for the rail. Anything parked on the bottom three steps is invisible to that person.
  • Water and electricityChanging the bulb over the stairwell means standing high on a flight with a live fitting overhead. Switch the light off at the wall, let a hot bulb cool, and work from the landing on a properly footed ladder rather than balancing on a tread.
Leave behind

Bare treads at full width, runner flat and fixed the whole way, handrail clear from newel to newel, and working light at both ends.

Trigger: When you head up to bed, you go with a hand on the rail and nothing left standing on a tread behind you.

Room 20 of 20

Patio or Deck

6 zones

A patio lives outdoors, which puts everything on it under a clock the indoor rooms never feel: sun makes plastic brittle, damp gets into fabric and end grain, and a season of putting things off arrives as a replacement cost. Start with the surface you walk on and the rail you lean on, because those are the only things out here that can put someone in hospital. Everything after that, the cushions and the grill and the pots, is about making the space usable within one trip through the back door.

Where to startDo the Surface, Rail, and Safety Zone first, on a dry morning, before you touch a single cushion. If the boards and the railing are not sound, everything you arrange on top of them is decoration.
Cleaning noteOut here, cleaning is how you inspect. You will never spot a popped fastener, a soft board end, or a spider web in a burner tube while sitting in a chair. You spot them with a brush in your hand and your face close to the thing.
The trapThe trap is storing things outdoors that cannot live outdoors. A cushion left out overnight in dew, a cardboard box inside the deck box, a bag of charcoal resting on the ground: each is fine for a week and ruined by autumn. If it cannot take a night of rain, it belongs behind a door.

Outdoor Seating Zone

45-75 min, most of it in Sort, deciding about cushions rather than moving chairs

Seats people can drop into without checking them first, with the cushions kept dry.

Done looks like

Four chairs you can sit in without testing, set close enough to talk across, every cushion in the deck box, and no cover crumpled on the ground behind a planter.

  • SortSit in every chair. The one that wobbles, the one with the cracked resin arm, and the cushion you would not offer a guest are decisions, not furniture. Count who actually sits out here on an ordinary evening and keep that many chairs plus two.
  • StraightenChairs face each other close enough to talk without raising your voice, and no chair sits in the swing of the back door. Cushions live in the deck box, not stacked on a seat under a towel that stays wet underneath.
  • ShineHose the frames and scrub the webbing where grit packs into the joints, then stand each chair on its side so water runs out of the tubing instead of sitting inside it all summer. Check the underside of every cushion for black speckling while it dries.
  • SafetyResin and plastic chairs go brittle from years of sun long before they look it. Flex each arm and press the seat pan hard with your palm; a chair that creaks or shows fine white cracks around a leg joint leaves today, not after someone drops through it onto the boards.
  • StandardizeOne cushion per seat and none spare, so a gap in the box tells you a cushion is still out on a chair. A photo of the chairs in their usual evening arrangement, taped inside the deck box lid, is what the last person out puts them back to.
